- Both toggles are logged to the active review cycle's change log the same way a status change is (`logReviewChange()`), just as plain text ("Marked as Not Applicable" / "Marked as Applicable again") rather than a structured `statusChange`/`tagChange`/`dateChange` — `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` already falls back to plain text for any entry without one of those three shapes, so nothing there needed to change.

`milestonesForCategory()` (seeding a new item's checklist from its category template), `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()` (adding milestones on template sync), and `openItemModal()`'s existing-item seeding path all construct fresh milestone objects with `notApplicable: false` explicitly — every place in this file that builds a milestone object needed this field added, not just the toggle functions themselves.

### Modals

Eight modals share the `.modal-bg` pattern (add `.open` class to show, remove to hide): `wsModalBg` (create/edit a workstream, with a delete button that moves that workstream's items to Unassigned rather than deleting them — see "Deletion tombstones and undo" below), `itemModalBg` (create/edit a scope item, `.modal-wide` at 520px to fit the milestones editor — see "Milestone checklist" above), `categoryModalBg` (create/edit an Admin-mode category and its milestone template — see "Categories (Admin mode)" below), `confirmModalBg` (generic confirm-then-run-a-callback, used by every delete, by restore-from-backup, and by cancelling a review cycle — see "Deletion tombstones and undo" below), `importModalBg` (merge vs. replace choice after picking a file), `backupsModalBg` (list + restore local daily snapshots), `fileSyncModalBg` (onboarding/reconnect for the linked-file feature), and `backupFolderModalBg` (the second step of that same launch-time onboarding sequence — see "Launch-time onboarding" under "Daily backups to a linked folder" below). Several more modals share the identical pattern beyond this original eight (the L1 Plans connect/pick modals, Minutes, Sync Conflicts, Theme, Role, Help, About, Role Passwords, …) — all close on Escape and on a background click via `wireModalBackdropClose()` (see just below) — the one exception is `scopeAssignModalBg`, deliberately excluded from both, since it has no dismiss path at all (see its own section above).

**Backdrop-click-to-close needs the mousedown to have started on the backdrop too, not just the click — a user-reported bug: dragging the mouse to select a few letters inside a modal's own input (e.g. the item modal's own Name field) could silently close the whole modal mid-selection.** Root cause is a plain, easy-to-miss browser-spec quirk: when a `mousedown` and the `mouseup` that follows it land on two *different* elements, the browser still synthesizes a `click` event — but fires it on the nearest common ancestor of the two, not on wherever the mouseup itself happened to land. A text-selection drag that starts inside a modal's own input and merely ends a pixel or two outside that input's own box (an ordinary side effect of dragging near a field's edge, not a deliberate "click the backdrop" gesture) has the modal's own full-bleed backdrop as that common ancestor once the drag exits the small, centered `.modal` card — so the original `addEventListener('click', e => { if (e.target.id === '...') close(); })` pattern every modal used closed on exactly that drag, since the synthesized click's own `e.target` genuinely *is* the backdrop in that case, indistinguishable from a real backdrop click by looking at the click event alone.

`wireModalBackdropClose(bg, closeFn)` fixes this the standard way any "click outside to close" implementation needs to be safe: it also tracks whether the *mousedown* itself started on the backdrop element specifically (`bg.addEventListener('mousedown', e => { downOnBackdrop = e.target === bg; })`), and only calls `closeFn()` when both the mousedown and the click agree the target was the backdrop. A real backdrop click always satisfies both; an in-progress text-selection drag only ever satisfies the click half, and no longer closes anything. Every modal that used to wire its own `addEventListener('click', ...)` inline now calls this one shared helper instead — a single definition of "what counts as a real backdrop click," not N slightly-differently-buggy copies of the same naive check. Verified live with real CDP-dispatched mouse events (press inside an input, move outside the modal card, release) confirming the modal stays open, and a genuine press-and-release-both-on-the-backdrop sequence confirming it still closes correctly.

**Testing caveat, the same "hand-rolled fake EventTarget, not the harness's own no-op DOM mock" technique `armPickerCommit()`'s own tests already use** (see "Every inline date field renders as a 'pill'" above) — the JXA harness stubs every fake element's `addEventListener`/`removeEventListener` as plain no-ops, so `wireModalBackdropClose()`'s own tests (`tests/cases/views.test.js`, right next to `armPickerCommit()`'s own) pass a small `fakeMouseTarget()` object implementing just enough of the real contract (`addEventListener`/`removeEventListener` storing/clearing a handler per event type, plus a `fire(type, target)` helper) for the actual mousedown-then-click logic to run for real against it.

### Data integrity

`normalizeData()` (called from `load()`, from every merge/import/file-sync entry point, and, for tests, from the `resetState()` glue) is the single source of truth for schema defaults: workstream `id`/`color`/`order` backfilled and the array re-sorted by `order`; an item's `id`/`name`/`owner`/`status`/`updatedAt`/`itStatus`/`businessStatus`/`budgetStatus` backfilled, `order` backfilled from current array position per workstream (a separate pass, after `workstreamId` reassignment below so it's never keyed by a stale one — see "Manual item order and drag-and-drop reordering"), an orphaned/stale `workstreamId` (pointing at a workstream that no longer exists) reset to `null` — Unassigned, same as an item deliberately created that way (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below) — rather than an arbitrary real workstream, so a broken reference surfaces for triage instead of silently landing wherever `workstreams[0]` happens to be; an orphaned/missing `categoryId` reassigned to the first category, a missing `startDate` defaulted from `dueDate`, a non-string `actualDate` defaulted to `null`, a missing `milestones` defaulted to `[]`, and each milestone's own `id`/`name`/`status`/`actualDate`/`notApplicable` backfilled (`false` for `notApplicable` — see "Not Applicable milestones" below) and a missing `dueDate` defaulted from its *parent item's* `dueDate` — then, once milestones are backfilled, any item with `milestones.length > 0` has its `status` recomputed via `computedStatusFromMilestones()` regardless of whatever value was saved (see "Item status roll-up"). A stale `filterWorkstreamId` (the one selector shared by Planning/Review/Dashboard — see "The shared workstream selector" above) is cleared if it no longer resolves, a stale/invalid `mode` falls back to `'planning'` (checked against the `MODES` array), and — since Review mode requires a specific workstream — `mode` additionally falls back to `'planning'` if it's `'review'` with no `filterWorkstreamId` selected. `deletedWorkstreamIds`/`deletedItemIds` are coerced to arrays and filtered to well-formed `{id, ...}` entries; `categories` and `reviewCycles` get the same array-coercion, and every category's own `id`/`name`/`milestones`/`order` is backfilled the same way a workstream's is. **If `categories` ends up empty** (a pre-categories save, or every category somehow got deleted), `normalizeData()` reseeds the whole out-of-the-box set — `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES` (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below for where that constant comes from) — this is also how a pre-categories item picks up a valid `categoryId` on first load after the upgrade, since the orphaned-`categoryId` backfill runs after categories are guaranteed non-empty. Review cycles get `id`/`startedAt`/`completedAt`/`cancelledAt`/`confirmations`/`milestoneConfirmations` backfilled but are otherwise left alone — there's no cross-referential repair needed since a cycle referencing a since-deleted workstream is simply unreachable through the shared sidebar (which only lists live workstreams), not actively cleaned up; see "Review mode" below for why that's the deliberate choice rather than an oversight.

It also runs a one-time schema migration: a pre-nested-milestones save could have a top-level item with `type: 'milestone'` (milestones used to be their own top-level item, siblings of scope items, before this became a nested checklist). Rather than silently dropping these on first load under the new schema, `normalizeData()` folds each one into its own new single-milestone item (`startDate`/`dueDate` both set to the old milestone's `dueDate`, `milestones: [{ ...that one milestone }]`) and strips the legacy rows out of `items`. `type` itself is `delete`d off every item as part of this pass, since it no longer means anything under the new schema.

**Every place that assigns `workstreams`/`items` from an external source calls `normalizeData()`, not just `load()`** — the current call sites are `load()`, both branches of `applyImport()`, `restoreBackup()`, `linkFolder()`'s merge-on-first-link branch, `reconnectFolder()`, and `initFileSync()`. If you add another data-loading path (e.g. a new sync mechanism), route it through `normalizeData()` too, or you'll reintroduce the exact bug class this fixed — a render function assuming a field exists that an older/hand-edited/externally-sourced save doesn't have.

`normalizeData()` is also the security boundary for ids: every workstream/item/milestone id is interpolated unescaped into `onclick` HTML attributes at render time (`esc()` only covers *content* fields — name, owner — never `id`). That's fine for locally-created data since `genId()` only ever produces plain alphanumeric strings, but `mergeData()` and `applyImport('replace')` trust ids from an external `.json` file as-is. `isSafeId(id)` (`/^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+$/`) plus the per-entity checks in `normalizeData()`'s three loops (workstreams, items, milestones) regenerate anything that couldn't have come from `genId()`, closing that off at the one place every external-data path already routes through — see `tests/cases/sync.test.js`'s "regenerates a workstream/item/milestone id" tests.

## Data durability

Pulse now matches PromptLab/mytasks' full data-durability feature set: JSON export/import, rolling local backups, deletion tombstones with toast undo, and optional File System Access API file sync. The "Data" dropdown in the topbar (`toggleDataMenu()`/`closeDataMenu()`, closed by the document-level click listener when a click lands outside `#dataDropdown`) is the entry point for Export, Import, and Backups. File sync deliberately has no entry there — see "File System Access API sync" below for why it lives entirely in the topbar's `#fileSyncIndicator` instead.

### Toast with undo

`showToast(msg, isError, undoAction)` — the third argument is new. When present, the toast shows an "Undo" button (`#toastUndoBtn`) and stays up 6s instead of 2s; `triggerToastUndo()` (wired to the button's `onclick`) invokes it and dismisses the toast immediately. Only one undo action is live at a time (`toastUndoAction`, a single module-level slot) — showing any other toast overwrites it, matching the single visible toast.

### Confirm modal — replaces native `confirm()`

`openConfirmModal({title, body, action, confirmLabel})` / `confirmModalAction()` / `closeConfirmModal()` replace the plain `window.confirm()` dialogs earlier versions used for delete. `modalTarget = {action}` holds the pending callback; clicking the modal's action button (`#confirmModalActionBtn`) runs it and closes the modal. This exists specifically so confirmations can describe *what* will happen (e.g. cascade counts) and hand off into the undo-toast flow below, which a same-tick `confirm()` return value can't do.

**`confirmLabel` defaults to `"Delete"`** — this one shared modal backs every confirm-then-act flow in the app, not just deletes (cancelling a review cycle, restoring a backup, applying a category's milestone-template sync to its items), and for a while those non-delete callers didn't override it, so the button read "Delete" for an action that wasn't a delete at all — confusing enough that a user reported a category-sync as "not working" when they'd likely declined it via a button that looked like it would destroy something. Every caller whose action isn't literally a delete now passes an explicit `confirmLabel` (`'Cancel Review'`, `'Restore'`, `'Apply Changes'`) — `tests/cases/*.test.js` assert the label alongside each of those flows specifically to keep this from silently regressing back to "Delete" if a new confirm-gated action gets added without one.

### Deletion tombstones and undo

`deletedWorkstreamIds`/`deletedItemIds` (each `{id, deletedAt}[]`) record "this id used to exist and was intentionally removed" — without them, an id absent from `workstreams`/`items` is indistinguishable from an id a merge has simply never seen, and a stale copy from an old linked file or import could silently resurrect something the user deleted on purpose. `tombstone(list, id)`/`untombstone(list, id)` add/remove an entry.

`deleteItem(id)` opens the confirm modal; its `action` splices the item out, tombstones it, then calls `showToast('Item deleted', false, undoFn)` where `undoFn` untombstones it and splices it back to its original index. The undo callback must clear the tombstone(s) it set, or a delete-then-undo would leave the item permanently un-mergeable from any linked file/import that still has it.

**`deleteWorkstreamFromModal()` no longer cascades to that workstream's own items — an explicit user request** ("if i remove a workstream that has scope items, move them to unassigned"), reversing an earlier design that deleted them right along with the workstream (tombstoned, undoable only as one bundled action). Losing real work — milestones, tags, dates, review history — just because the workstream containing it was removed was the actual complaint, not the workstream deletion itself. Its confirm-modal `action` now only removes the workstream itself, and separately walks `items` setting `workstreamId = null` (plus a fresh `updatedAt`, so the change is real for merge purposes — see "Merge and last-write-wins" below) on every item that belonged to it, collecting their ids into `movedItemIds` as it goes; nothing about a moved item is otherwise touched — it keeps its own category, milestones, tags, dates. This lands a moved item in exactly the same "Unassigned" bucket a stale/orphaned `workstreamId` already gets from `normalizeData()` (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket..." below) — `unassignedItemsSorted()`/`unassignedSectionHtml()` are keyed purely on `workstreamId == null`, with no assumption that everything landing there came through the quick-add flow's Pending category, so a moved item with a real, already-assigned category shows up there normally: fully editable (its Category select isn't locked — only a genuinely Pending item's is, see "A still-Pending item's category can never be changed..." above), Actual date and Dependency still visible, and reachable to re-assign to a real workstream again at any time through the ordinary item modal. The confirm dialog's own body text changed to match ("N items will be moved to Unassigned") — no longer describing a deletion that no longer happens.

The undo callback restores the workstream (spliced back to its original index) and then reassigns *only* the items this specific delete moved, by id (`movedItemIds`) — not a whole-`items`-array snapshot the way the old cascade-delete version used, and deliberately guarded on `it.workstreamId === null` before restoring: if one of the moved items was independently reassigned to a different real workstream in the few seconds before Undo was clicked, undo must not clobber that later, unrelated edit back to the deleted workstream. There's nothing left to tombstone/untombstone on the item side at all any more — only the workstream id itself still goes through `tombstone(deletedWorkstreamIds, wsId)`/`untombstone(...)`, since items are never removed from `items` in the first place.

**`deleteItem(id)` allows deleting a still-Pending (Unassigned) item, same as any other** — an explicit user request. An earlier version of this function refused outright (a toast, the confirm modal never even opening) for a Pending-category item, on the reasoning that one still waiting on real triage (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below) shouldn't be able to just disappear before that triage happens; a later, separate user request reversed that, so a not-yet-triaged item now goes through the exact same confirm-modal-and-undo-toast flow as everything else. Both places `deleteItem()` is reachable from — the row's own trash icon (`itemRowHtml()`'s `.item-actions`) and the full edit modal's Delete button (`deleteItemFromModal()`) — funnel through this one function, so this applies to both without a second check anywhere else.

### Merge and last-write-wins

`mergeData(data, opts)` is the additive merge used by every "read data from outside `localStorage`" path (`linkFolder()`, `reconnectFolder()`, `initFileSync()`, `doFileSyncWrite()`'s cross-tab-conflict branch, `pollFileSync()`, and `applyImport('merge')`). It merges incoming tombstones first (union by id, keeping the later `deletedAt`, covering `deletedWorkstreamIds`/`deletedItemIds`/`deletedMilestoneIds`/`deletedActionLogIds`/`deletedDecisionLogIds`), then workstreams *and* categories (workstreams merge field-by-field into an already-known id via `mergeWorkstreamFields()` — see below — categories stay simple additive-by-id-or-name, merged silently either way, not counted in the return value, mirroring how mytasks' projects merge), then review cycles (field-by-field into an already-known id via `mergeReviewCycle()` — see below — additive-by-id for a genuinely new one), then items. Returns `{added, updated, conflicts, changed}` — `added`/`updated` only ever count items (unchanged from before); `conflicts` is covered under "Conflict detection" below; `changed` is the umbrella "did this merge actually mutate anything at all" flag `doFileSyncWrite()`/`pollFileSync()` gate their own `save()`/`render()` on (see their own paragraph further down) — `added > 0 || updated > 0` alone isn't enough any more, since a merge can now be entirely about review-cycle confirmations or a workstream's `actionLog`/`decisionLog`, with zero item changes.

**Review cycles and workstreams used to merge as one opaque, whole unit each — `mergeData()` simply skipped an incoming one once its id was already known locally, silently discarding everything on it.** This was a real, user-reported gap, surfaced by a direct question about what happens when several people run a review in parallel against the same file synced through something like SharePoint/OneDrive: three reviewers confirming different milestones in the same active cycle, or attaching meeting-minutes action items to the same workstream around the same time, would each write their own full local copy of the cycle/workstream back to the file — and since the *other* side's id was already known, its confirmations/`changeLog`/`actionLog`/`decisionLog` entries were never merged in at all, just silently overwritten by whichever write reached the file last. Items/milestones never had this problem (see the per-field milestone merge just below, which predates this) — review cycles and workstreams now get the identical treatment, one level up.

**`mergeKeyedRecords(existingList, incomingList, keyField, tombstones, respectTombstones)`** is the shared primitive both new merges are built on — the same "newer `updatedAt` wins, an id not yet seen locally gets appended" idiom `mergeMilestonesArray()` already used for an item's own milestones, generalized to any flat array of `{[keyField]: id, updatedAt, ...}` records: a review cycle's own `confirmations`/`milestoneConfirmations`, or a workstream's `actionLog`/`decisionLog`. `tombstones`/`respectTombstones` are omitted for the two confirmation arrays — a confirmation record is never actually removed (see "Confirmation is milestone-level" above), only flipped, so there's nothing to tombstone there — but passed for `actionLog`/`decisionLog`, which *can* be genuinely deleted (see below).

**It also proactively sweeps a *locally-existing* record whose own tombstone has just merged in, not only guards against re-adding one that's incoming — a user-reported bug.** The function used to be purely additive: its one loop only ever looks at `incomingList`, adding or updating whatever's actually present there — a record already sitting in `existingList` that's simply *absent* from `incomingList` (the ordinary shape of "someone else already deleted it" — their own `actionLog`/`decisionLog` no longer lists it at all) was never even considered, tombstone or not. Concretely: device A deletes a review cycle's meeting minutes (`removeMinutes()` → `removeActionLogForCycle()`/`removeDecisionLogForCycle()`, tombstoning and shrinking its own `actionLog`/`decisionLog`, correctly written out) — but a device B that had already synced in its own copy of that action item *before* the deletion ever reached it never dropped that copy, since nothing in the incoming (now-shorter) array ever told this function to remove anything. Device B's own next write — triggered by *any* unrelated edit at all, not just a related one — would then republish its still-stale, longer array right back into the shared file, silently resurrecting a deletion that had already correctly landed everywhere else. Verified directly: two real Chromium pages (Playwright) sharing one fake folder — device 2 polls in its own copy of a freshly-created action item/decision, device 1 deletes the source minutes and writes, device 2 polls again (its own in-memory `actionLog`/`decisionLog` correctly empty out), then device 2 makes a wholly unrelated edit (a new scope item) and its own write is confirmed to leave the shared file's `actionLog`/`decisionLog` empty rather than resurrecting them.

Fixed by adding a sweep pass over `merged` (a copy of `existingList`) *before* the existing incoming-processing loop runs: for each record with a tombstone whose `deletedAt` is at least as new as that record's own `updatedAt`, drop it — the exact same deletedAt-vs-updatedAt comparison the incoming loop already used, just applied up front to what's already local rather than only gating what's arriving. Running it first, not after, is what lets the untouched incoming loop still correctly *resurrect* a record on its own if the incoming copy turns out to be a genuine edit made after the deletion (newer than the tombstone) — swept out here, it simply falls into that loop's existing "not found locally" branch as if this were the first time this device ever saw it, tombstone comparison and all. Gated on `tombstones && respectTombstones`, mirroring the incoming loop's own gate exactly: the two confirmation arrays (no `tombstones` passed at all) are untouched, and `applyImport('merge')`'s own `respectTombstones:false` opt-out (see below) correctly leaves a local copy alone here too, not just on the incoming side.

**`mergeReviewCycle(existing, incoming)`** merges each piece of a cycle independently: `confirmations`/`milestoneConfirmations` via `mergeKeyedRecords()`; `changeLog` as a plain union by its own `id` (append-only and never mutated after creation, so no `updatedAt` comparison needed — just keep every entry either side has, re-sorted by `changedAt` so the merged history still reads chronologically regardless of which side logged which entry); `minutes` as one whole nested object, newer `updatedAt` wins (the same coarser granularity an item's own *scalar* fields get, not milestones' finer per-field treatment — minutes are normally written by one person after a meeting, so a real per-field merge of its own `actionItems` would mean a second `mergeMilestonesArray()`-shaped function for comparatively little real-world benefit); `completedAt`/`cancelledAt` (plus the count snapshots that travel with `completedAt`) are set once, then frozen, so merging is just "prefer whichever side actually has it set."

**Two people clicking "Start review cycle" for the same workstream before either side had synced the other's write leaves two distinct cycles both marked active** — `activeReviewCycle()` only ever finds the first match, so the second would otherwise sit invisibly in `reviewCycles` with its own confirmations/`changeLog` nobody ever sees again. `reconcileDuplicateActiveCycles()`, called from `normalizeData()` (so it runs on every load/merge, not just right after a merge that could have caused it — cheap, and self-heals the same situation however it arose, including from data saved before this reconciliation existed at all), folds every later-started active cycle for a workstream into the earliest one via `mergeReviewCycle()` itself, then drops the duplicate.

**`mergeWorkstreamFields(existing, incoming, respectTombstones)`** is `mergeReviewCycle()`'s counterpart one level up: `actionLog`/`decisionLog` merge per-entry via `mergeKeyedRecords()`, tombstone-aware (`deletedActionLogIds`/`deletedDecisionLogIds` — new tombstone lists, same shape as `deletedItemIds`); `name`/`color` merge as plain scalars, newer `updatedAt` wins. This is the reason workstreams gained a real `updatedAt` at all (see "Data model" above) — dedup-by-name only applies any more to a genuinely *new* id; an already-known workstream (matched by id first) always merges into itself regardless of what its current name happens to be.

**Deleting an Action Log/Decision Log entry now tombstones it, where it never used to need to.** `deleteActionLogItem()`/`deleteDecisionLogItem()` (undo-toast, same shape as `deleteItem()`) and the two cascade-removal functions, `removeActionLogForCycle()`/`removeDecisionLogForCycle()` (called from `removeMinutes()` — see "Review mode" below), all now call `tombstone(deletedActionLogIds/deletedDecisionLogIds, id)` — undo calls `untombstone()` back. **`syncDecisionLogFromMinutes()`'s own implicit drop needs the identical treatment**: re-saving a cycle's minutes with a decision line removed doesn't just filter that row out of `w.decisionLog` any more, it tombstones it too — without that, a later merge with a stale incoming copy of the same workstream's `decisionLog` (unaware the row was intentionally removed here) could silently resurrect it. `syncActionLogFromMinutes()` has no matching implicit-drop case at all — it never removes rows on its own (see "Action Log" below), only `text`/`owner`/`dueDate` update in place, and only bump that row's own `updatedAt` when something actually changed (the same "don't make an untouched record look freshly edited" discipline `stampChangedMilestones()` already applies to an item's own milestones).

**An existing item's milestones and its own scalar fields (name/owner/status/dates/tags) merge independently of each other**, not as one all-or-nothing snapshot. `mergeMilestonesArray(existingList, incomingList, respectTombstones)` merges the `milestones` array by milestone `id`: an incoming milestone with a newer `updatedAt` than its local counterpart replaces it; one whose id isn't found locally is appended (tombstone-gated the same way items are, via `deletedMilestoneIds` — see below); one absent from the incoming list simply isn't touched (mirroring how a remotely-deleted *item* also isn't actively removed locally — see the next paragraph). Only *then* does `mergeData()` decide the item's own scalar fields, comparing the item's own top-level `updatedAt` (`Object.assign`-ing everything except `milestones`, which was already handled). This split is what lets two people editing *different* milestones on the same item — or one editing a milestone while another renames the item — both keep their change, instead of whichever side's `updatedAt` happened to be newer winning the entire item, milestones included. Each milestone carries its own `updatedAt`, stamped wherever its content actually changes: `updateMilestoneDateField()`/`cycleMilestoneStatus()` (direct row edits), `saveItem()` (see `stampChangedMilestones()` below), and `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()` for a freshly-appended one. `normalizeData()` backfills a missing `updatedAt` (legacy data) from the *parent item's* `updatedAt`, not `Date.now()` — see its own comment for why arriving-late data shouldn't look freshly edited to a merge just because this device happened to load it first.

**`stampChangedMilestones(newList, prevList)`** (used only by `saveItem()`) is what keeps the modal save from defeating the point of the above: the modal edits a detached working copy (`editingMilestones`) and writes the *whole* array back on Save, so naively stamping every milestone's `updatedAt` there would make an untouched milestone look freshly edited to a later merge — silently outranking a genuine edit someone else made to it elsewhere in the meantime. It diffs each new milestone's `name`/`dueDate`/`status`/`actualDate`/`notApplicable` against the item's current (pre-save) copy and only bumps `updatedAt` for the ones that actually changed; unchanged ones keep their prior timestamp exactly. A milestone present in the item before the save but missing from the new list was removed in the modal, and gets tombstoned into `deletedMilestoneIds` (same shape as `deletedItemIds`) so a merge can't resurrect it from a stale external copy. `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()` follows the same rule at the template-sync level: a newly-appended milestone gets `updatedAt: Date.now()`, and every milestone removed by the sync (this is the destructive add/remove path, not the reorder-only one) is tombstoned the same way.

Inside the items pass, a tombstoned incoming id is skipped *unless* the incoming `updatedAt` is newer than the tombstone's `deletedAt` — that case is treated as a genuine edit made elsewhere after the local delete, and the stale tombstone is cleared to let it back in. **A deleted workstream still has no such escape hatch inside `mergeData()` itself, even now that workstreams have a real `updatedAt`** (see "Data model" above) — the workstream-deletion tombstone check never compared against `iw.updatedAt` in the first place (there's nothing to "re-add newer than the deletion," since a deleted workstream is spliced out of `workstreams` entirely, not left behind with a stale copy the way an item/milestone deletion's own escape hatch is reasoning about); the only way one comes back is still through `opts.respectTombstones: false` (below).

**Items and milestones — and, one level further down, Action Log/Decision Log entries — now proactively sweep a locally-existing record whose tombstone just merged in, not only guard against an incoming copy resurrecting one.** This used to be a documented, accepted scope limit: `mergeData()` merged the tombstone *lists* themselves (so a deletion made elsewhere was at least known locally), but never actively removed a locally-existing item/milestone just because an incoming file's own copy no longer listed it — it only ever *prevented re-adding* something already known to be deleted. A device that already had its own copy of an item/milestone/action item/decision *before* a deletion made elsewhere ever reached it kept that copy indefinitely — worse, its own next write (triggered by any unrelated edit at all) could silently republish that stale copy right back into the shared file, resurrecting a deletion that had already correctly landed everywhere else. A user-reported bug (first diagnosed for meeting-minutes deletion cascading to Action Log/Decision Log — see `mergeKeyedRecords()`'s own comment below — then confirmed to apply identically to scope items and milestones) is what turned this from an accepted limit into a fixed one. `mergeData()`'s own items pass and `mergeMilestonesArray()` both now run the identical sweep `mergeKeyedRecords()` does (see its own comment for the full mechanics): before ever looking at what's incoming, drop any locally-existing record whose own tombstone (just merged in by the top-level tombstone pass, which always runs first) is at least as new as that record's own last edit — gated on `respectTombstones`, so `applyImport('merge')`'s own deliberate opt-out still leaves a local copy alone here too. Verified with two real Chromium pages (Playwright) sharing one fake sync folder: device 2 (with its own real local data, including its own synced-in copy of a scope item) correctly drops it once device 1 deletes the scope item and its tombstone merges in, and device 2's own subsequent unrelated write is confirmed not to resurrect it in the shared file.

**The workstream-level deletion case is the one exception that's genuinely unaffected by any of this** — a deleted workstream is spliced out of `workstreams` entirely rather than tombstoned-and-swept the way an item/milestone/action-item deletion is, so there's no analogous "local copy lingers after its tombstone merges in" gap to close there in the first place; see the paragraph above for why that check never had the incoming-side escape hatch either.

### Conflict detection

`lastSyncedAt` (module-level, starts at `0`, reset to `0` whenever the folder link is cleared) is the merge "base" — the last time this browser is known to have successfully reconciled with the linked folder (a link, reconnect, write-time merge, or background poll). `mergeMilestonesArray()`/`mergeData()` use it to tell a *genuine* concurrent edit apart from "their copy just happened to be newer": an item or milestone only counts as conflicting if **both** sides' `updatedAt` are newer than `lastSyncedAt` — i.e. both were touched since the two sides last agreed. `lastSyncedAt === 0` disables this entirely, which matters specifically for the very first merge against a freshly-linked folder: with no prior sync, "since the last sync" has no meaning yet, and everything existing locally would otherwise look freshly edited relative to a base that never happened. Conflict detection is deliberately per-milestone-id, not "did anything change on each side of the whole array" — two *different* milestones each independently edited on their own side merge cleanly and are correctly not a conflict, even though naively checking "something changed on both sides" for the array as a whole would say otherwise (a bug caught in the test that specifically distinguishes the two).

Detecting a conflict never changes how it's resolved — the merge above already picked a winner (newer `updatedAt`, per-item or per-milestone) regardless. `conflicts` (an array of `{itemName, milestoneName, note}`) is purely a plain-English audit trail so "resolved automatically" doesn't also mean "invisibly": `reportSyncConflicts(conflicts)` appends timestamped entries to `syncConflictLog` (module-level, session-only — not persisted, since this is a heads-up for whoever's at the keyboard right now, not permanent history), shows a toast with a "Review" action (see `showToast()`'s generalized `actionLabel` param below), and updates a small amber badge next to `#fileSyncIndicator` (`updateSyncConflictsUI()`) that stays up for as long as anything is unreviewed, since the toast itself is easy to miss. `openSyncConflictsModal()`/`closeSyncConflictsModal()`/`renderSyncConflictsModal()`/`clearSyncConflictLog()` follow the same open/render/close pattern as the Backups modal. `showToast(msg, isError, undoAction, actionLabel)` gained the 4th parameter for this — every other caller omits it and keeps the original "Undo" label; conflicts is the one case where that label would be a lie about what the button does.

**The badge/toast/modal are all Editor+ only — an explicit user request.** `syncConflictLog` itself keeps accumulating regardless of the current role — a conflict can happen while a Visitor/Reviewer is the one looking at the screen, and shouldn't be silently dropped just because that role can't act on it — this is purely a display gate, the same "log everything, gate what's shown" split several other role-gated features in this app already use. Three call sites: `updateSyncConflictsUI()` renders the badge only when `hasRole('editor') && syncConflictLog.length` (below Editor it's an empty string, same as the badge simply having nothing to show); `reportSyncConflicts()` still always pushes onto the log, but only shows the "Review" toast at Editor+ — the same "don't prompt a role that has nothing to do here" reasoning `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` already applies to a Visitor/Reviewer, since `openSyncConflictsModal()` would just refuse them anyway; and `openSyncConflictsModal()` itself carries the actual `requireRole('editor')` guard as the defense-in-depth second layer behind the other two. `render()` now also calls `updateSyncConflictsUI()` unconditionally (cheap enough, same as `renderPlanningStatusChips()`'s own "no mode check needed" reasoning) specifically so a role switch mid-session immediately hides or reshows the badge, rather than leaving it stale until the next conflict actually happens or gets cleared.

**The one exception: `applyImport('merge')`.** `mergeData()` defaults `opts.respectTombstones` to `true`; `applyImport('merge')` is the only caller that passes `false`. Picking a specific file and clicking Import → Merge is a deliberate, one-off action — unlike `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()`/`initFileSync()`/`doFileSyncWrite()`, which merge automatically in the background, where silently resurrecting a deletion via a stale copy would be surprising. With the flag off, a matching tombstone is cleared and the incoming item (or workstream) is added regardless of relative timestamps.

**`mergeReviewCycle()`/`mergeWorkstreamFields()` deliberately do not feed `conflicts` at all — a genuine, deferred scope decision, not an oversight.** Both always resolve a concurrent edit the same deterministic way (newer `updatedAt` wins, per record) regardless of whether it's reported anywhere; wiring their own concurrent-edit cases into the same `lastSyncedAt`-based "both sides touched since last sync" check `mergeMilestonesArray()` already uses would be a mechanical extension of the same idea, but wasn't part of what was actually asked for when this shipped, so it was left for a genuine follow-up request rather than folded in silently.

**`programme` now merges field-by-field too, via `mergeProgramme(existing, incoming)` — a later, explicit user request/fix, closing a real gap `mergeData()` had from the start: it never touched `data.programme` in any form.** Before this, the *only* place an incoming `programme` object ever got applied at all was `replaceFromFileData()`, gated by `isFreshLocalState()` — which only ever fires for a genuinely untouched, brand-new install (see that function's own comment). Once a device has any real data of its own, an incoming `programme` change — a role password set on a different device (see "Role passwords" in "Roles (RBAC)" above), or a rename — was simply never applied through the ordinary link/reconnect/poll/write-time merge cycle, staying permanently stuck at whatever that device's own copy already had.

`name` merges as a plain scalar sharing `programme.updatedAt` — it changes rarely enough (a rename) that a genuinely concurrent edit isn't worth a finer-grained merge. `rolePasswords` gets real per-role granularity instead, via a companion `rolePasswordsUpdatedAt` map (one timestamp per role, independent of whether that role's own entry is currently a real `{salt,hash}` or `null` — a plain `null` can't carry its own `updatedAt` inline the way a milestone's own field can, which is why this timestamp lives in its own companion object rather than inside each entry): an Admin on one device changing the Editor password must never have any chance of clobbering a Reviewer password someone else set on another device around the same time, or vice versa, the way a single shared `programme.updatedAt` would risk — whichever side touched *anything* about `programme` most recently would otherwise win the *whole* object, silently discarding an unrelated, non-conflicting change made on the other side. `normalizeData()` backfills both `programme.updatedAt` and `rolePasswordsUpdatedAt` to `0` (not `Date.now()`) when missing, the same "an untouched legacy record shouldn't look freshly edited to a merge just because this device happened to load it first" convention every other legacy-`updatedAt` backfill in this app already follows. `mergeData()` calls `mergeProgramme(programme, data.programme)` right at the top (a no-op when `data.programme` is absent — e.g. a stray *workstream*-only file's own wrapped merge, which was never meant to touch `programme` at all), and folds its own return into the umbrella `changed` flag the same way every other sub-merge already does. Verified with two real Chromium pages (Playwright) sharing one fake sync folder, both seeded with their own independent real data (so `isFreshLocalState()` is false on both): setting a role password on one and polling from the other picks it up through the ordinary merge path, correctly verifies, and leaves the second device's own unrelated local workstream untouched.

**`programme.lastSeenAppVersion` is a third field merged into `programme`, alongside `name`/`rolePasswords` — an explicit user request, following an exploratory discussion about the real risk of a teammate's device running stale code, not just stale data.** The concrete failure mode discussed: `normalizeData()` mostly heals data-*shape* drift (a missing field backfills to a sensible default), but a device running an older build has no way to know about a genuinely new field or merge behavior at all — its next write could quietly omit something a newer build would have populated. Two directions were weighed for how disruptive the warning should be (a hard, undismissable block vs. a soft nudge) — the user chose the soft direction explicitly, matching the "guardrail, not a gate" stance RBAC and everything else in this app already takes, and for a concrete reason: Pulse has no server and no real push-update mechanism, so a hard block could strand someone with no way to actually resolve it in the moment (away from a terminal, no git access right now).

`APP_VERSION` (top of the `<script>` block) is a plain, hand-incremented integer — not semver, since there's no real major/minor/patch distinction worth tracking for a single-maintainer app, and a bare integer is trivially comparable with no parsing edge cases. It's bumped by hand whenever a change is worth other devices knowing about (a new/changed data field, a merge-behavior change, a real bug fix) — there's no automation enforcing this, the same manual-discipline convention this app's own hand-tuned CSS pixel math and column numbering already rely on throughout.

**`programme.lastSeenAppVersion` merges by plain `Math.max()`, deliberately *not* the `rolePasswordsUpdatedAt`-style companion-timestamp pattern `rolePasswords` needs.** A bare integer already has a total order, so "highest wins" is conflict-free and commutative on its own — the entire reason `rolePasswords` needs a *separate* `rolePasswordsUpdatedAt` map is that a plain `null` password value can't carry its own `updatedAt` inline the way a real `{salt,hash}` object could (see that field's own comment above); an integer has no such gap, so no companion field is needed here at all. `save()` stamps `programme.lastSeenAppVersion = Math.max(programme.lastSeenAppVersion || 0, APP_VERSION)` on *every* save, not just once at load — so the shared file always reflects the highest version any device has actually *written from*, not just loaded or viewed. The `Math.max` (not a plain assignment) matters specifically for a device that's behind: saving its own, older-build edits must never regress the stamp back down from a teammate's already-synced-in newer value.

`normalizeData()` backfills a missing `lastSeenAppVersion` to this device's own current `APP_VERSION`, not `0` — legacy data predating this field carries no real signal about what version last wrote it, and `0` would make every pre-existing save falsely read as "behind" the instant this feature ships, for every device, regardless of whether anyone is actually ahead of anyone else yet.

**`updateOutdatedVersionBanner()` is what actually surfaces the gap** — called unconditionally from `render()` (the same "cheap enough, no mode check needed" reasoning `renderPlanningStatusChips()`/`updateSyncConflictsUI()` already follow), so a version gap shows the moment it's known: right after load, or the instant a sync/poll/merge pulls in a teammate's newer stamp, not just on this device's own next unrelated save. It compares `programme.lastSeenAppVersion` against this device's own running `APP_VERSION`; if the stamp is ahead, `#outdatedVersionBanner` (a plain, persistent, amber bar sitting between the topbar and whichever mode's own container is showing — outside `#scopedBody`/`#adminBody`/`#l1PlansBody` entirely, the same "not mode-scoped" placement the topbar itself already has) shows a message naming both versions explicitly ("You're on an older version of Pulse (vX) — a teammate is on a newer one (vY)."). Amber (`--stat-amber`/`--stat-amber-bg`), not red — the same "worth a look, not an emergency" tier the sync-conflicts badge and a stale review's own tint already use, since being a version behind is a nudge, not a broken or dangerous state. **Deliberately not role-gated at all**, unlike the sync-conflicts badge right next to it in the topbar (Editor+ only) — updating your own local copy of Pulse isn't a permission-gated action the way resolving a sync conflict is, so every role benefits equally from knowing about it. **No dismiss button** — the whole point is staying visible for as long as the gap is real; since it never blocks interaction with the rest of the app underneath it, there's no competing need to let it be closed away.

**The About modal shows this device's own `Version X · <build date>` — a later, explicit user follow-up, so a device can be checked directly rather than only ever being told it's behind.** `APP_BUILD_DATE` (`'YYYY-MM-DD'`, alongside `APP_VERSION` near the top of the script) is hand-updated the same manual-discipline way, but is purely informational — never compared, merged, or read by anything else the way `APP_VERSION` itself is. `openAboutModal()` writes `` `Version ${APP_VERSION} · ${fmtDateY(APP_BUILD_DATE)}` `` into `#aboutVersionLine` at open time (the one piece of this otherwise entirely-static modal that isn't just fixed HTML) — `fmtDateY()`, not the plain, no-year `fmtDate()`, since a build date is exactly the kind of value that can be read long after the year it was set and shouldn't rely on "same year as now" being implied.

### Export / Import

`downloadData(data, filename)` is the shared primitive (Blob → object URL → synthetic `<a download>` click). `exportProgramme()` calls it with `currentExportPayload()` (`{version, exported, programme, workstreams, items}` — deliberately no tombstones, same reasoning as PromptLab/mytasks' export format). `triggerImport()` clicks the hidden `#fileInput`; `handleFileImport(event)` reads the picked file, validates it has both `workstreams` and `items` before staging it in `pendingImportData` and opening `importModalBg`. `applyImport(mode)`: `'replace'` downloads a `pulse-backup-before-replace-<date>.json` safety copy first, then overwrites `programme`/`workstreams`/`items` outright; `'merge'` calls `mergeData(pendingImportData, {respectTombstones: false})`. Both branches call `normalizeData()` before `save(); render()`.

### Export to Excel

**A second, distinct export path (an explicit user request) — a formatted, human-facing `.xlsx` report, not the plain-data JSON above.** The plain Export is a lossless backup/interchange format meant to come back in through Import; this one is meant to be opened, read, filtered, or handed to someone who will never open Pulse itself, so it prioritizes looking like a real report over being a complete data dump — no tombstones, no ids, no raw field names.

**ExcelJS (`exceljs.bare.min.js`, cdnjs, pinned to 4.4.0) is this app's one and only external JS dependency** — everything else in this otherwise zero-build, zero-dependency PWA is hand-written. The `.bare` build specifically (not the plain/full build) skips ExcelJS's Node-only stream/fs polyfills that a browser-only page never needs. Loaded eagerly via a `<script>` tag in `<head>` (not lazily on first use of the feature) so it's one predictable, always-precached thing for `sw.js`'s `APP_SHELL` to fetch on install, same as Font Awesome. `exportToExcelReport()` guards against it not having loaded (`typeof ExcelJS === 'undefined'` — a first launch with no network, or the CDN being blocked) with a clear toast rather than a crash; `tests/cases/sync.test.js` exercises exactly that guard, since the real library is never loaded inside the JXA test harness at all.

Three sheets, each with a bold white-on-`#2D3648` header row, a frozen top row, and an autofilter (`excelStyleHeaderRow()`):
- **Scope Items** (`buildScopeItemsSheet()`): Workstream / Category / Scope Item / Status / Due / Actual / Milestones / IT / Business / Budget — **one row per scope item, always**, unlike every other sheet's own row-per-record shape. Unassigned first, then real workstreams in their own display order, items within each in their own manual `order` — matching the status board's own section/row order exactly. Start/IT/Business/Budget stay item-only, since none of those are meaningful at the milestone level (Start's own column is gone entirely now, see below).

  **This used to be one bold item row followed by one indented, italic row per milestone (`alignment: { indent: 2 }`) — a later, explicit user request ("move the milestones of a scope item into column so that every scope item has only one line. remove start as a column") collapsed that into the current single-row shape.** Every milestone is now one line inside a single "Milestones" cell on the item's own row (`wrapText: true`, `vertical: 'top'`), built by `milestoneExcelSummaryLine(m)` — a plain, ExcelJS-free string function kept standalone specifically so it's directly unit-tested (`tests/cases/sync.test.js`) despite `buildScopeItemsSheet()` itself needing a real `ExcelJS.Workbook` the JXA harness never loads. Each line reads `"<name>: <date> (<status>)"`, or a plain `"<name>: N/A"` for a Not Applicable milestone (no date, no real status — mirroring the app's own frozen-N/A-badge treatment for exactly the same milestone) — the Milestones cell itself stays non-bold/italic/muted (`FF666666`), the same look the old per-milestone sub-rows already had, so it still reads as detail on the item rather than part of its own bold line. `row.height` is set from the milestone count (`Math.max(20, count * 15)`, ~15px per line beyond the header row's own 20px floor) so every line actually shows instead of clipping under Excel's own default single-line row height.

  **The date shown for each milestone prefers its own `actualDate` over its `dueDate`, falling back to an em dash when neither is set** — an explicit part of the same request, matching the identical "prefer what's already happened over what's merely planned" convention Dashboard's own Overdue/Upcoming feed already established (see `toEntry()` under "Dashboard mode" above), rather than a separate Due/Actual pair the way the old per-milestone sub-row used to show both. Verified live end-to-end (a real Chromium instance, `Browser.setDownloadBehavior` capturing the actual downloaded `.xlsx`, read back with `openpyxl`): a milestone finished before its own due date reads plain "Completed" with the earlier (actual) date shown; one finished after reads "Completed Late" with that same later date; an undated, still-Pending one reads its status with a bare em dash; a Not Applicable one reads just its own name plus "N/A"; a zero-milestone item's own Milestones cell is genuinely blank, with its row height correctly floored to `20` rather than `0`.
- **Action Log** (`buildActionLogSheet()`): Workstream / Action Item / Owner / Due Date / Source / Created / Closed, across every workstream (reuses `sortedActionLog()`, the same open-first/soonest-due-first-then-most-recently-closed ordering the in-app "All Workstreams" Action Log view already uses). A completed row's whole line gets a struck-through grey font rather than a separate "done" column.
- **Decision Log** (`buildDecisionLogSheet()`): Workstream / Decision / Source / Logged, across every workstream, reusing `sortedDecisionLog()` (most recent first).

**Status/RAG cells are colored with a fixed, hardcoded hex palette (`EXCEL_RAG_COLORS`), not by reading the page's current CSS custom properties** — a report exported today should look identical regardless of which of Pulse's several color schemes (Dracula, GitHub, …) happened to be selected in the browser at export time; the values are simply copied from the "standard" scheme's own `--stat-*` block at the very top of the stylesheet, and are the only thing this feature hardcodes rather than deriving live. `excelRagCellStyle(cell, ragId)` applies both the fill and a matching bold font color in one call, used for Status *and* the IT/Business/Budget tag cells alike, all four being the same `'not-started'|'green'|'amber'|'red'|'complete'` (or plain `'green'|'amber'|'red'` for the tags) vocabulary.

**`excelDateCell(row, key, iso)` builds a real Excel date value (so it sorts/formats/filters as one, not inert text), and deliberately does *not* reuse this app's own `fmtDate()`-style local-time parsing (`new Date(iso + 'T00:00:00')`) to do it.** ExcelJS converts a JS `Date` to an Excel serial number using that Date's *UTC* fields — a `Date` built from local-time parts (as every other date-parsing spot in this app correctly does, since those all stay inside the browser's own local-time rendering) silently rolls back to the *previous* calendar day once serialized, for anyone exporting from a UTC+ timezone (confirmed directly: opened the generated file and read a `2026-07-29` due date back as `2026-07-28`). The fix is `new Date(Date.UTC(y, m - 1, d))` — a Date whose *UTC* representation is exactly midnight on the intended calendar date, so ExcelJS's own UTC-based conversion round-trips it correctly regardless of the exporting browser's timezone. This is the one place in the whole app that has to think about UTC at all, specifically because it's the one place handing a date to something outside the browser's own local-time rendering path.

The download itself follows `downloadData()`'s own Blob-URL-synthetic-`<a>` pattern by hand (rather than reusing that helper directly, since its body is JSON-specific): `wb.xlsx.writeBuffer()` (a `Promise<ArrayBuffer>`) wrapped in a `Blob` with the real OOXML MIME type, filename `<slugified programme name>-<date>.xlsx` (falling back to `Pulse` if the name has no alphanumeric characters left after slugifying).

### Local backups

`save()` calls `maybeSnapshotBackup()` on every save, which takes at most one rolling snapshot per calendar day (keyed by `todayStr()`) into `localStorage["pulse-backups"]`, keeping the last `MAX_BACKUPS` (7) days — a local safety net distinct from file sync, which mirrors live state rather than preserving history. A backup taken earlier in the day isn't refreshed by later saves that same day. The "Data → Backups" modal (`openBackupsModal()`/`renderBackupsModal()`) lists snapshots newest-first; `restoreBackup(date)` opens the confirm modal, and on confirm downloads a safety backup of the *current* data first (same `downloadData()` primitive), then overwrites from the snapshot and calls `normalizeData()`. Backups don't carry tombstones (same reasoning as export/import — a restore is an explicit full replace, not a merge).

### File System Access API sync

**Linking is folder-based, not a single file — one `pulse-index.json` plus one `pulse-ws-<id>.json` per real workstream — a later, explicit user request** ("this is caused since review cycles for workstreams can be edited by other users in parallel. usually, a workstream is only edited by one user. would it make sense to seperate the workstream data into individual json files?"), replacing this feature's own original single-linked-file design. Every `save()` used to rewrite one shared file regardless of which workstream it touched, so two people editing *different* workstreams at the same time still collided on the same file — and when that file lived inside a cloud-synced folder (iCloud Drive, OneDrive, …), the sync client resolved the collision at the byte level, not the JSON level, by renaming one write into a device-named "conflicted copy" file that Pulse never automatically merges back in. Splitting persistence per workstream means two people on two different workstreams never touch the same file at all — only concurrent edits to the *same* workstream's own file still can, which is exactly the case Pulse's existing field-level merge (`mergeReviewCycle()`/`mergeWorkstreamFields()`, see "Merge and last-write-wins" above) already handles losslessly.

**None of this touches the in-memory data model.** `workstreams`/`items`/`categories`/`reviewCycles` stay exactly the flat, single arrays every other part of this app already reads and writes — Planning, Review, Dashboard, Admin, Excel export, plain Export/Import, local rolling backups are all completely unaffected by any of what follows. Only the file-sync layer's own read/write logic changed.

**File layout.** `pulse-index.json` holds `programme`, `categories`, `deletedWorkstreamIds`, and — a deliberate simplification, see below — all five of `deletedItemIds`/`deletedMilestoneIds`/`deletedActionLogIds`/`deletedDecisionLogIds`/`deletedReviewCycleIds`. Its own `workstreams` array is metadata only (`{id, name, color, order, updatedAt}` — no `actionLog`/`decisionLog`, which live in each workstream's own file instead), and its own `items` array holds only the ones with `workstreamId === null` — Unassigned scope items, since they don't belong to a single workstream. Each real workstream's own file holds its own `items`, its own `reviewCycles`, and its own `actionLog`/`decisionLog`. Tombstones deliberately all stay in the index rather than being split per-workstream too — an item can move between workstreams (or in/out of Unassigned) over its lifetime, so a tombstone has no one fixed owning file the way live data does (a review cycle's own `workstreamId` never actually moves once created, unlike an item's, but its tombstone lives here anyway for the same simplicity/consistency reasons every other tombstone type does); a delete inside a workstream ends up touching two files (that workstream's own file, plus the index) instead of one, but tombstones are tiny `{id, deletedAt}` pairs and rare relative to the actual friction (milestone status/date edits, confirmations, action items) this split exists to isolate.

**`mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme`/`filterWorkstreamId` are deliberately excluded from `pulse-index.json` entirely — a later, explicit user correction, after tracing through what actually consumes an incoming synced file.** These four fields were part of the index from the original single-file-to-multi-file split (carried over from the old flat blob, which always included them), but neither `mergeData()` (the ordinary merge path, used by every write/poll cycle) nor `replaceFromFileData()` (the one-time fresh-device replace path) ever reads any of the four back out of an incoming file — both simply ignore them. That made writing them pure cost with no corresponding benefit: `mode`/`filterWorkstreamId` are also read by `buildIndexPayload()`, so switching Review/Planning tabs or picking a different workstream in the sidebar changed `indexText` on every navigation, triggering a real (write-only, never-consumed) rewrite of `pulse-index.json` — reintroducing, on a smaller scale and for zero payoff, exactly the kind of index-file collision surface this whole split exists to shrink. `buildIndexPayload()`/`recombineSyncData()` no longer read or write any of the four; they remain ordinary per-device `localStorage` state (see "Roles (RBAC)"'s own `userRole`-isolation paragraph below for the same "device state doesn't belong in the shared file" reasoning applied to a different field) — round-tripping the *last active view* to a teammate opening the folder fresh was never actually implemented in the first place, so nothing observable changed by removing the write.

**A workstream's own filename is readable, not the bare id — `pulse-ws-<slug>-<id-suffix>.json` — a later, explicit user follow-up** ("the workstream json file now has a crypting id. can we use the workstream name instead?"). `slugify(name)` (lowercase, non-alphanumeric runs collapsed to one `-`, edges trimmed, capped at 40 characters, falling back to the literal string `"workstream"` for a name with nothing slug-safe in it at all — blank, or symbols/emoji only) makes the file readable at a glance in the linked folder; `wsFileName(id, name)` still appends the trailing 6 characters of the workstream's own `id` (exactly the random suffix `genId()` itself already ends with) specifically so the slug alone is never relied on for uniqueness — two workstreams named identically, or that merely slugify to the same string, still get two distinct files. **The filename is recomputed fresh from a workstream's *current* name on every write, not stored or pinned at creation** — the same "no persisted state, always fresh at render/write time" instinct several other computed values in this app already follow (Dashboard mode's own rollups, for instance) — so renaming a workstream is not a rename-on-disk: the very next write simply computes a new slug and writes a new file under it, with no special rename-handling code anywhere. **The old file is left behind as a harmless orphan**, the identical "never delete data we don't have to" stance a deleted workstream's own now-unreferenced file already takes just above.

This one property — the filename itself, not just a workstream's file *content*, can change between two writes with nothing else about the workstream touched — needed its own tracking, separate from content-diffing: `lastSyncedFileNames` (module-level, `{[workstreamId]: filename}`, reset to `{}` alongside `lastSyncedSnapshots` whenever the folder link changes) records which filename each workstream's own file was last read from or written to. `buildWorkstreamPayload()`'s own content (items/reviewCycles/actionLog/decisionLog) never includes the workstream's `name` at all — only the index does — so a pure rename leaves that content byte-identical to what was already on disk, and content-diffing alone (`text !== lastSyncedSnapshots[id]`) would never notice that the *filename* computed for it (via `buildAllSyncPayloads()`'s own `wsFileNames`, keyed by id like `wsTexts`) has changed. `doFileSyncWrite()`'s write loop checks both: `text !== lastSyncedSnapshots[id] || fname !== lastSyncedFileNames[id]` — either alone is enough to trigger a write (a rename with unchanged content writes that same content to a brand-new file; a content edit under an unchanged name is the ordinary case this already handled). Every place a workstream's own file is successfully *read* — `doFileSyncWrite()`'s and `pollFileSync()`'s own read loops, and `readAndRecombineFolder()` (used by `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()`) — stamps `lastSyncedFileNames[id]` to whatever filename it just read from, the same "the baseline is set the moment the file is seen" timing `lastSyncedSnapshots` already follows, so a read never mistakes an already-correctly-named file for one needing a redundant rewrite.

**Which *name* to use when reading an existing workstream's file is a separate question from which name to use when writing one, and the two deliberately don't share a code path.** Writing always uses the workstream's own *current* live name (via `buildAllSyncPayloads()`, iterating live `workstreams` directly) — that's unambiguously correct, since the write is what's establishing the current state. Reading needs `wsNameLookup(indexWorkstreams)` instead: a plain `{id: name}` map built from local `workstreams` first, then overridden by whatever the *freshly-read* index says for the same id — the index wins, because the file that's actually sitting on disk was written under whatever name its own last writer used, and the just-read index is the best available signal for what that was; reading under a stale locally-cached name after a remote rename would look for a file that no longer exists. (Local name is the only source for an id the index doesn't know about yet — a workstream created locally and not yet synced out — which is moot in practice, since there's no remote file to find for one of those anyway.)

**A pure partition/recombine layer makes this tractable with zero changes to the ~150 call sites of `save()` scattered across the app.** `wsFileName(id, name)` (→ `` `pulse-ws-${slugify(name)}-${id.slice(-6)}.json` `` — see "A workstream's own filename is readable" below for why it takes a name at all), `buildIndexPayload()`/`buildWorkstreamPayload(wsId)` (pure functions of the live globals, building the plain objects described above), `buildAllSyncPayloads()` (→ `{indexText, wsTexts, wsFileNames}`, `JSON.stringify`'d, both `wsTexts` and `wsFileNames` keyed by workstream id — never by filename, which the next section explains isn't a stable enough key to use), and `recombineSyncData(indexData, wsDataById)` (the inverse — reconstructs the flat `{programme, workstreams, items, categories, reviewCycles, deletedWorkstreamIds, deletedItemIds, deletedMilestoneIds, deletedActionLogIds, deletedDecisionLogIds}` shape `mergeData()`/`isFreshLocalState()`/`replaceFromFileData()` already expect — `mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme`/`filterWorkstreamId` are deliberately not part of this shape at all any more, see the paragraph above, each recombined workstream `{...indexMeta, actionLog: wsFile?.actionLog||[], decisionLog: wsFile?.decisionLog||[]}`, a missing/unreadable per-workstream file recombining as empty rather than throwing) live near the other merge functions. At write time, the current in-memory state's full JSON text is computed for the index and for every known workstream file, diffed against what was last actually written, and only the files that changed get written — no caller needs to say which workstream it touched, the diffing figures it out. At read/merge time, every file the index lists gets read and recombined into that one familiar flat shape, which is then handed to `mergeData()`/`isFreshLocalState()`/`replaceFromFileData()` completely unchanged — none of the merge logic itself (`mergeData()`, `mergeWorkstreamFields()`, `mergeReviewCycle()`, `mergeKeyedRecords()`, `mergeMilestonesArray()`) has any idea it's looking at data assembled from several files instead of one. `readJsonFile(dirHandle, name)`/`writeJsonFile(dirHandle, name, text)` are the only I/O primitives underneath all of this — thin wrappers with no understanding of file *content* at all; `readJsonFile()` returns `null` (not a throw) for a missing or unparseable file, the same "never crash the whole cycle over one unreadable file" stance `recombineSyncData()` itself takes for a missing per-workstream entry.

`syncDirHandle` (module-level, `null` until linked) plus the same tiny hand-rolled IndexedDB wrapper (`_fsIdb`/`_fsGet`/`_fsPut`/`_fsDel`, db `pulse-fs`) persist the linked *directory* handle across reloads — Chrome/Edge only (`window.showDirectoryPicker` feature-detected; Safari has none of this). `save()` calls `queueFileSyncWrite()` whenever `syncDirHandle` is set. `doFileSyncWrite()` (the actual write) reads the index first (the authoritative list of which workstream files exist at all); a workstream's own file can change without the index ever changing (the common case — a milestone confirmation or an `actionLog` entry never touches the index), so every currently-known workstream is re-read too, not just re-checked when the index itself looks stale. If anything read differs from its own last-known text, it's recombined and merged via `mergeData()` before writing — then whichever files the (possibly just-merged) live state implies have actually changed get written, **workstream files first, index last**, so a device polling mid-write never sees the index announce a workstream whose own file doesn't exist on disk yet.

**Writes are serialized and always reflect the latest state, not a stale snapshot.** `save()` fires on nearly every mutation in the app — a keystroke, a status cycle, a rename — and each one used to kick off its own unawaited write with a snapshot captured at that exact moment. Since a single write cycle does several `await`s (`requestPermission`, reading files back for the conflict check, `createWritable` per changed file) before anything touches disk, two overlapping write cycles could finish in either order — and if the one carrying *older* data happened to win, whatever the newer write should have persisted (a just-typed rename, say) would silently be overwritten back to the stale value, with no error and nothing visibly wrong in the app itself (`localStorage` — checked on next load — still had the correct value). `queueFileSyncWrite()`/`fileSyncWriteInFlight`/`fileSyncWritePending` fix this: at most one write cycle is ever in flight; a `save()` that lands mid-write just sets a pending flag rather than starting a second overlapping one, and when the in-flight cycle finishes, exactly one more starts if anything was flagged — reading `programme`/`workstreams`/etc. fresh at that later moment, not whatever they were when originally queued. This collapses any burst of rapid saves into the minimum necessary writes, and guarantees the linked folder always converges on the true latest state. A write that fails for any other reason (not just a lost permission, which already has its own reconnect flow) now also toasts an error, instead of failing completely silently.

**`doFileSyncWrite()`'s and `pollFileSync()`'s own "did the merge find anything worth reacting to" gate is keyed off `mergeData()`'s `changed` flag, not `added || updated` alone** — a workstream/category/review-cycle merge can genuinely change something (a confirmation, an `actionLog` entry) with zero items involved, and that must still reach `localStorage`/the screen rather than sitting unsaved in memory until some unrelated later `save()`. The "Synced changes from another tab/device" toast falls back to a plain, count-less message when `added`/`updated` are both zero but `changed` is still true, rather than showing a confusing "0 added, 0 updated." `linkFolder()` merges (or, for a genuinely fresh local state, wholesale-replaces from) any existing content in the chosen folder on first link — see "Linking is folder-based" above for the three shapes it can find there. `reconnectFolder()` re-requests write permission (browsers can silently revoke it, e.g. after a restart) and merges. `initFileSync()` runs at page load: if a handle was persisted in IndexedDB, it tries to reuse it (merging the folder's content in if permission is still granted, or prompting to reconnect if not) — **and, since a handle persisted by a pre-this-feature version of the app is a `FileSystemFileHandle` (`kind: 'file'`), not a directory, that mismatch is checked explicitly and treated exactly like "nothing usable stored" rather than crashing on a directory-only method call** — this is the one explicit upgrade-compatibility guard the whole multi-file layout hinges on for an existing linked-file setup; if there's no usable persisted handle at all, it opens the onboarding modal (`openFileSyncModal()`), which — see "Linking is mandatory" below — a first-time user must actually resolve, not dismiss. Every one of these three (plus `doFileSyncWrite()`) sets `lastSyncedSnapshots` (one entry per file — `index` for the index, one per workstream id for the rest, the multi-file counterpart of what used to be a single string) and, for workstream files specifically, `lastSyncedFileNames` too (see "A workstream's own filename is readable" above) *and* `lastSyncedAt` (still one global timestamp, not per-file — it's a time threshold for "touched since we last agreed", meaningful across the whole reconciliation regardless of how many files it spanned) together at the point they've just reconciled with the folder — see "Conflict detection" above for why the latter matters. `updateFileSyncUI()` swaps the topbar's `#fileSyncIndicator` between four states (no picker support → hidden entirely, unlinked → "Link folder" button, linked-and-granted → folder name + unlink button, linked-but-permission-lapsed → amber "Reconnect" button) via `fadeSwapHTML()`. This indicator is the *only* entry point into `openFileSyncModal()` — there's deliberately no static "File sync" item in the Data dropdown, for the same "a fixed menu entry can't reflect which of four states is current" reason as before.

**Linking a folder is mandatory for every role — the same policy this feature has always had, now applied to a folder instead of a file.** `fileSyncModalBg` has no dismiss button and is excluded from both the global Escape-key handler and the generic backdrop-click-to-close wiring, the same "must complete before closing" treatment `scopeAssignModalBg` established elsewhere in this file. It now offers only **2 options, not 3** — "Choose a folder" (`linkFolder()`) and the existing conditional "Reconnect to `<name>`" (`reconnectFolder()`) — since a folder transparently covers what used to need a separate "link existing file"/"create new file" pair: `linkFolder()` itself branches on what it finds there — a current-format `pulse-index.json` (read, merge-or-replace exactly as before), a pre-this-feature single legacy `pulse-data.json` and no index yet (**migrated automatically, with no separate "migrate" step for the user to find** — read as the already-flat shape it is, merge-or-replace, then let the normal write path materialize the full index+per-workstream file set in one pass, since with no prior `lastSyncedSnapshots` entries *everything* computes as "changed"; the old file is left in place, untouched, never auto-deleted, and `pulse-index.json`'s presence always takes priority over it on every future link), or neither (an empty or unrelated folder — start fresh there, same as the old "Create new file" case). Both option `onclick`s call `closeFileSyncModal()` only from *inside* their own success path (after `syncDirHandle` is actually set and the initial read/write/merge has completed) — a cancelled or failed native picker (an `AbortError`) leaves the modal open with no extra handling needed, since nothing ever closed it in the first place.

**A collapsed-by-default "sync this first" disclosure sits inside `fileSyncModalBg`, below the two real options — a user-reported onboarding gap, not a code bug: the native picker can only ever show a folder already synced to local disk by the OneDrive desktop client, and a teammate who's never clicked "Sync" on the SharePoint/Teams site itself has nothing to pick here at all, with nothing in the modal hinting that a separate, outside-this-app step needs to happen first.** `fileSyncHelpToggle`/`fileSyncHelpPanel` is a plain expand/collapse (`toggleFileSyncHelp()`) — not a gate, not part of the mandatory-linking flow, just a "still stuck?" checklist a confused first-time user can open on their own; it never blocks or changes what `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()` actually do. `detectFileSyncHelpOs()` reads `navigator.platform`/`navigator.userAgent` (each teammate opens Pulse on their own machine, so this is a genuine per-device signal, not something to cache or persist) to decide which OS-specific steps `fileSyncHelpStepsHtml(os)` renders — Finder/menu-bar wording for Mac, File Explorer/system-tray wording for Windows — falling back to showing **both**, clearly labeled, when neither is recognized, deliberately not a hard requirement to get right (guessing wrong just shows one extra, harmless section). `openFileSyncModal()` always resets the panel back to collapsed on open, so a returning user who already knows what to do never sees a stale, still-expanded panel from a previous open. The toggle's own chevron icon is a separate `id`ed element (`fileSyncHelpToggleIcon`), not reached via `.querySelector()` from the button — this project's JXA test harness stubs every fake element's `querySelector()`/`querySelectorAll()` to return `null`/`[]` unconditionally (see "Tests" above), so reaching into a button for its own icon that way would crash the very first test that opens this modal.

**Unlinking is still allowed — a legitimate way to switch to a different folder or undo a mistake — but immediately reopens the same mandatory modal rather than leaving the programme unlinked.** `unlinkFolder()` calls `openFileSyncModal(null, false)` right after `_clearFileLink()` (the `isLaunch: false` matters here — this reopen is not part of the original launch sequence, so its own close must not unexpectedly chain into the backup-folder onboarding modal a second time; see `closeFileSyncModal()`'s own `fileSyncModalIsLaunchFlow` check below). The topbar's own Unlink button (inside `updateFileSyncUI()`'s linked-and-granted state) is otherwise untouched.

**Background polling.** Every write-triggering path above only ever notices an external change (a teammate's edit, synced down by something like OneDrive/SharePoint) at the moment *this* browser itself writes — someone just reading the board, or with it open in an idle tab, would otherwise never see anything new until they touched something or reloaded. `startFileSyncPolling()`/`stopFileSyncPolling()` run `pollFileSync()` on a `setInterval` (`FILE_SYNC_POLL_MS`, 45s) for as long as `syncDirHandle` is set — started from every successful link/reconnect/init path above, stopped in `_clearFileLink()`. `pollFileSync()` reads the index plus every workstream file the union of (locally known ids ∪ the freshly-read index's own ids) implies — a workstream's own file can change without the index ever changing at all, so it isn't enough to only re-read the index and compare its own text — and, when nothing among those texts differs from `lastSyncedSnapshots`, skips straight to the stray-file scan below rather than merging/writing anything; only merges + `save()`s (which *does* trigger a real write, through the normal path) when it finds something worth reconciling. This costs more reads per poll than the old single-file version (one per known workstream, plus the index), but reads alone never create an OS-level sync conflict — only concurrent *writes* do, and `doFileSyncWrite()`'s own per-file diffing is what keeps those scoped to just the files that actually changed. It uses `queryPermission`, never `requestPermission` — a background timer has no user gesture behind it, so it must never risk popping an OS permission prompt on its own; a lapsed permission surfaces through the existing "Reconnect" affordance instead, same as any other permission loss.
