**Stray sync-conflict-copy detection & absorption.** A user-reported gap, surfaced by a direct question: with several people editing the same workstream through a cloud-synced folder (OneDrive, in the reported case), a genuine byte-level collision on that workstream's own file leaves the cloud client unable to reconcile the two versions itself — its usual move is to keep one under the file's real name and rename the *other* into a "conflicted copy" (the exact convention varies by provider: `<name>-<device>.json`, `<name> (<user>'s conflicted copy ...).json`, `<name> (1).json`, ...). Every read path in this app only ever looks for the *exact* filename `pulse-index.json`/`wsFileName()` computes — a renamed conflict copy was completely invisible to all of them, so whatever edits it carried would sit there, unmerged, until a human happened to notice the stray file and reconcile it by hand. Waiting doesn't fix this on its own: the cloud client has no semantic understanding of the JSON content, so the two files just sit there indefinitely with no automatic reconciliation of any kind.

`isStrayFileName(name, base, exactName)`/`strayMergedFileName(name, n)` are the pure detection/naming logic — a deliberately loose prefix match (`name.startsWith(base) && name.endsWith('.json')`, excluding the exact canonical name and anything already carrying the suffix below) rather than an attempt to parse any one provider's own convention, so it works the same way regardless of which cloud sync client produced the copy. `findStrayFiles(dirHandle, base, exactName)`/`fileExists(dirHandle, name)`/`renameStrayFile(dirHandle, name, text)`/`scanAndMergeStrayFiles(dirHandle)` are the I/O layer around them. Each stray file found — the index's own, or any currently-known workstream's own — is folded into the in-memory state through the exact same `mergeData()` the canonical files already use: a stray file's content is structurally nothing different to that function than "another incoming version of this data" (a stray *index* file is recombined via `recombineSyncData(strayData, {})` first, so a workstream mentioned only there still gets its `actionLog`/`decisionLog` correctly defaulted to `[]` before reaching `mergeData()`'s own push-a-new-workstream branch, the same normalization every other index read already gets; a stray *workstream* file is wrapped as `{items, reviewCycles, workstreams:[{id, name, color, order, updatedAt:0, actionLog, decisionLog}]}` — the `updatedAt:0` on that synthetic wrapper is deliberate, since a workstream *file*'s own content never carries its real name/color at all, and `0` guarantees `mergeWorkstreamFields()` only ever moves `actionLog`/`decisionLog`/items/reviewCycles, never a stale name/color overwrite).

**Renamed, not deleted** — prefixing with `merged_` (e.g. `merged_pulse-ws-alpha-abc123-JOHNS-MAC.json`; disambiguated with `merged_2_`, `merged_3_`, ... if that exact name is somehow already taken) rather than removing the stray file outright, the same "never delete data we don't have to" stance a renamed/deleted workstream's own now-orphaned sync file already gets (see `wsFileName()`'s own comment above). A **leading** prefix, not a trailing suffix — an explicit user request, so "merged" reads at a glance at the front of the filename instead of trailing after a long device-suffixed original name. `slugify()` never produces an underscore (every non-alphanumeric run collapses to a single `-`), and neither does a workstream's own id-derived suffix, so no genuine `pulse-index.json`/`wsFileName()` output can ever start with `merged_` — it's an unambiguous marker for "this app already absorbed this one". There's no direct rename in the File System Access API, so this is write-under-the-new-name-then-`removeEntry()`-the-original, the same two-step shape any other operation in this app with no atomic primitive already takes (`writeBackupCopy()`'s own existence-check). A file already renamed by a previous scan is recognized by that same prefix and skipped outright on every later scan — merging it again would be a guaranteed no-op, but skipping it means one less file read every cycle. Every merge is also logged through the exact same conflict-audit trail (`reportSyncConflicts()`) a field-level clash already uses — an explicit part of the request, since resolving this automatically shouldn't also mean it happened invisibly; each entry names the stray file and what it was renamed to.

**Runs from `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()`** (right after the normal canonical-file merge, so `workstreams` already reflects current state — a natural moment to sweep up whatever accumulated in the folder while this device wasn't watching) **and from every `pollFileSync()` cycle** — unconditionally, not just when the canonical files' own text changed, since a stray conflict copy can appear in the very same moment the winning side's content stays byte-identical to what this device already has. Deliberately *not* also wired into `doFileSyncWrite()` — that path fires on every single save, and a directory-wide listing (`dirHandle.values()`) is cheap but not free, so it stays confined to the two lower-frequency hooks; a stray file left behind between saves is still caught within one `FILE_SYNC_POLL_MS` interval regardless.

**Unlike the rest of this section, `scanAndMergeStrayFiles()`/`renameStrayFile()`/`findStrayFiles()`/`fileExists()` all take a `dirHandle` as a plain parameter rather than reading the `syncDirHandle` global directly, so — unlike `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()`/`pollFileSync()` themselves, real entry points gated behind `window.showDirectoryPicker`/`syncDirHandle` and unreachable under the JXA harness — they're exercisable directly in `tests/cases/sync-multifile.test.js` against a minimal in-memory fake standing in for a real `FileSystemDirectoryHandle`** (the same "substitute a minimal fake, not the real DOM/FS mock" technique already established for `backupDirHandle`), covering the actual merge-then-rename behavior end to end: a stray workstream file's action item lands in memory and the file itself is renamed not deleted; a stray index file's brand-new item is picked up via `recombineSyncData()`; a file already carrying the `merged_` prefix is left alone on a later scan; the disambiguating `merged_2_`/`merged_3_` prefix kicks in when the plain target name collides; and a folder with nothing stray in it is a true no-op. `isStrayFileName()`/`strayMergedFileName()` are covered directly too, no fake needed at all. Verified end to end against real, unmocked production code in a live Chromium browser (Playwright): a fake `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` wired in as the live `syncDirHandle`, seeded with a canonical workstream file plus a stray conflict copy, confirmed both `pollFileSync()` and `linkFolder()` (the latter with `window.showDirectoryPicker` itself overridden to hand back the fake) merge the stray copy's action item in and rename the stray file, leaving only the canonical files plus the renamed copy behind.

**Testing caveat.** The pure partition/recombine layer (`slugify()`/`wsFileName()`/`buildIndexPayload()`/`buildWorkstreamPayload()`/`buildAllSyncPayloads()`/`recombineSyncData()`) has no DOM/IndexedDB/filesystem dependency at all and is fully unit-tested directly (`tests/cases/sync-multifile.test.js`) — round-trip fidelity (build then recombine reproduces the original state), correct partitioning (an item never appears in two payloads; Unassigned items always land in the index; `actionLog`/`decisionLog` correctly relocate into each workstream's own file), that both build functions are genuinely pure (re-derived fresh on every call, never cached — including a workstream's own filename immediately reflecting a rename on the very next `buildAllSyncPayloads()` call), `slugify()`'s own edge cases (blank/symbols-only names falling back to `"workstream"`, the 40-character cap never leaving a trailing dash), and that two workstreams whose names slugify to the identical string still get distinct filenames via the id suffix. `wsNameLookup()` (`readAndRecombineFolder()`'s and the two read loops' own local-then-index-overridden name lookup) has no DOM/IndexedDB dependency either and is covered the same way. Everything that actually touches `indexedDB` or the real filesystem stays outside what the JXA test harness can exercise, for the same reasons as before: the harness doesn't mock `indexedDB` (its no-op `setTimeout` would leave any real async IndexedDB request forever unresolved, hanging a test) or `setInterval` (stubbed as a no-op, so it never actually fires in a test), and every I/O-touching function is gated on a `window.showDirectoryPicker`/`syncDirHandle` check first, which the harness's fake `window` has neither — so `linkFolder()`, `initFileSync()`, `reconnectFolder()`, `doFileSyncWrite()`, `pollFileSync()`, `readJsonFile()`, and `writeJsonFile()` all safely no-op in tests. `unlinkFolder()` is the one exception (it unconditionally calls into IndexedDB) and is deliberately not exercised by the test suite for that reason, verified instead with a real Chromium instance (Playwright) — see below. What *is* tested directly, since none of it touches `syncDirHandle`/IndexedDB: `mergeData()`'s per-milestone merge and conflict detection, `stampChangedMilestones()`/`saveItem()`'s and `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()`'s `updatedAt`-stamping and tombstoning, `reportSyncConflicts()`/`syncConflictLog`, and — since `mergeData()`/`mergeReviewCycle()`/`mergeWorkstreamFields()`/`reconcileDuplicateActiveCycles()` are all plain synchronous functions with no `syncDirHandle`/IndexedDB dependency of their own — the full review-cycle/workstream field-level merge behavior too. The genuinely multi-device scenario this whole feature exists for — two independent app instances, each with their own `items`/`workstreams` globals, both reading and writing the *same* simulated folder — was verified with two real Chromium pages (Playwright), each with `window.showDirectoryPicker` overridden to hand back a fake `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` backed by `page.expose_function()` callbacks into one shared Python dict standing in for the real folder (the same "substitute a minimal fake, not the real DOM/FS mock" technique already established for `backupDirHandle` in this project's own test history, extended across two independent browser contexts so the "folder" is genuinely external to either page's own JS heap): editing an item in one workstream was confirmed to rewrite only that workstream's own file (never the index, never the other workstream's file); a concurrent edit to a *different* workstream from the second page was confirmed to merge in via `pollFileSync()` on both sides with zero data loss; and linking a folder containing only a legacy `pulse-data.json` was confirmed to migrate automatically into a correct `pulse-index.json` + per-workstream file set, leaving the original file untouched.

### Daily backups to a linked folder

**A second, independent copy of every live-sync write — dated, and written directly into a separately-chosen folder — an explicit user request, distinct from both the linked sync folder itself and the existing localStorage-based "Local backups" above.** Where the linked sync folder is a live mirror of current state (its own index+per-workstream files, always overwritten), this is a growing history of dated snapshots on real disk, the same "one per day, self-heals from a prior state" instinct as `maybeSnapshotBackup()`, just written to the filesystem instead of `localStorage` — and, unlike that 7-day-capped rolling window, kept forever: nothing here ever deletes an old dated file. **Files land directly in the chosen folder, not inside a nested `Backup` subfolder** — an earlier version created one; removed after a user correction, since the user picking a folder specifically for this purpose already communicates the intent, and a subfolder they didn't ask for just adds a level of nesting to dig through.

**Getting a folder handle needed its own, separate permission grant.** Even now that the main sync target is itself a folder (`syncDirHandle` — see "File System Access API sync" above), the two are deliberately independent locations picked separately, not derived from one another — a user might well want dated archival copies somewhere other than the live sync folder itself. `chooseBackupFolder()` is that flow: it stores the result in a second module-level handle, `backupDirHandle`, deliberately independent of `syncDirHandle` — unlinking the main sync folder doesn't clear it, so relinking one later resumes backups into the already-chosen folder with no need to pick it again. `dir.isSameEntry(syncDirHandle)`, when supported, is used as a soft sanity check right after picking — it tells us whether the chosen backup folder is literally the same one already linked for live sync, so picking the same folder by mistake surfaces as a toast rather than silently mixing the two file sets in one place; a match doesn't block anything, since a user might deliberately want that.

**Both `showDirectoryPicker()` calls (`linkFolder()`'s and `chooseBackupFolder()`'s own) pass a stable `id` option — a later, explicit user ask ("would it be possible to open the file system folder for the provided link to ask for access?"), answered honestly: no, the File System Access API has no way to jump the native picker to an arbitrary path or URL at all — a deliberate browser security boundary, real filesystem paths are never exposed to JS — but a stable `id` (`'pulse-sync-folder'`/`'pulse-backup-folder'`, deliberately two different ids so each remembers its own location independently, the same independence `backupDirHandle`/`syncDirHandle` already have from each other) is the one real, supported hint the API does offer: Chrome/Edge remembers which folder was last picked under a given `id` and reopens the native picker there next time, rather than at its own default location — so re-choosing a folder (e.g. after unlinking) starts back at the one actually in use, a OneDrive/SharePoint-synced folder included, instead of Downloads/Desktop. Verified live by stubbing `window.showDirectoryPicker` and confirming each function calls it with its own correct `id`.

`backupDirHandle` persists across reloads the same way `syncDirHandle` does — the same tiny IndexedDB wrapper (`_fsIdb`/`_fsGet`/`_fsPut`/`_fsDel`, db `pulse-fs`) takes an optional `key` parameter (defaulting to the linked-sync-folder slot, so every pre-existing call site is unaffected) and stores the backup folder handle under a second key, `_FS.backupKey`, in the same object store. `initFileSync()` restores it in its own isolated `try`/`catch` block, run before (and independent of) the rest of the function's own `syncDirHandle` restoration, so a problem reconnecting one handle can never trip the other's error handling; restoration only uses `queryPermission` (never `requestPermission`), the same background-safe convention `pollFileSync()` already follows — a lapsed backup-folder permission just means backups silently stop rather than popping an unprompted OS dialog.

**`writeBackupCopy(data)` is called from `doFileSyncWrite()`, right after the primary write to the linked sync folder succeeds** — the same recombined flat blob just written (post-merge if one happened), so the backup and the linked folder always agree on content. It's deliberately best-effort and silent on any failure: by the time it runs, the primary write has already succeeded, so a backup-side problem (permission lapsed, folder deleted, disk full) must never surface as an error over a save that otherwise worked fine. Gated on `lastBackupWrittenDate !== todayStr()` — the first save of each calendar day writes that day's file; every other save that same day is a no-op here, mirroring `maybeSnapshotBackup()`'s own "one snapshot per day" rule.

**It checks whether today's file already exists on disk before writing it, rather than unconditionally overwriting — an explicit user request.** `lastBackupWrittenDate` alone only guards against *this one session* writing more than once a day: it's plain in-memory state, never persisted, so a page reload — or, more importantly, a genuinely different session/user pointed at the same shared/synced folder — starts with it back at `null` and would otherwise go ahead and write again regardless of what's already there. The original version called `backupDirHandle.getFileHandle('pulse-backup-<date>.json', {create:true})` unconditionally, which always succeeds (handing back a handle to the existing file if there is one, or creating a fresh one if not) and proceeds to overwrite either way — so two sessions saving on the same day would silently clobber whichever wrote last, with no error, no merge, and no way for either side to know it happened (the same "best-effort and silent" trait that protects a genuine failure from surfacing also meant a *collision* looked identical to success). Fixed by checking first: a plain `getFileHandle(name)` with no `{create:true}` throws if the file is missing, which is now used purely as an existence probe before ever calling the `{create:true}` version. Finding it already there is treated the same as this session having written it itself — `lastBackupWrittenDate` is set either way, so the rest of the day's saves skip straight past the (cheap, but not free) existence check too, same as before. This narrows, but can't fully close, the race between two sessions saving at nearly the same instant — the File System Access API has no atomic "create only if missing" primitive to check-and-write in a single step — but it turns "always overwrites" into "only the very first writer each day wins," which is what was asked for.

Because backups are wired in as a side effect of `doFileSyncWrite()`, they only ever happen while the main sync folder is actively linked and syncing — unlinking it stops them too (silently, same as any other consequence of `queueFileSyncWrite()`'s own `if (!syncDirHandle) return` guard), even though `backupDirHandle` itself stays remembered for whenever a folder is linked again.

**Day-to-day UI is entirely in the topbar, not a modal** — `#backupSyncIndicator`, sitting right next to `#fileSyncIndicator`, an explicit user request for the same at-a-glance visibility the linked sync folder itself already has. (An earlier version put a matching row inside `fileSyncModalBg`, `#fileSyncBackupSection`/`fileSyncBackupSectionHtml()` — removed once this indicator made it redundant; the modal went back to just its own Link/Create/Reconnect options (and, later, the 2-option Choose-folder/Reconnect shape it has now — see "File System Access API sync" above), its original scope — see "Launch-time onboarding" below for the separate, later modal that *did* eventually come back, for a different reason.) `updateBackupSyncUI()` is `updateFileSyncUI()`'s own counterpart, same `fadeSwapHTML()`-driven crossfade and the identical `.sync-linked`-span-plus-unlink-button markup for the "on" state, but with only two states rather than four: hidden outright when `window.showDirectoryPicker` isn't supported (mirroring `updateFileSyncUI()`'s own check), a plain "Backups off" button when `backupDirHandle` is unset, or the chosen folder's name plus an unlink button once it's set. Unlike `fileSyncIndicator`'s own "off" state (which opens a modal offering ways to link a folder), the topbar's own "Backups off" button calls `chooseBackupFolder()` directly, not a modal — there's only ever one action to reach this way, so routing through one with nothing else in it would just be an extra click. There's also no third "permission lapsed" state the way the linked sync folder's own indicator has (its amber "Reconnect" button) — a lapsed backup-folder grant has no dedicated reconnect flow at all (see `writeBackupCopy()`'s own comment above); it just silently stops backing up until `chooseBackupFolder()` is run again. Called from `chooseBackupFolder()`/`unlinkBackupFolder()` (so the badge updates the moment the folder changes) and twice from `initFileSync()` — once immediately (so the badge doesn't sit blank while IndexedDB restoration is still in flight) and once after `backupDirHandle` is actually restored.

**Unlike linking the main file, daily backups are Editor+ only — a later, explicit user request ("offer backup link only for editor and admin role. disable it (grey) for all other roles") reversing this feature's own earlier "no role gate at all" design.** `chooseBackupFolder()`/`unlinkBackupFolder()` both now start with `if (!requireRole('editor')) return;`, mirroring the defense-in-depth pattern every other role-gated mutation in this app uses. `updateBackupSyncUI()` computes `canManage = hasRole('editor')` once and threads it into both of `#backupSyncIndicator`'s states: below Editor, the "Backups off" button (or, once a folder's chosen, the unlink button next to the folder name) still renders — the same "always a real, visible control, just inert" convention every other role-gated control in this app follows (the Not Applicable toggle, the review confirm toggle) — but with `disabled` set (greyed via the existing `.btn:disabled` rule) and a title explaining the role requirement, rather than hidden outright. The linked folder's own name, once one is chosen, still shows as plain informational text regardless of role — only the button that would actually change something is gated. **A later, separate explicit user request ("make backup linking mandatory for editor and admin role") went further still: for Editor and Admin specifically, choosing a backup folder is no longer optional at all** — see "Daily backups are mandatory for Editor and Admin" below for the mechanism. This doesn't change who's gated *out* (still below Editor, exactly as above) — only what's required of the roles gated *in*.

**Launch-time onboarding is a second, separate modal (`backupFolderModalBg`), shown right after the file-sync onboarding modal closes — an explicit user request ("handle file link and backup link in 2 modals after each other... ask when launching the app").** This is deliberately *not* folded back into `fileSyncModalBg` (which the paragraph above already explains was tried once and reverted) — it's a distinct modal for a distinct moment: only at launch, only once, chained specifically off the file-sync modal's own close rather than living as a permanent option inside it. `openFileSyncModal(reconnectHandle, isLaunch)` gained a second parameter for this: `isLaunch` is `true` only at the three call sites inside `initFileSync()`/`maybeShowPendingFileSyncPrompt()` that represent genuine startup onboarding, and omitted (falsy) at the topbar indicator's own manual "Link folder"/reconnect calls — `closeFileSyncModal()` reads the resulting `fileSyncModalIsLaunchFlow` flag to decide whether to call `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` right after removing its own `open` class, so a modal reopened manually later in the session never unexpectedly cascades into a second one on close. This fires regardless of *how* the file-sync modal was closed (there's only one way now — see "Linking is mandatory" above — but the chaining itself doesn't care which of the two options was used to get there) — the same "doesn't gate on what was chosen" shape the folder-link step itself already has relative to the mandatory role picker before it. `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` itself is a no-op if daily backups aren't supported at all, a folder's already chosen (restored from IndexedDB earlier in the same `initFileSync()` call), or — a later, explicit user request matching `chooseBackupFolder()`'s own Editor+ gate above — the current role can't act on it anyway (`!hasRole('editor')`): prompting a Visitor/Reviewer to pick a folder they have no permission to actually set would just be a dead end. Safe to check role here without its own race to worry about — by the time this can ever fire, a role is always already chosen, since every file-sync prompt (this one included) is itself deferred via `pendingFileSyncPrompt` until the mandatory role picker has been resolved. A returning user who already has both linked (or who can't manage backups at all) never sees either onboarding modal again. **The one case with no file-sync modal to chain off of** — the sync folder is already present and fully working (`initFileSync()`'s `perm === 'granted'` branch, which reads and syncs the folder without ever opening a modal) — calls `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` directly instead, right after that branch (guarded on neither `pendingFileSyncPrompt` being set nor the file-sync modal currently being open, so it can't double-fire against the two branches that *do* open one).

### Daily backups are mandatory for Editor and Admin

**A later, separate explicit user request ("make backup linking mandatory for editor and admin role") made `backupFolderModalBg` undismissable — no "Not now" button, and excluded from both the global Escape handler and the generic backdrop-click-to-close wiring, the exact same "must complete before closing" treatment `fileSyncModalBg` got for the main sync folder (see "Linking is mandatory" under "File System Access API sync" above).** This needed no new role-check inside the modal at all: `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` already only ever opens it for Editor+ in the first place (`!hasRole('editor')` bails out before it gets there — see that function's own comment) — so the modal is structurally never shown to a Visitor/Reviewer to begin with, and making it undismissable automatically makes it mandatory for exactly the two roles the request named, with zero additional gating logic. `chooseBackupFolder()`'s own eager `closeBackupFolderModal()` (previously called from the modal's own `onclick`, *before* the picker even ran) had to move for the identical reason `linkFolder()`'s own eager close did: cancelling the native folder picker (`AbortError`) used to leave the modal silently closed with nothing chosen, an accidental escape hatch that would have quietly undermined the mandate. `closeBackupFolderModal()` is now called from *inside* `chooseBackupFolder()`, only once `backupDirHandle` is actually set — a cancelled or failed attempt leaves the modal open, with no extra handling needed since nothing ever closed it in the first place.

**Unlinking is still allowed — switching to a different folder, or undoing a mistake — but `unlinkBackupFolder()` now immediately reopens the same mandatory modal afterward**, the identical "allowed to change, not allowed to end up unconfigured" stance `unlinkFolder()` already takes for the main sync folder. Both functions stay `requireRole('editor')`-guarded, so the reopen can only ever happen for a role the mandate actually applies to.

**Mid-session role switches are covered too, not just the launch-time chain.** The launch-time sequence (`closeFileSyncModal()` → `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()`, or `initFileSync()`'s own direct call when a folder was already linked and working) only ever fires once, right after linking — it says nothing about a user who starts the session as Visitor and switches to Editor an hour later. `closeRoleModal()` — already the one place `maybeShowPendingFileSyncPrompt()` resolves a *deferred* file-sync prompt once a role exists — gained a second, similarly-guarded call to `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` right after it: `if (syncDirHandle && !pendingFileSyncPrompt && !document.getElementById('fileSyncModalBg').classList.contains('open')) maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding();`. The three-part guard mirrors `initFileSync()`'s own "folder already linked and working" call site exactly, and matters for a concrete reason: at the very first launch, `syncDirHandle` is still `null` at the moment `closeRoleModal()` first runs (nothing has been linked yet), so this correctly no-ops there and leaves the launch-time chain as the *only* thing that opens the backup-folder modal — without that check, both paths could race to open two different mandatory modals stacked on each other before the folder-link mandate had even been satisfied. `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` itself is idempotent (a no-op once a folder's chosen or the role isn't Editor+), so calling it unconditionally on every `closeRoleModal()` — not just an actual role change — is harmless.

**Testing caveat, in addition to the ones already documented for this whole area**: `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` itself still always no-ops under the JXA harness (`window.showDirectoryPicker` is never provided), so `closeRoleModal()`'s own new *outer* guard — the part that decides whether to call it at all — is the one piece of this feature that's actually reachable in tests, via a temporary spy (`maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding` reassigned to a counting stub, restored in a `finally`): confirmed not to fire before a file is linked (the race-avoidance case), fires once a file is linked and nothing else is pending, and doesn't fire while the file-sync modal is itself still open.

**Testing caveat, in addition to the one above**: `chooseBackupFolder()`/`unlinkBackupFolder()` and the backup-restoration half of `initFileSync()` all touch IndexedDB or `window.showDirectoryPicker` and so are gated the same way the rest of file sync is — safely un-reachable under the JXA harness's fake `window`. `unlinkBackupFolder()` is a second instance of `unlinkFolder()`'s own documented exception (it unconditionally calls into IndexedDB) and isn't exercised for the same reason. What *is* tested directly, since none of it touches either: `backupFileName()` (the pure filename builder), `writeBackupCopy()`'s two early-return guards (no folder chosen; today's copy already written) — both fire before ever calling a method on `backupDirHandle`, so they're safe to exercise even with a plain fake object standing in for a real `FileSystemDirectoryHandle` — and `updateBackupSyncUI()` hiding `#backupSyncIndicator` outright, since the harness's fake `window` has no `showDirectoryPicker` either. `writeBackupCopy()`'s own existence-check logic (the paragraph above) goes further than either of those guards and *does* call methods on `backupDirHandle`, so it's outside what the JXA harness can exercise at all — verified instead with a real Chromium instance (Playwright), substituting a plain JS object for `backupDirHandle` whose `getFileHandle()` either always resolves (simulating the file already existing) or only resolves when called with `{create:true}` (simulating it not existing yet), confirming the write is skipped in the first case and happens exactly once in the second. **`chooseBackupFolder()`/`unlinkBackupFolder()`'s own `requireRole('editor')` guards are the one part of this whole feature that *is* directly testable, though** — both run before either function ever touches `window.showDirectoryPicker`, so a below-Editor role returning early (leaving `backupDirHandle` untouched) and an Editor+ role falling through to the harness's own missing-API toast are both exercised for real, unlike everything else in this section.

## Roles (RBAC)

**This is a soft, UI-level permission ladder, not real access control.** Pulse has no server, no accounts, and no auth of any kind — every byte of app state lives in this browser's `localStorage` (or a linked file the browser itself has read/write access to), so anyone with the browser's dev tools, or direct access to the data file, can already see and change everything regardless of which role is showing. The feature exists purely to guide day-to-day workflow on a shared device or a small team (keep a Visitor from *accidentally* editing a scope item) — not to keep anyone out on purpose. This was an explicit, deliberate tradeoff confirmed with the user up front, not an oversight.

**`ROLES` is an increasing ladder — `['visitor', 'reviewer', 'editor', 'admin']` — never a set of independent, non-overlapping permissions.** Each role can do everything the one before it can, plus more:
- **Visitor** — view Planning, Review, and Dashboard; no edits anywhere.
- **Reviewer** — + start/confirm/complete/cancel review cycles, attach/edit/remove meeting minutes, manage the Action Log, and — scoped specifically to Review mode itself, see "Review-mode field edits are Reviewer+, Planning stays Editor+" below — correct a milestone's Due/Actual dates and status, and an item's own IT/Business/Budget tags.
- **Editor** — + create/edit/delete workstreams, scope items, milestones, and categories.
- **Admin** — + import/restore data.

**Linking the main synced folder (linking, unlinking, reconnecting) is deliberately not part of this ladder at all — it's available at, and now mandatory for, every role including Visitor (an explicit user request).** Unlike the ladder's other tiers, there's no "who's meant to own this" framing here: connecting the shared data folder is treated as closer to a required workflow/device setting than a data-mutating action a role should gate — see "Linking is mandatory" under "File System Access API sync" above for the undismissable-modal mechanism this requirement is built on.

**Daily backups to a separate folder are the one part of file sync that *does* sit on the ladder — Editor+ only, a later, explicit user request** ("offer backup link only for editor and admin role. disable it (grey) for all other roles") carving out an exception to the paragraph above. `chooseBackupFolder()`/`unlinkBackupFolder()` are `requireRole('editor')`-guarded, and `#backupSyncIndicator`'s own button renders `disabled` below that tier — see "Daily backups to a linked folder" above for the full mechanism. Unlike the main file link, backups stay entirely optional at every role that can reach them at all; this is a *who can toggle it* gate, not a *must be on* one.

Reviewer sits *below* Editor on the ladder — a user-requested swap from the original order (`['visitor', 'editor', 'reviewer', 'admin']`), since a Reviewer running review cycles shouldn't automatically also be able to restructure the plan (add/delete workstreams, scope items, milestones) the way the original ordering implied. Because every `requireRole('editor')`/`requireRole('reviewer')` guard and every `hasRole('editor')`/`hasRole('reviewer')` UI-gating check throughout this file reference role *names*, not positions, reordering `ROLES` (plus rewording each `ROLE_META[...].desc`'s "Everything a `<X>` can do, plus..." chain to match) was the entire fix — every call site automatically picked up the new hierarchy with no changes of its own. This is the actual payoff of `roleLevel(r) { return ROLES.indexOf(r); }` being the *only* thing that knows the ladder's order: the ladder's shape lives in exactly one place.

**Review-mode field edits are Reviewer+, Planning stays Editor+ — a later, explicit user request** ("a reviewer should be able to change milestone dates (due and actual), milestone status and, IT, Business and Budget. But only in review view/process, not in normal planning view"), **extended to the Not Applicable toggle too by a follow-up request** ("reviewer should also be able to toggle n/a for milestons in review mode only"). Before this, a milestone's Due/Actual dates and status, its own Not Applicable toggle, and an item's own IT/Business/Budget tags were all plain Editor+ everywhere, no matter which mode was showing them — including a milestone's own Due date, which was actually *locked read-only during a review for every role, Editor included* ("Due is part of the plan"), on the reasoning that a review is a confirmation pass, not a planning edit. The request reverses that stance specifically for a Reviewer, and specifically while actually inside Review mode: `canEditReviewFields()` (next to `hasRole()`/`requireRole()` near `ROLES` above) is `hasRole('editor') || (mode === 'review' && hasRole('reviewer'))` — Editor+ unconditionally (unchanged, and this is also what un-locks Due for an Editor during a review for the first time, since Editor already passes `hasRole('reviewer')` on the ladder), or Reviewer+ but only while the global `mode` is genuinely `'review'`. `requireReviewFieldRole()` is `requireRole()`'s counterpart for this same check — toast-and-`false` on failure, worded as "This can only be edited from Review mode" specifically for a Reviewer being blocked in Planning (a plain "Editor role or higher required" would be actively wrong for that case), falling back to the ordinary Editor-role message for anyone below Reviewer.

Checking `mode === 'review'` directly (rather than, say, trusting that a `reviewCycle` argument was passed into whichever row is rendering the control) is the actual defense-in-depth boundary here: `itemRowHtml()`/`milestoneRowsHtml()` only ever receive a `reviewCycle` from `renderReview()`, which only ever runs while `mode` is already `'review'`, so the two agree in practice — but gating the real mutation on `mode` itself, not on an argument the render layer happened to pass, is what makes the five mutating functions below safe to call directly (e.g. from the console) regardless of how they were reached, the same "never trust the render-time check alone" stance `requireRole()` already takes.

Five call sites, each swapping a bare `requireRole('editor')` for `requireReviewFieldRole()`: `cycleMilestoneStatus()`, `cycleItemAttr()`, `updateMilestoneDateField()` (which handles both a milestone's `dueDate` and `actualDate` — one function, one guard, same as before), and `toggleMilestoneNotApplicable()`. **`updateItemDateField()` — an item's own manual Start/Due, only ever editable at all when it has zero milestones — is deliberately untouched, still plain Editor+ regardless of mode**: the request named milestone dates specifically, not an item's own plan-date range, and `planDatesHtml` already forces that field to a read-only computed span the instant `reviewCycle` is passed regardless of milestone count, so it was never reachable during a review to begin with — nothing to elevate. **The item modal's own Not Applicable toggle (`toggleEditingMilestoneNotApplicable()`, inside `renderMilestonesEditor()`) is also untouched, still plain Editor+ regardless of mode** — the modal is a completely separate editing surface from the review row, and is never reachable from a review row in the first place (`itemRowHtml()` hides the row's own Edit action outright whenever `reviewCycle` is passed, for every role — see "Review mode" below), so there was nothing to elevate there either.

On the render side, `itemRowHtml()` gained `reviewFieldsEditable` (`canEditReviewFields()`) alongside its existing `editable` — the tags-only flag. `milestoneRowsHtml()` gained the equivalent `fieldsEditable` (`canEditReviewFields()`) alongside its own existing `editable` — used for `dueEditable`/`actualEditable`/the status badge's clickable-vs-plain-span split, and (once the follow-up request above landed) `naEditable` too, which originally stayed pinned to the plain `editable` (Editor+ only) when this feature first shipped — `editable` itself is untouched, still plain Editor+, and still what gates the row's other Editor-only actions (Edit/Delete/drag on the item's own row). Due's own render branch changed from an unconditional `reviewCycle ? computed-readonly : editable-pill` to `(reviewCycle && !dueEditable) ? computed-readonly : (ghost-or-pill, using dueEditable)` — when `dueEditable` is true (Reviewer+ inside Review, or Editor+ anywhere), Due renders through the exact same ghost-reveal/pill machinery Planning already uses (`revealDueDate()`/`cancelRevealDueDate()`, the picker-commit wiring, the incomplete-edit-snaps-back-on-blur fix — see "Every inline date field renders as a 'pill'" above), not a bespoke review-only editing path; when it's false, the original locked `<span class="item-dates-computed">` renders, unchanged. Actual's own render branch needed no structural change at all — it was never forced read-only during a review in the first place, just gated on `editable`, which is now `fieldsEditable` instead — so it silently picked up the same Reviewer-in-Review elevation for free. The Not Applicable toggle likewise needed no structural change — `naHtml`'s own `naEditable ? button : span` branch was already exactly the right shape, it just needed `naEditable` itself repointed from `editable` to `fieldsEditable`.

`roleLevel(r)` is just `ROLES.indexOf(r)`; `hasRole(minRole)` is `roleLevel(userRole) >= roleLevel(minRole)` — true for the given role *or anything above it* on the ladder, which is what makes it a ladder rather than a flag check. `requireRole(minRole)` is the defense-in-depth guard called as the very first line of every state-mutating function gated by role (`if (!requireRole('editor')) return;` etc.) — it shows a toast (`"<Role> role or higher required for this action"`) and returns `false` rather than throwing, so a stray call (e.g. typed into the console) fails quietly instead of corrupting anything. **This guard is the second layer, not the primary one** — the real, user-facing gate is that the render functions never show the control in the first place (see "UI gating by role" below); `requireRole()` exists so a control that's merely disabled-but-still-clickable (a `disabled` input) or reachable by some path the UI pass missed still fails safely rather than silently mutating state.

**`userRole` is `null` until a role is chosen, and its persistence is deliberately isolated from every other piece of state.** Unlike `mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme`/`filterWorkstreamId` (which live inside the same blob `save()` writes to `localStorage['pulse-v1']`), `userRole` lives under its own key, `ROLE_STORAGE_KEY = 'pulse-role-v1'` (`loadRole()`/`saveRole()`), and is never folded into `save()`'s data object at all. `mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme`/`filterWorkstreamId` themselves are *not* written out to the linked sync file at all any more either (see `buildIndexPayload()`'s own comment under "File System Access API sync" above) — but that's a separate, later fix made once it turned out nothing ever read them back; `userRole`'s own isolation here predates that and was never about the sync file specifically, it's about `localStorage['pulse-role-v1']` never being folded into the same blob `save()`'s data object represents in the first place. This was an explicit requirement: a role chosen on one device must never leak into the shared/synced data file and silently change what role another device's picker defaults to, or what a teammate sees when they open that same file. `loadRole()` validates against `ROLES.includes(raw)` the same defensive way `load()` validates `colorScheme` against `THEME_SCHEMES` — an invalid/missing stored value just leaves `userRole` at `null` (i.e., the picker gate fires) rather than crashing.

**The role picker (`#roleModalBg`, `renderRoleModal()`/`openRoleModal()`/`closeRoleModal()`/`setUserRole(role)`) doubles as two different things depending on whether a role has ever been chosen on this browser.** With `userRole` still `null` (a brand-new browser, or `localStorage` cleared), it's a mandatory, undismissable first-launch gate: `closeRoleModal()` itself checks `if (!userRole) return;` before closing anything, and both the backdrop-click handler and the global Escape handler call `closeRoleModal()` the same way every other modal's does — so both just no-op rather than closing the app's very first, load-bearing choice. `renderRoleModal()` also disables/greys out the modal's own "Done" button (`#roleModalDoneBtn`) while `userRole` is still null, for the same reason. The init sequence (`load(); loadRole(); ...; render(); initFileSync(); if (!userRole) openRoleModal();`) deliberately calls `openRoleModal()` *last* — after everything else has already rendered underneath — so the very first thing a brand-new user sees is a role prompt sitting on top of a normal (if role-gated-down-to-Visitor-looking) UI, not a blank/broken page. Once a role exists, the exact same modal is reachable anytime via the topbar's shield icon (`#roleBtn`, `onclick="openRoleModal()"`) — an explicit, confirmed design decision that switching roles should need no confirmation or reload, since this is a workflow aid, not a login.

**`updateRoleBtn()` keeps that topbar icon itself in sync with the current role — a user-reported bug, since it used to be a single hardcoded `fa-user-shield` glyph regardless of which role was active, so switching roles visibly changed nothing there.** It sets `#roleBtnIcon`'s class to `ROLE_META[userRole].icon` (falling back to `fa-user-shield` if `userRole` is still `null`, pre-first-pick) and `#roleBtn`'s `title` to `"Role: <Label> (click to switch)"`. Called from `render()` itself, not just from `setUserRole()`, so it's also correct immediately after the very first pick, after a page reload, and after any other state change that happens to call `render()`.

**`initFileSync()`'s auto-onboarding/reconnect prompt is deferred via `pendingFileSyncPrompt` until a role actually exists, to avoid a first-launch race with the mandatory role picker.** All three of `initFileSync()`'s `openFileSyncModal()` call sites (no previously-linked folder at all; a previously-linked folder whose permission has lapsed and needs reconnecting; a hard error reading the previously-linked folder) used to call `openFileSyncModal()` immediately — including on a *brand-new* browser where `userRole` is still `null`. Since `initFileSync()` runs (and its internal `await`s resolve) concurrently with the init sequence's own `if (!userRole) openRoleModal()` call right after it, this raced the mandatory, undismissable role-picker gate: the file-sync modal could get visually buried behind the role modal, appearing before the user had picked *any* role yet. Fixed by checking `!userRole` at each of the three call sites: instead of opening immediately, they stash what they wanted to show in `pendingFileSyncPrompt` (`'link'`, or the handle needing reconnect/re-read) and return. `closeRoleModal()` calls `maybeShowPendingFileSyncPrompt()` right after removing the modal's `open` class, which opens the parked prompt now that a role exists — any role, since file sync isn't gated by role at all (see below). On a **returning** browser (`userRole` already set from `loadRole()` before `initFileSync()` ever runs), none of this deferral triggers — `!userRole` is false immediately, so the modal opens exactly as it always did, with no race to avoid.

**`openFileSyncModal(reconnectHandle)` has no role-gated content — file sync is available at, and now mandatory for, every role, including Visitor (an explicit user request).** It previously swapped its entire body below Admin (hiding `#fileSyncOptions` behind a plain "Admin required" notice), matching `reconnectFolder()`/`linkFolder()`/`unlinkFolder()` each being `requireRole('admin')`-guarded themselves — all three of those guards, and the modal's admin-notice branch, were removed together. The modal now always shows the real Reconnect/Choose-a-folder options regardless of who's looking, and `#fileSyncIndicator` in the topbar is likewise no longer hidden below Admin (`updateFileSyncUI()`'s only remaining hide condition is lack of picker support, i.e. not Chrome/Edge). A later request made this modal mandatory on top of being role-agnostic — see "Linking is mandatory" under "File System Access API sync" above.

**UI gating by role** — the render-time half of the system, which is what actually determines what a given role sees, not just what it can successfully submit:
- **Sidebar** (`renderSidebar()`): the "+ new workstream" button (`#addWsBtn`), each row's edit pencil (`.ws-row-edit`), and its drag handle (`.ws-row-drag`, see "Manual item order and drag-and-drop reordering" above) all only render at Editor+.
- **Planning status board** (`itemRowHtml()`/`milestoneRowsHtml()`): the Unassigned section's own "+ Add item" affordance (`unassignedSectionHtml()` — see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below), the item row's Edit/Delete/drag-handle actions, the IT/Business/Budget tag badges, and every inline date pill (item Plan dates, milestone Due/Actual) are all editable only at Editor+ — below that, tags/status badges render as plain, non-interactive `<span>`s (not omitted — see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above for why an omitted cell would break alignment) and date pills render with `disabled` rather than an `onblur` handler. A milestone's status badge (`cycleMilestoneStatus`) follows the same span-vs-button split. The item-level and milestone-level review confirm toggles (`grid-column:11`) render as an inert `<span>` — still showing the real confirmed/unconfirmed icon, just not clickable — below Reviewer, rather than Editor: seeing review progress while planning is fine, changing it isn't.
- **Item/Workstream/Category modals** stay reachable to *view* regardless of role — `openItemModal()`/`openWorkstreamModal()`/`openCategoryModal()` are never role-gated themselves, since reading an item's full detail is exactly what "view only" should still allow. What role gates is editability: every input/select/textarea in the modal gets `.disabled = !editable`, the Save/Add-milestone/Add-category-milestone buttons are hidden outright below that, and the Delete button is hidden below it *and* when there's nothing to delete yet (both conditions already existed independently — a new `it`-less modal never showed Delete either). `editable` is `hasRole('editor')` for a workstream/category/item. `renderMilestonesEditor()`/`renderCategoryMilestonesEditor()` apply the same `disabled`/hidden-button treatment to each dynamically-rendered row.
- **Review mode**: "Start review cycle" is replaced with a plain explanatory line below Reviewer; the "Complete review"/"Cancel review" button row is omitted entirely (not just disabled) below Reviewer.
- **Meeting Minutes modal**: reachable to view (past minutes are worth reading regardless of role) but every field (`disabled`) and every action button (Auto-fill/Clear all fields/Add action item/Save/Remove, plus each Action Item row's own inputs and its per-row Remove button) is hidden/disabled below Reviewer.
- **Action Log** (`actionLogRowHtml()`): the Delete button and the complete/reopen toggle both fall back to the same "real empty span" / "inert icon-only span" pattern used elsewhere, below Reviewer.
- **Admin mode / Categories** (`renderAdmin()`): the "+ Category" button and each row's Edit pencil only render at Editor+ (categories are Editor-tier, same as scope items/workstreams — there's nothing Admin-specific about a milestone template).

**The category row's Edit pencil was rendering but permanently invisible — an Editor/Admin genuinely could not edit a category once created, despite the gating above being correct.** Root cause: `renderAdmin()`'s category row wraps its Edit pencil in `<span class="item-actions">` (line ~4718), reusing the same class the item row uses for its own Edit/Delete/drag icons. But `.item-actions`'s hover-reveal (`visibility: hidden` at rest, flipped to `visible`) was only ever wired up for `.item-row:hover .item-actions` — there was no equivalent `.category-row:hover` rule, so on a category row the button sat in the DOM, fully functional, at `visibility: hidden` with no hover state that ever cleared it. Fixed with one added rule, `.category-row:hover .item-actions { visibility: visible; }`, right next to `.item-row`'s own — same reasoning as `.item-row`'s (visibility, not display, so the row never reflows on hover).
- **Data menu**: the Import and Backups dropdown items (`#dataMenuImportItem`/`#dataMenuBackupsItem`) are hidden below Admin — set directly in `render()`, since they're static markup rather than something a mode-specific render function owns. Export is deliberately left available to every role: it's a read-only download, not a mutation, so there's nothing to gate.

**Role passwords add an optional per-role password gate on top of the picker — an explicit user request ("add a password mechanism for all roles except Viewer"), still deliberately just a *soft* deterrent, not real access control.** `programme.rolePasswords` (`{ reviewer: {salt,hash}|null, editor: {salt,hash}|null, admin: {salt,hash}|null }`, backfilled by `normalizeData()` — Visitor has no entry at all, since it's never gated) is part of `programme` itself, which — unlike `userRole` — *is* written out to the shared/synced data file (see `doFileSyncWrite()`'s payload above): an Admin-set password is meant to apply for every teammate opening that same file, not just this one browser, so it has to live in the synced blob rather than `ROLE_STORAGE_KEY`. `null` (the default) means "no password set for this role yet" — picking it still switches immediately, exactly like before this existed, so a brand-new programme has no bootstrapping deadlock (an Admin has to be able to freely reach Admin *before* setting any passwords, including the Admin one). A follow-up request ("can the password be stored as a hash in the file, not plain text?") is why it's `{salt, hash}` rather than the plain string an earlier version of this used: real, meaningful protection for the password's own *storage* (opening the shared file or `localStorage` no longer just hands it over), but **not** a different category of protection overall — the check itself (`verifyRolePassword()`, below) still runs in this same page's own client-side JS, so anyone technical enough to read it, or to just call `setUserRole('admin')` from the console, bypasses the whole thing regardless of how the password is stored. Both the in-app copy (`#roleModalBg`'s own paragraph) and this doc say so explicitly, since a password specifically invites the assumption that it's real access control more than the rest of RBAC already does.

**A role password change made on one device used to never reach a second device that already had any real data of its own — a user-reported bug, and the reason `programme` now merges field-by-field (`mergeProgramme()`, see "Merge and last-write-wins" below) instead of not merging at all.** Before that fix, `mergeData()` never touched `data.programme` in any form — the only place an incoming `programme` (rolePasswords included) ever got applied was `replaceFromFileData()`, gated by `isFreshLocalState()`, which only ever fires for a genuinely untouched, brand-new install. So setting a role password from one browser, then opening the same linked folder from a second device that already had any real workstreams/items of its own, would never pick up that password change through any of the ordinary link/reconnect/poll/write-time merge paths — it stayed permanently stuck at whatever the second device's own copy already had (`null`, most likely), no matter how many times it synced. `rolePasswordsUpdatedAt` (a companion per-role timestamp map, alongside `rolePasswords` itself) is what makes the fix genuinely per-role rather than "whichever device touched *anything* about `programme` most recently wins the whole object" — see `mergeProgramme()`'s own comment for why.

**Hashing (`hashRolePassword()`/`verifyRolePassword()`/`sha256Hex()`/`randomSaltHex()`) uses the real Web Crypto API (`crypto.subtle.digest('SHA-256', ...)`, `crypto.getRandomValues()`), not a hand-rolled algorithm.** `hashRolePassword(password)` generates a fresh random 16-byte salt (hex-encoded) *every time a password is set or changed* and hashes `` `${salt}:${password}` ``, returning `{salt, hash}`; `verifyRolePassword(password, stored)` re-hashes the candidate with `stored.salt` and compares the hex digest to `stored.hash` (`false` if `stored` is falsy, rather than throwing). The fresh-salt-per-password choice specifically matters here: without it, two roles given the *same* password would land on identical hashes, visibly revealing the match to anyone comparing `programme.rolePasswords` even without ever knowing either password. `crypto.subtle` only exists in a secure context (https, or a `localhost`/`127.0.0.1` origin — exactly how `start-pulse.command` already serves this app, but not a plain `file://` open); `hasSecureCrypto()` is checked up front by both `submitRolePassword()` and `saveRolePasswords()`, which show a plain-language error/toast instead of letting a missing `crypto.subtle` throw from deep inside an `await` chain. Both of those functions are themselves `async` now (hashing is inherently asynchronous), called straight from their `onclick`/`onkeydown` handlers same as any other handler — nothing about the surrounding UI needed to change to accommodate that.

`pickRole(role)` is what the role modal's tiles now call instead of `setUserRole(role)` directly: for `'visitor'`, or any role whose `programme.rolePasswords[role]` is still `null`, it just calls `setUserRole(role)` immediately, same as before. Otherwise it doesn't switch yet — it sets `pendingRolePick`, hides `#roleOptions`/`#roleModalDoneRow`, and shows `#roleModalPasswordStep` (a masked `<input type="password">` and Cancel/Unlock buttons) in their place, all still inside the same `#roleModalBg` rather than a second modal. `submitRolePassword()` awaits `verifyRolePassword()` against `programme.rolePasswords[pendingRolePick]`; a match calls `cancelRolePasswordStep()` (resetting the step UI back to the tile grid) then `setUserRole(role)`; a mismatch clears the input and shows an inline error, leaving the step open to retry. `cancelRolePasswordStep()` (also called by `openRoleModal()` itself, so a modal freshly opened never shows a stale password step left over from a previous abandoned attempt) just resets the UI with no role change. A role with a password set also renders a small lock icon next to its label in the tile grid (`renderRoleModal()`) — the affordance that a password will be asked for, rather than a surprise after clicking.

**A user-reported bypass: picking a role for free (no password set on this device *yet*) and then linking/reconnecting to an existing file that turns out to already have a real password for that same role left the already-active role completely unchallenged.** Concretely — the exact repro — start fresh (`programme.rolePasswords.admin` still `null` on a brand-new local state), pick Admin via `pickRole()`'s own free path, then link a folder whose `pulse-index.json` has a real Admin password set: `replaceFromFileData()`/`mergeProgramme()` correctly bring the file's own `rolePasswords` in, but nothing ever went back and re-checked the role already sitting active in this session against them, so the session stayed Admin throughout with no challenge ever shown.

`verifiedRolePasswordHash` (declared next to `pendingRolePick`) is the exact `hash` string most recently *verified* (by successfully typing the real password into `pickRole()`'s own password step) for whichever role is currently active — persisted alongside `userRole` itself, in a second `localStorage` key (`ROLE_VERIFIED_HASH_KEY`, `'pulse-role-verified-v1'`), specifically so a page reload doesn't forget it and re-challenge for a password that hasn't actually changed since it was last verified. `loadRole()`/`saveRole()` both read/write it in the same call as `userRole`. `setUserRole()` unconditionally resets it to `null` (and persists that reset) on every role switch — a role reached via the free "no password set" path starts *unverified*, not implicitly trusted — and `submitRolePassword()` sets (and persists) the real hash the moment a password check actually succeeds.

`checkCurrentRolePasswordChallenge()` is the actual fix: if the currently-active `userRole` isn't Visitor/null and `programme.rolePasswords[userRole]` is now set to something that doesn't match `verifiedRolePasswordHash`, it downgrades `userRole` back to Visitor and immediately re-challenges for the *same* role via the exact same `pickRole()` password step a fresh pick would show — reusing that flow outright rather than a second, parallel one — plus a toast explaining why. Comparing by the stored hash's own *value* (not object identity, which a merge/replace always changes regardless of whether the password itself is actually different) is what makes this correctly re-trigger if the password changes again later too, not just the first time one ever appears.

Called after every point `programme` can be updated from an external source — `linkFolder()`, `reconnectFolder()`, and `initFileSync()`'s own quiet launch-time reconnect — deliberately **not** also wired into the routine background poll/write-time merges (`pollFileSync()`/`doFileSyncWrite()`'s own read-and-merge phase): those are meant to stay unobtrusive, the same toast-and-badge treatment sync conflicts already get, not a modal interrupting whatever the user happens to be mid-typing: linking/reconnecting is already a deliberate, foreground action the user just took, so popping the password step right after it is the expected continuation of that action, not a surprise interruption. Verified with two real Chromium pages (Playwright): a genuinely fresh "device" picks Admin for free, then links to a folder a different "device" already wrote a real Admin password into — confirmed the session is downgraded to Visitor, `hasRole('admin')` genuinely returns `false` until the real password is entered (not just the modal reappearing cosmetically), and typing the real password correctly reclaims Admin.

**A user-reported bug: at first launch, picking a role that this challenge then downgrades could leave the password step completely unreachable — hidden behind the mandatory backup-folder modal, which has no dismiss of its own either, leaving both stuck.** Root cause was call order, not the challenge logic itself: `linkFolder()`/`reconnectFolder()` used to call `closeFileSyncModal()` *before* `checkCurrentRolePasswordChallenge()`. `closeFileSyncModal()` immediately chains into `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` whenever this was part of the launch sequence (see "Daily backups are mandatory for Editor and Admin" below) — and at that point `userRole` was still whatever had been freely picked a moment earlier (this file's own `rolePasswords` weren't known locally yet), so `hasRole('editor')` still passed and the undismissable backup modal opened right away. `checkCurrentRolePasswordChallenge()` then ran a tick later, downgraded `userRole` to Visitor, and reopened the role modal with the password step underneath — but by then the backup modal (opened, and openable, on this exact stale-role window) was already sitting on top of it with no way to dismiss either one. Fixed by swapping the order in both `linkFolder()`'s two success branches and `reconnectFolder()`'s own: `checkCurrentRolePasswordChallenge()` now always runs *before* `closeFileSyncModal()`. If it fires, the downgrade to Visitor happens first, so the very next `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` call correctly sees `hasRole('editor') === false` and stays closed — the backup-folder mandate only actually engages once `closeRoleModal()`'s own guarded call fires later, after the real password has been entered and the role's genuinely been reclaimed (see `closeRoleModal()`'s own `syncDirHandle && !pendingFileSyncPrompt && !fileSyncModalBg.open` guard, which was already correct and needed no change). `initFileSync()`'s own quiet launch-time reconnect path already had this ordering right (its own `checkCurrentRolePasswordChallenge()` call already precedes its `maybeShowBackupFolderOnboarding()` check) — only the two foreground link/reconnect paths had it backwards. Verified with Playwright against a fake linked folder carrying a real, password-protected Admin role: reproduced the exact bug on the pre-fix code (backup modal open at the same moment as the password step, hiding it), then confirmed the fix leaves only the password step open, lets the real password reclaim Admin, and *then* correctly surfaces the backup-folder modal once the role modal's own Done button is clicked.

**`pickRole()`/`cancelRolePasswordStep()` also swap the modal's own title and hide its intro paragraph while the password step is showing — a user-reported issue: "Choose your role" plus its full paragraph explaining the whole password system stayed on screen while someone was just trying to type one password in, reading as a much bigger dialog than the single field it actually needed.** `#roleModalTitle`/`#roleModalIntro` (the `<h3>`/`<p>` at the top of the modal) and a new `#roleModalPasswordIntro` (a one-line "Enter the password to switch to `<Role>`." sitting inside `#roleModalPasswordStep` itself) replace the password step's old, redundant `#roleModalPasswordLabel` field label. `pickRole(role)` sets `#roleModalTitle`'s text to `` `${ROLE_META[role].label} password` `` and hides `#roleModalIntro`; `cancelRolePasswordStep()` restores both back to "Choose your role" / visible, so the tile grid always reopens looking like the full picker again, never mid-password-step framing.

**Setting the passwords themselves is Admin-only, both to view and to edit — a Reviewer/Editor being able to see *which* roles are currently password-protected (even without the values) would narrow down what's worth guessing against.** `rolePasswordsSectionHtml()`, appended to the end of `renderAdmin()`'s output, returns `''` outright below Admin; at Admin it renders a "Role Passwords" header, a "Manage" button (`openRolePasswordsModal()`), and a summary line naming which roles currently have a password set (never the values, and never the hash either — there's genuinely nothing to show, since only the hash exists, not the password). The actual editing happens in a real modal (`rolePasswordsModalBg`/`openRolePasswordsModal()`/`closeRolePasswordsModal()`/`saveRolePasswords()`, both `requireRole('admin')`-guarded) rather than inputs living directly in that admin-page section, deliberately: `renderAdmin()` re-renders on every `render()` call while in Admin mode — including background ones, like the file-sync poll's — and a live input sitting directly in that output would have its in-progress, unsaved keystrokes (and cursor focus) wiped out from under whoever's mid-typing the moment any unrelated `render()` fired; every other editable field in this app (categories included, just above) already lives in a modal for exactly this reason. The modal's three fields are plain `type="text"`, not masked — this is an Admin *setting* the values (not someone else entering one to unlock a role, which `pickRole()`'s own step does mask) — but they always open blank regardless, since only a hash is stored and there's no plain password left to pre-fill even for a role that already has one set; `openRolePasswordsModal()` sets each field's `placeholder` instead, to whichever of "Currently set — type a new password to change it" / "No password set — type one to require it" actually applies. Because the fields can't reflect the current value, blank-and-Save can no longer mean "clear this role's password" the way it did with a plain string — it now means "leave this role's password unchanged" instead, and Reviewer/Editor each have their own "Remove password requirement" checkbox for the (now explicit, unambiguous) case of actually wanting to drop one back to `null`; `saveRolePasswords()`'s internal `applyOne(role, inputId, clearId)` checks that checkbox first (wins outright over anything typed alongside it), then hashes and writes a non-blank field, and otherwise leaves that role's existing entry untouched.

**Admin has no "Remove" checkbox at all — an explicit user request ("for Admin this should not be deactivatable"), unlike Reviewer/Editor, which can be freely turned on and off.** `saveRolePasswords()` calls `applyOne('admin', 'rolePasswordAdminInput', null)` — a hardcoded `null` `clearId`, not a real checkbox's id — and `applyOne` only ever nulls a role out when `clearId && document.getElementById(clearId).checked`, so there is no code path capable of clearing Admin's password at all, independent of whatever markup happens to exist; `saveRolePasswords()` is still the *only* function that ever writes to `programme.rolePasswords`, so this one `null` is the entire enforcement. Admin can still be freely *changed* to a different password (typing a new one and saving hashes and replaces it, same as any other role) — only removing it back to "no password required" is foreclosed. This doesn't reopen the bootstrapping problem `normalizeData()`'s all-`null` default exists to avoid: a brand-new programme still starts with Admin unset, so the very first Admin can freely reach Admin and choose whether to lock it down — this only forecloses undoing that choice once actually made, not the initial free pick.

**`setUserRole(role)` leaves Admin mode for the All-workstreams Dashboard whenever the newly picked role isn't Admin and `mode` is currently `'admin'` — an explicit user request.** Without this, switching away from Admin while actually sitting on the Admin page would leave Admin-gated content (the Role Passwords section especially — see above) disappear out from under whoever's looking, mid-page, the next `render()`. Gated on the role having *actually changed* (`role !== userRole`, checked before reassigning it) so re-picking the role already active — clicking your own current tile in the modal — doesn't boot you off the Admin page for no reason; and on `role !== 'admin'` so picking Admin (from Admin or otherwise) never triggers it. When it does fire, both `mode = 'dashboard'` and `filterWorkstreamId = null` are set together — landing specifically on "All Workstreams", not whatever happened to be filtered before — and `save()` is called directly (mode/`filterWorkstreamId` are normally persisted by `setMode()`/`setFilterWorkstream()`, neither of which `setUserRole()` goes through here, so this is the one place that needs its own explicit `save()` for a mode/filter change).

**Testing**: `tests/harness.js`'s `resetState()` defaults `userRole = 'admin'` — every test written before RBAC existed (i.e. almost the entire suite) is testing *functionality*, not permissions, and would otherwise fail on the very first `requireRole()` guard it hit. Tests that specifically cover role gating (`tests/cases/role.test.js`) set `userRole` to a lower tier themselves before exercising the behavior they're checking, and reset nothing else about the fixture — same pattern as any other test that deviates from the shared baseline.

## Categories (Admin mode)

**The topbar tab for this mode is labeled "Settings" in the UI — an explicit user request to rename the page — but every internal identifier still says `admin`**: `mode === 'admin'`, `#tabAdmin`, `#adminBody`, `renderAdmin()`, `MODES`. Only the tab's own visible text changed (`#tabAdmin`'s `<button>` text, plus the role modal's "under Settings &gt; Role Passwords" reference — see "Roles (RBAC)" above); nothing about the *Admin role* changed, since that's a separate concept from this page and wasn't part of the request — `ROLE_META.admin.label` is still `"Admin"`, and every other visible "Admin" in this file (the role picker, the Role Passwords modal's own "Admin password" field) refers to that role, not this page. This doc keeps calling it "Admin mode" throughout, matching the code's own internal naming, the same way `ROLE_META[r].label` already differs from the bare role string it's keyed by (`'reviewer'` → `"Reviewer"`) elsewhere in this app.

`renderAdmin()` lists every category (name, its milestone template, and a live count of items currently using it) with an Edit button; "+ Category" opens the same `categoryModalBg` with nothing pre-filled. There's no separate "new" vs "edit" function — `openCategoryModal(id)` handles both, mirroring the workstream/item modal pattern (`editingCategoryId` is `null` for a new one).

**The milestone template renders as one small numbered chip per milestone (`.milestone-chip`), not a single `→`-joined string** — a later, explicit user request ("rendering it as small ordered chips would scan faster, especially for longer templates"), replacing the original plain-text preview (`c.milestones.join(' → ')`, ellipsis-truncated at the row's own width, so a longer template just read as text cut off mid-name). `.category-milestones-chips` is a `flex-wrap` row so a template with many milestones grows the row taller instead of truncating or requiring a scroll — every step in the checklist stays visible. Each chip is the milestone's name plus a small circular index badge (`.milestone-chip-num`, 1-based) rather than repeating a `→` between chips, since a chip sequence already reads left-to-right/top-to-bottom without needing its own separator glyph, and the number doubles as an explicit ordinal a bare `→` chain didn't give you at a glance. A template with zero milestones still renders through the same `.category-milestones-chips` container (for the row's own flex-alignment consistency) but with the plain "No milestones in template" text instead of any chips, styled muted via `.category-milestones-empty`.

`editingCategoryMilestones` is a working array of *plain strings* (not `{id, name, dueDate, status}` objects like an item's own milestones) — a category's `milestones` field is a template of names only, since a template has no dates or statuses yet. `renderCategoryMilestonesEditor()`/`addCategoryMilestoneRow()`/`removeCategoryMilestoneRow(idx)` mirror the item modal's milestone editor (same `.milestone-row` CSS class, just missing the date input and status select), including the "no re-render on keystroke, only on structural changes" discipline — see "Milestone checklist" above for why that matters for focus.

Order matters here (it's the sequence new items get seeded with), so each row is drag-and-drop reorderable — an explicit user request replacing an earlier up/down-arrow `.milestone-reorder` control (removed outright, not kept as a fallback). It's the same grip-handle/whole-row-drop-target split as `dragStartItem()`/`dropOnItem()` and `dragStartWs()`/`dropOnWs()`: only the small grip icon is `draggable="true"` (`dragStartCategoryMilestoneRow(e, idx)`, stashing `idx` in module-level `draggedCategoryMilestoneIdx`), so dragging doesn't fight with clicking into the name `<input>` next to it, while the whole `.milestone-row` is the drop target (`ondragover`/`ondrop` → `dropOnCategoryMilestoneRow(e, idx)`), so dropping anywhere on the row works, not just precisely on its own handle. Unlike `reorderItem()`/`reorderWorkstream()`, there's no id to key off — `editingCategoryMilestones` is a plain array of strings, in-memory only until `saveCategory()` commits it, and it's always fully re-rendered after every change anyway — so `dropOnCategoryMilestoneRow()` just splices the dragged name out of `fromIdx` and back in at the drop target's `idx` (a no-op if dropped back on itself) and calls `renderCategoryMilestonesEditor()` directly, rather than reassigning an `order` field the way the id-keyed arrays do. Both the grip handle and the row's drop-target attributes are gated to Editor+ (`requireRole('editor')` inside `dropOnCategoryMilestoneRow()` itself, defense-in-depth behind `renderCategoryMilestonesEditor()` only ever rendering them for `hasRole('editor')` in the first place) — reordering the template is still an edit, same tier as adding/removing/renaming a row. Like every other edit to this array, reordering only touches `editingCategoryMilestones`; the category's saved `milestones` array is untouched until `saveCategory()` commits it.

`deleteCategoryFromModal()` refuses outright (toast, no confirm modal) if the category is the reserved Pending one (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below — deleting it would strand any item still waiting on the scope-assign prompt), or if `categories.filter(c => !c.pending).length <= 1` — there must always be at least one *real, selectable* category for an item to reference, the same invariant `normalizeData()` enforces on load (Pending itself doesn't count toward that minimum, since it's never a destination a real item is meant to stay in). Otherwise it opens the confirm modal describing how many items will be reassigned, and on confirm reassigns every item using the deleted category to `categories.find(c => c.id !== catId && !c.pending)` (the first *other, non-Pending* category) before removing it. **This originally shipped with no undo toast at all** — an Admin-level structural concept rather than day-to-day content, so a plain "this cannot be undone" confirm was judged sufficient — but a later, explicit user request ("build an undo... for delete operations") extended the same toast-with-undo pattern here too. The extra wrinkle over a plain item/workstream delete: this one delete moves *other* records (every reassigned item's `categoryId`) as a side effect, so undo has to reverse both halves — splicing the category back into `categories` at its original index, then walking `movedItemIds` (collected while reassigning, the same "track only what this action itself moved" approach `deleteWorkstreamFromModal()`'s own undo already uses) and setting each one's `categoryId` back to the deleted category's id — guarded on `it.categoryId === fallback.id` first, so undo can't clobber an item that was independently reassigned to a *third* category in the few seconds before Undo was clicked. Item milestones are never touched by any of this either direction — unlike the destructive category-*switch* swap on an already-saved item (see "Switching an existing item's category" above), a category *deletion*'s fallback reassignment only ever changes `categoryId` itself.

### Category template edits sync to items already using that category

Editing an *existing* category's milestone template (add/rename/remove) propagates to every item with that `categoryId`, not just future new items — a deliberate reversal of this project's earlier stance (an item's checklist used to be a one-time snapshot, fully independent of its category after creation). `saveCategory()` diffs `editingCategoryOriginalMilestones` (the template as it was when the modal opened, captured by `openCategoryModal()`) against the final `editingCategoryMilestones` **by name, as sets** — there's no id linking one template entry across an edit, so a rename is indistinguishable from "the old name was removed and a new name was added," and is treated exactly that way.

**Reordering the template also syncs to existing items, but through a separate, non-destructive path.** A pure reorder (same names, different order — `added`/`removed` both empty but the arrays differ index-for-index) sets a `reordered` flag and, if any items use the category, calls `applyCategoryMilestoneSync(catId, [], [], milestones)` directly — no confirm modal, since nothing is added or removed, just resorted. `applyCategoryMilestoneSync`'s 4th parameter, `orderedNames`, is the template's current name order; after handling any add/remove, it sorts every affected item's `milestones` array by each milestone's index in `orderedNames` (a milestone whose name isn't in the template at all — a one-off custom milestone added by hand — ranks after every template-matched one, keeping its relative position via the sort's stability). This reorder is also applied on the *destructive* add/remove path, for the same reason: a newly-added milestone lands at its correct template position rather than always at the array's end.
