**This feature's alignment story took four passes before landing where it is now — worth documenting in full, since each pass looked correct in isolation and only the next user report caught what was actually wrong.**
1. **First attempt** ("align all action columns (L1 plan, L1 milestone, connected milestones)"): matched `.l1-milestone-row`'s/`.l1-linked-row`'s own Actions column to `--item-grid-cols`' *default* Actions width (`56px`), and added a blank `116px` spacer between Status and Actions on both, mirroring the default grid's own already-present blank column in that spot.
2. **A user report caught it as still wrong** ("actions are still ot aligned, also status of l1"): an L1 Plan's own row never actually renders under the *default* `--item-grid-cols` at all — `renderL1Plans()` wrapped its rows in a container whose own grid override at the time widened the Actions track specifically to `120px`, not `56px`. The fix widened `.l1-milestone-row`'s/`.l1-linked-row`'s own Actions column to `120px` to match, keeping the `116px` spacer.
3. **A second user report flagged the fix itself as the real problem** ("why is there space next to actions? is there an empty column? if so, delete it"): the `116px` spacer read as exactly what it was — a big, empty column, only there to chase pixel alignment with that oversized `120px` Actions track. The fix removed the spacer outright and shrank Actions back to `56px` on `.l1-milestone-row`, dropping the trailing alignment column from `.l1-linked-row` entirely — trading alignment with the L1 Plan row for eliminating the dead space, on the reasoning that the shared grid it was borrowed from couldn't safely be touched.
4. **A third user report reversed that trade-off** ("no L1 is not aligned gaind" / "now l1 is not aligned"): giving up on L1 alignment wasn't actually acceptable — the real fix was never to touch a shared, protected grid at all, it was to stop *reusing* someone else's grid override for L1 Plans in the first place. That borrowed `120px` Actions column was sized for a 4-icon worst case elsewhere in the app, which an L1 Plan's own row never has (only 3: drag/edit/delete, the same as a plain scope item's row, since Connect only ever lives on `.l1-milestone-row`, one level down). Reusing that grid was a reasonable-looking shortcut (both were flat, workstream-independent lists built on the same `itemRowHtml()`) that happened to import a mismatched width along with it. `renderL1Plans()` now wraps its rows in a **dedicated `.l1-plans-list` class** instead — its own `--item-grid-cols` override, sized correctly for what an L1 Plan's own row actually shows: every column it never populates shrinks to a minimal `10px`, and Actions returns to the plain, unmodified default's own `56px` (correctly sized for 3 icons).
5. **This same pass also had a real, self-inflicted bug in it: it mis-mapped which of `--item-grid-cols`' 11 columns was actually Status.** `--item-grid-cols`' authoritative shape (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above) is Chevron, Name, an always-blank column, Milestones-badge, an always-blank spacer, Plan-dates, a second always-blank spacer, "Milestone-Due" (always blank on an *item's* own row — `dependencySpocHtml`, unconditionally empty), **Tags** (`100px`, the *9th* value), **Status** (`116px`, the *10th* value), Actions (`56px`, the *11th* value) — Status and Actions are directly adjacent, with *no blank column between them at all*. Step 4 above mistook the 9th value (`100px`, actually Tags) for Status, and mistook the real Status column (`116px`, the 10th value) for "a blank spacer before Actions" — which is exactly why it inserted a spurious `10px` spacer into `.l1-milestone-row`/`.l1-linked-row` that was never actually needed, and set their own Status width to the wrong `100px`. **Fixed for real this time**: `.l1-plans-list` keeps Status at its correct, real `116px` (not `100px`) and shrinks the genuinely-always-blank Tags column down to `10px` alongside the rest; `.l1-milestone-row`/`.l1-linked-header`/`.l1-linked-row` all use Status `116px` too, with **no spacer at all** — Status sits directly next to Actions (or `.l1-linked-row`'s own blank alignment cell), the exact same real adjacency the shared grid has. This shrinks the L1 Plan row's own real Status-to-right-edge distance to `86px` (`gap(10) + Actions(56) + padding-right(20)`), and `.l1-milestone-row`/`.l1-linked-row` match it exactly with the identical, spacer-free formula. Status (and, between `.l1-milestone-row`/`.l1-linked-row`, Due too) genuinely aligns across all three levels, with no dead space anywhere — and, unlike step 4's version, no leftover mis-mapped column width either.

**The L1 Plan row's own Plan-dates column briefly changed to a single, discrete Due value, then was removed outright — two later, explicit user requests in quick succession** ("for l1 column, at add discret Due column. remot start-due range", then "remove due on L1 level"). The intermediate discrete-Due-value state (a read-only `fmtDate(it.dueDate)` once milestones exist, or a genuinely editable Due-only pill for a still-empty plan) no longer exists — `itemRowHtml()`'s `planDatesHtml` now renders a plain `` `<span></span>` `` for `isL1Plan`, unconditionally, the exact same "always a real element, never omitted" placeholder treatment `dependencySpocHtml`/other blanked-out columns already use elsewhere in this row (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above) — an omitted element here would collapse the shared `--item-grid-cols` column and misalign everything after it. This remains **display-only**, unchanged from the intermediate state: `it.startDate`/`it.dueDate` are still computed and stored exactly the way the universal "item with milestones" roll-up always has (`normalizeData()`, `saveL1MilestoneQuickAdd()`'s and `removeL1Milestone()`'s own recompute calls — none of that touched), just never rendered anywhere on an L1 Plan's own row at all now. Every other multi-milestone item (an ordinary scope item) is completely unaffected either way — this was always a scoped `isL1Plan`-only branch, never a change to the shared Start→Due range convention documented in "Item plan date range roll-up" above.

Due reuses the existing, generic `dateInputHtml()` component (the same guarded `onblur`/`onchange`/`armPickerCommit()` machinery every other inline date field in this app already uses for reliable commits — see "Every inline date field renders as a 'pill'" above); Status is the same click-to-cycle `.status-badge` pattern `cycleMilestoneStatus()` already uses, just scoped to `cycleL1MilestoneStatus(planId, milestoneId)`; Delete tombstones via the existing `deletedMilestoneIds` — the same mechanism an ordinary item's own milestone removal already uses — and, since a removed L1 milestone might have live links pointing at it, `removeL1Milestone()` also walks `linkedWorkstreamMilestones(milestoneId)` and removes just this one id from each linked workstream milestone's own `l1MilestoneIds` (stamping `updatedAt`), immediately, rather than leaving a dangling reference for the next `normalizeData()` pass to eventually self-heal — a multi-linked milestone's other, unrelated links are untouched.

**`removeL1Milestone()` goes through a confirm modal before doing any of that — a later, explicit user request** ("add a delete modal l1 plans and l1 milestones, asking for confirmation, like deleting milestones"), since the original version deleted the instant the trash icon was clicked, with no confirmation at all. Now shaped exactly like every other destructive delete in this app (`deleteItem()`, `deleteWorkstreamFromModal()`, `deleteCategoryFromModal()`): `openConfirmModal({title, body, action})` first, with the body naming the one real side effect worth calling out — "It will be unlinked from N connected workstream milestone(s)" when `linkedWorkstreamMilestones(milestoneId)` is non-empty, omitted when there's nothing linked — and the actual splice/tombstone/unlink logic only runs inside `action`, once confirmed. Pairs with the same undo-toast convention too: `showToast('Milestone deleted', false, undoFn)` reverses everything the delete did, not just the splice — `deletedMilestoneIds`/`untombstone()`, the milestone spliced back into `plan.milestones` at its original index, and (captured by `{itemId, milestoneId}` pairs *before* the delete runs, so undo can re-find each one fresh rather than trusting a closed-over object reference) every workstream milestone re-linked — but **only if it's still unlinked** at undo time, the identical care `deleteWorkstreamFromModal()`'s own undo already takes for the items it moved, so undoing doesn't clobber an independent link someone made to something else during the undo window. **An L1 Plan's own deletion already went through a confirm modal before this change and needed none of this work** — it's a plain `items` row, so its trash icon already called the shared `deleteItem()`, unrelated to `removeL1Milestone()` entirely.

**A zero-milestone L1 Plan's own chevron used to be dead — a user-reported bug.** `itemRowHtml()`'s `expandable` check (`total`) never included `isL1Plan`, so a brand-new L1 Plan — always starting with zero milestones — rendered the same non-clickable placeholder chevron a zero-milestone *ordinary* item gets, with no way to ever expand it and reach the inline "+ Add Milestone" affordance underneath. Fixed by adding `isL1Plan` to that check (`total || isL1Plan`) — always expandable, even with nothing yet, since there's no other on-row signal that expanding it does anything.

**An L1 milestone with at least one linked workstream milestone gets its own chevron too, expanding to reveal those links read-only underneath it — a later, explicit user request** ("if a L1 milestone has linked workstream milestones, show the linked milestone under the L1 milestone (with a chevron to collapse)"). `expandedL1MilestoneIds` is `expandedItemIds`'s own counterpart one level deeper (module-level, UI-only, not persisted, reset between tests the identical way) — `toggleL1MilestoneExpanded(milestoneId)` flips membership and calls `render()`. The chevron cell (`l1MilestoneRowsHtml()`'s leading column) is only ever wired to a real `onclick` when `linkedWorkstreamMilestones(m.id).length` is non-zero; a milestone with nothing linked yet renders the same inert, empty `<span class="item-chevron"></span>` placeholder every other "nothing to expand" cell in this app renders — never omitted outright, so the grid column stays aligned. This is a pure *display* affordance, not a second editing surface: adding/removing a link is still only ever done through the "Connect"/"N linked" button right next to the chevron (`openL1ConnectModal()`), and editing the linked milestone's own due date/status is still only ever done from its real row on the Planning board — nothing here writes to `items` at all.

**Five columns, with their own header row — Workstream / Scope Item / Milestone / Due / Status.** `l1LinkedRowHtml(item, milestone)` renders each linked workstream milestone's own row once expanded, with Workstream and Scope Item in two separate cells (`<span class="l1-linked-source">` each) rather than one combined "source" string — an explicit user follow-up request — falling back to "Unassigned" for a linked milestone belonging to a still-Unassigned scope item. `l1LinkedHeaderHtml()` labels all five columns, rendered once immediately above the first linked row for a given expanded milestone (`l1MilestoneRowsHtml()`'s `linkedRowsHtml = l1LinkedHeaderHtml() + linked.map(...)`) — the same "one header, right above the list it describes" pattern `l1MilestoneHeaderHtml()`/`milestoneHeaderHtml()` already use elsewhere, so the header only ever shows alongside actual rows, never on its own. Due and Status are still **plain, non-interactive text/a non-clickable badge**, unchanged. `.l1-linked-header`/`.l1-linked-row` share one grid, exactly five real columns and no trailing blank cell of any kind (see "This feature's alignment story..." above for why an earlier alignment-reservation column was removed) — Scope Item is a fixed `140px`, not its own equal-share `minmax(...,1fr)` the way it first shipped: with both Scope Item and Milestone as `1fr`, they always split whatever space was left over evenly regardless of either column's actual content, which read as Scope Item being "way too wide" (a user report) and pinned Milestone's own start position further right than it needed to be; capping Scope Item at a fixed width and leaving Milestone as the *only* flexible track (`110px 140px minmax(160px,1fr) 110px 100px`) moves Milestone's start position directly left in proportion to however much narrower Scope Item's fixed width is than its old flexible share. Indented 64px — **an explicit user request to align its own first column directly under `.l1-milestone-row`'s own Milestone-name column**: 64px is exactly that row's `34px` `padding-left` + its `20px` chevron column + its `10px` column gap, i.e. precisely where its Milestone-name column begins. This is a derived, not independent, value — if `.l1-milestone-row`'s own padding/chevron-width/gap ever changes, this needs to move with it to stay aligned.

**The item modal is reachable for an existing L1 Plan only via Edit, for renaming/deleting — never for milestone management, which stays fully inline on the L1 Plans page itself.** `openItemModal()`'s `isL1Plan` flag hides Workstream/Category/Milestones. An L1 Plan is never *created* through this modal either — `openL1PlanQuickAdd()`/`saveL1PlanQuickAdd()`, inline on the L1 Plans page, is the only way.

**`saveItem()` used to have zero awareness of `isL1Plan` at all, despite the fields above being hidden — a user-reported bug ("L1 plans are being shown as unassigned scope items... deleted L1 plans were still shown as unassigned scope items").** Hiding a field in the modal only ever meant `style.display = 'none'` — the underlying `<select>`/`editingMilestones` were still fully populated and still fully read back on Save, since nothing in `saveItem()` ever branched on `editingItemType`. Two things went wrong at once, traced by reproducing live: `#itemCategorySelect` has no blank "no category" option (there's nothing to select for an L1 Plan, which has `categoryId: null`), so `populateCategorySelect(it.categoryId)` silently defaulted it to whichever real category renders first — and since that now read as *different* from the L1 Plan's real (null) `categoryId`, it tripped `zeroMilestoneCategorySwitch` (meant for an ordinary zero-milestone item switching category), which wholesale-replaced the L1 Plan's own real, hand-managed milestones with that category's generic template — silently destroying them (tombstoned, unrecoverable) and orphaning any workstream milestone still `l1MilestoneId`-linked to the discarded ones. `workstreamId` itself happened to stay `null` by coincidence (`populateWorkstreamSelect()`'s own `includeUnassigned` check, `it.workstreamId == null`, is true for an L1 Plan for the same underlying reason it's true for a still-Pending scope item, so the select correctly offered/preselected "Unassigned") — this half was never actually broken, just accidentally correct rather than deliberately handled. `itemType` itself was never at risk either (`data` never includes it on the edit path), so a corrupted L1 Plan always stayed correctly excluded from Planning's Unassigned section and the per-workstream sections (both filter on `itemType !== 'l1plan'` — see `unassignedItemsSorted()`/`renderStatusView()`'s own `wItems` filter) — reproduced directly and confirmed live, corruption alone never actually leaked one into Planning. What *did* change, visibly, on the L1 Plans page itself: a corrupted plan's own real checklist was gone, replaced by a category's generic template — which is what likely read as "looks like an unassigned scope item" (a category-seeded checklist is exactly what an ordinary scope item's own looks like) rather than a literal appearance in Planning's own Unassigned list.

**Fixed by giving `saveItem()` an early, narrow `isL1Plan` branch, right after the name is read and validated** — `if (savingType === 'l1plan') { plan.name = name; plan.updatedAt = Date.now(); save(); closeItemModal(); render(); return; }`, before any of the scope-item-only field reads (`workstreamId`/`categoryId`/`milestones`/`status`/dates/`dependency`) ever run. Renaming is the only thing this modal is for on an L1 Plan, so this is the entire save path for one now — `categoryId`/`workstreamId` stay permanently `null`, `milestones` is never touched at all. **This only prevents the corruption going forward — it can't undo milestone data already lost on an L1 Plan corrupted before this fix shipped** (the discarded milestones were genuinely tombstoned, not just hidden); `categoryId` on an already-corrupted plan self-heals back to `null` on the very next `normalizeData()` pass regardless (its existing `if (it.itemType === 'l1plan') it.categoryId = null;` backfill, unconditional and independent of this fix), but the real milestone content itself does not come back.

**`normalizeData()` backfill.** `itemType` accepts `'l1plan'` as a second valid value alongside `'scope'` (checked in the same enum-fallback line, before the `categoryId` repair that needs to know about it); `categoryId` is forced `null` for `'l1plan'`. `l1MilestoneIds` is backfilled on every *ordinary* item's milestone: forced to an empty array outright on an L1 Plan's own milestones (an L1 milestone never links to another L1 milestone), migrated from a legacy scalar `l1MilestoneId` (or normalized from a missing/undefined value to an empty array) if it isn't already a real array, then filtered so any id that's since been deleted or is no longer a real L1 milestone is dropped, via `findL1Milestone()`, rather than left dangling — the same per-item orphaned-reference treatment a stale `workstreamId` already gets elsewhere, just applied per-id across the array instead of to a single scalar. **`normalizeData()` does not independently re-force an L1 Plan's own `workstreamId` back to `null`** — its `workstreamId: null` is guaranteed structurally, by every creation path always setting it that way, not defensively re-validated on every load.

**Mode/nav placement mirrors Admin's own "not workstream-scoped" reasoning exactly.** `'l1plans'` is a member of the `MODES` array (`MODES` also includes `'admin'`, since a single generic `MODES.forEach(...)` loop drives every `#tab<Mode>` button's active-class toggle, not just the `.view-tabs` pill's own three). `#l1PlansBody` is a sidebar-less container (a plain `.main`, no `.body-row`/`.sidebar`), the same shape `#adminBody` already has, since L1 Plans are never filtered by the shared workstream selector (`filterWorkstreamId`) the way Planning/Review/Dashboard are. `#tabL1plans` sits in the topbar's right-hand cluster next to `#tabAdmin`, outside `.view-tabs`. `renderL1Plans()` is a single flat list (`allL1Plans()`, sorted by `order`) — no per-workstream sections, since an L1 Plan isn't workstream-scoped at all.

### L1 Plan import/export

**A formatted `.xlsx` round-trip dedicated to L1 Plans specifically — an explicit user request, distinct from the full-programme Excel report ("Export to Excel" above) and from the plain-data JSON Export/Import.** The column shape — L1 Plan / Stream / Milestone / Target Date / Reporting Level — matches a real template the user already had in use (`ImportTemplate_L1Plan.xlsx`) for tracking these by hand before this feature existed, so importing it is a genuine migration path from that spreadsheet, not a generic data dump. Import and Export both live as buttons directly on the L1 Plans page header (next to "+ Add L1 Plan"), not in the topbar's Data dropdown — confirmed with the user, since this feature is scoped entirely to that one page. Export is available at any role (read-only, matching the plain Export's own no-gate precedent); Import is Editor+ (`triggerL1PlanImport()`'s own `requireRole('editor')` guard, ahead of even opening the file picker).

**Stream is read from the file (so a wrong-column-count file doesn't silently misalign every other column) but never stored anywhere** — Pulse has no equivalent concept — **and is exported back out as an always-blank column**, purely to keep the round-trip shape identical to the source template rather than silently dropping a column a re-imported file would still expect to find.

**Reporting Level is a brand-new field with no equivalent anywhere else in this app before this feature — confirmed directly with the user as a new, real field on the L1 milestone (`m.reportingLevel: String|null`), not just read-and-discarded like Stream.** It started as a genuinely free-form string (only one example value, `"Programme"`, was ever seen at first, with no fixed enum since the full set of real-world values wasn't specified yet) but was reversed into a real fixed vocabulary on a later, explicit user request — see "reportingLevels — a fixed, admin-managed vocabulary" further below. Displayed as a small, neutral badge (`.l1-milestone-reporting-level` — grey/`--panel-2`, not the accent-colored `--accent-bg` `.l1-milestone-linked-count` uses right next to it, since this is purely informational, not an active/on state) appended to the milestone's name via `l1MilestoneRowsHtml()`'s `nameHtml`, omitted entirely when `null` — the same "badge only when there's something to show" convention `l1-milestone-linked-count` already uses one token over. **There was originally no UI to hand-set it at all** — the only way a milestone got a `reportingLevel` was by importing a file that provided one for it; `makeL1Milestone()`'s own hand-add path (`saveL1MilestoneQuickAdd()`) still always creates one with `reportingLevel: null`. `normalizeData()` backfills/enforces it the exact mirror image of `l1MilestoneIds`'s own backfill just above: a real string (or a missing/undefined value normalized to `null`) on an L1 Plan's own milestone, forced `null` outright on an ordinary item's — the two fields are each other's structural opposite, one only ever meaningful on a workstream milestone, the other only ever meaningful on an L1 one. **A later, explicit user request reversed the "no UI" half of this** ("provide a real edit modal, where i can change due date and Reporting Level") — see "The L1 milestone edit modal" below for the real editing surface this field (and Due) got.

**`reportingLevels` — a fixed, admin-managed vocabulary — a further, later explicit user request ("Reporting Level should be a fixed selection... editable as admin in settings"), reversing the field's own brief run as free text.** `DEFAULT_REPORTING_LEVELS` (`['Programme', 'Management Reporting', 'Executive Committee (ExCo)']`, next to `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES`) is what a fresh programme seeds `reportingLevels` with (`seedDefaults()`); an Admin can add, rename, or remove entries afterward, the identical "a starting set, not a permanent one" relationship `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES` already has to `categories`. Unlike a category, a Reporting Level is nothing more than a plain string — no id, no order, no milestones template — so it rides a much lighter version of the same machinery: `normalizeData()` coerces `reportingLevels` to an array of trimmed, non-empty, case-insensitively-deduplicated strings (first occurrence wins) and reseeds the defaults outright once the list is genuinely empty, the identical "always something to fall back to" invariant `categories.length === 0` already enforces one block up. Synced via `buildIndexPayload()`/`recombineSyncData()` exactly like `categories`; merged the same additive, silent way too (`mergeData()`, deduped case-insensitively by value since there's no id to key on) — a level removed on one device can still be reintroduced by a merge from another device that still has it, the identical, already-accepted limitation `categories` themselves have.

**Admin-only, both to view and to manage — `reportingLevelsSectionHtml()`, appended to `renderAdmin()`'s own output right before `rolePasswordsSectionHtml()`.** The actual editing happens in `reportingLevelsModalBg` (`openReportingLevelsModal()`/`saveReportingLevelsModal()`), a real modal rather than live inputs sitting directly in that admin section — the identical "renderAdmin() redraws on every render(), including background ones, so a live input there would lose in-progress keystrokes/focus" reasoning `rolePasswordsSectionHtml()`'s own comment already documents. `editingReportingLevels` is a detached working array of plain strings (the same shape `editingCategoryMilestones` already has in the category modal), edited via `addReportingLevelRow()`/`removeReportingLevelRow(idx)` — no drag-and-drop reordering here, deliberately: this list is typically a handful of entries and reordering it was never part of what was actually asked for, unlike a category template's own row order (which directly drives a new item's seeded checklist sequence). `saveReportingLevelsModal()` applies the identical trim/dedup coercion `normalizeData()` already applies, so a save can never produce a state the very next load would immediately have to self-heal away from, and refuses to save down to zero entries (a toast, modal stays open) — the same "an L1 milestone edit modal with nothing to pick from would be a real dead end" reasoning that drives `categories.length === 0`'s own reseed.

**The L1 milestone edit modal's own Reporting Level field is a real `<select>` now, not free text — `openL1MilestoneModal()` populates it fresh from `reportingLevels` every time it opens.** A milestone's *current* value that isn't in the admin-managed list at all — imported from a file before this shipped, or set by hand during this field's brief free-text era — is appended as its own extra option, clearly labeled `"<value> (not in the current list)"`, and stays selected until someone deliberately picks a real one; it's never silently dropped or force-changed just because an Admin's list moved on without it. The match against the current list is case-insensitive (`m.reportingLevel.toLowerCase() === l.toLowerCase()`), so a value differing from a real entry only by casing (`"programme"` vs. `"Programme"`) is treated as the same value, not a spurious orphan — selecting and saving without changing anything quietly normalizes it to the list's own canonical casing, a harmless, arguably desirable side effect rather than a bug.

**The L1 milestone edit modal (`l1MilestoneModalBg`) — an explicit user request** ("provide a real edit modal, where i can change due date and Reporting Level"), landing alongside two related, separately-requested additions to the same row (inline name editing, drag-and-drop reordering — both documented in their own paragraphs above/below). Before this, Connect and Delete were the only two actions available on an L1 milestone's own row; there was no way to hand-set Reporting Level at all, and no dedicated place to edit Due beyond the row's own inline pill. `openL1MilestoneModal(planId, milestoneId)` (Editor+, `requireRole('editor')`-guarded) populates the modal's title with the milestone's own name (read-only here — renaming is still the row's own always-live inline input, `updateL1MilestoneName()`, not duplicated into this modal) and two real fields: a plain `<input type="date">` for Due, and a plain `<input type="text">` for Reporting Level. `saveL1MilestoneModal()` routes a Due change through the exact same `updateL1MilestoneDateField()` the row's own inline pill already calls — one implementation of what a Due edit does, regardless of which of the two UIs made it — and writes Reporting Level directly (trimmed, empty stored as `null` not `''`, stamping `updatedAt`) since that field has no other write path to share. The modal's own trigger sits in the row's Actions cluster, alongside Connect/Delete/the new drag handle — see that cluster's own CSS comment for how it grew to fit four real icons. **Its icon started as `fa-pencil`, then was corrected to the plain `fa-pen` + `"Edit"` title on a later, explicit user request** ("use the same pen icon for edit like in normal workstream planning") — matching the exact icon/label every other Edit button in this app already uses (`itemRowHtml()`'s own row-level Edit, the sidebar's `.ws-row-edit`, the category list's), rather than a one-off `fa-pencil` glyph nothing else in the app used.

**`makeL1Plan(name)`/`makeL1Milestone(name, dueDate, reportingLevel)` factor out the exact object shape a fresh L1 Plan/milestone needs — a refactor made *while building this feature*, since import needed a second creation path for each and duplicating a 10-field object literal across two call sites each would have been real, avoidable drift risk.** `saveL1PlanQuickAdd()`/`saveL1MilestoneQuickAdd()` (the pre-existing hand-add paths) now call these too, so there is exactly one place each shape is defined, not two that could silently diverge.

**Matching is by exact name — an L1 Plan by its own name, and within it a milestone by its own name — confirmed directly with the user, including that this is intentionally exact (not case-insensitive/trimmed-only-on-import).** `importL1PlansFromExcel(file)` groups the sheet's rows by `L1 Plan`; for each row's plan name, `items.find(it => it.itemType === 'l1plan' && it.name === planName)` either finds an existing plan or creates a new one via `makeL1Plan()`. Within that plan, `plan.milestones.find(m => m.name === milestoneName)` either finds an existing milestone or creates one via `makeL1Milestone()`.

**Updating an existing milestone happens strictly *in place* (same `id`), never by replacing the milestone object outright — the one behavior explicitly confirmed and re-confirmed with the user up front** ("if a l1 plan already exists, check if a milestone exists. if yes, update the date (from file), but keep linked milestones"). This matters concretely because a workstream milestone links to an L1 milestone *by that id*, one entry in its own `m.l1MilestoneIds` array (see above), with deliberately no reverse-reference to repair if the id ever changed — replacing the object (even with identical field values otherwise) on every re-import would silently orphan every link pointing at it, with nothing to ever notice or fix it back up. Target Date and Reporting Level are each only overwritten when the file's row actually provides a non-blank value for that cell — a blank cell leaves whatever the milestone already had untouched rather than destructively clearing it, since a gap in a hand-maintained tracking sheet reads as "no update for this field this time," not "erase this." A brand-new milestone (no existing name match) is unaffected by any of this nuance — it's simply created fresh via `makeL1Milestone()` with whatever the row provides, blank or not.

**Each touched plan's own `status`/`startDate`/`dueDate` roll-up is recomputed once the whole file has been processed** — the identical `computedStatusFromMilestones()`/`computedDateRangeFromMilestones()` recompute `saveL1MilestoneQuickAdd()`/`removeL1Milestone()` already do after any change to a plan's milestones, just batched to run once per plan touched by the import rather than once per row.

**Dates round-trip through a real Excel date value in both directions, not text — and both directions have to agree on *which* timezone convention they're using, or the date silently drifts by a day.** `excelDateCell()` (shared with the full Excel report) writes via `new Date(Date.UTC(y, m-1, d))`, not `new Date(iso + 'T00:00:00')` (parsed in the browser's *local* timezone, the way every other date in this app deliberately parses one — see `fmtDate()`) — ExcelJS converts a JS `Date` to an Excel serial number using that Date's *UTC* fields, so a locally-parsed midnight in any UTC+ timezone silently rolls back to the *previous* day's serial once written (confirmed directly: opened a locally-parsed export and read a `2026-07-29` due date back as `2026-07-28`, testing from UTC+2). `excelCellToIsoDate()` is the mirror image on the read side: it reads the `Date` ExcelJS hands back for a date-formatted cell via `getUTC*()` getters, not local ones — since a serial number has no timezone of its own to begin with, this is what makes the round trip exact regardless of either side's browser timezone. A plain string cell (someone typed a date as text rather than a real formatted date) falls back to a best-effort native `Date` parse; a bare number is deliberately *never* treated as a raw Excel serial (`new Date(number)` reads a number as milliseconds-since-1970, not days-since-1900, and would silently produce a nonsense date rather than the intended one) — in practice ExcelJS always hands back a real `Date` for a genuinely date-formatted cell, so a bare number here only ever means the cell wasn't really a date at all, and reading it as "no date" is correct, not a gap.

**Column matching is by header name, not fixed position** — `sheet.getRow(1).eachCell(...)` builds a `{lowercased header: column number}` map once, so `L1 Plan`/`Milestone`/`Target Date`/`Reporting Level` can appear in any column order (or with extra/reordered columns around them) and still be found correctly; only `L1 Plan` and `Milestone` are required (missing either aborts the whole import with a toast explaining which), `Target Date`/`Reporting Level` are optional and simply contribute nothing when absent. A row missing either an L1 Plan or a Milestone name is skipped and counted, never partially imported. `importL1PlansFromExcel()` ends with one toast summarizing exactly what happened — new plans, new milestones, milestones updated, rows skipped — each clause only appearing when its count is non-zero, plus a distinct message for the "genuinely nothing to import" case (an empty sheet vs. every row being skipped get their own wording, so the reason is never a mystery).

### L1 Plans Dashboard

**A second sub-tab on the L1 Plans page itself, Plans/Dashboard — an explicit user request, built after an exploratory "what would a good L1 dashboard look like, don't code yet" discussion narrowed the scope to two of five proposed ideas** ("ok, then build the dashboard with features 1+2": a status breakdown card, and a delayed/at-risk list). Two earlier, separate exploratory questions shaped where this landed before any code was written — "would it make sense to move L1 Plans into the left navigation bar?" (answered: no, it isn't workstream-scoped, so it doesn't belong alongside the shared sidebar's per-workstream rows) and "if I kept L1 Plans in the top bar, where would an L1 dashboard go?" (answered: a sub-tab within the L1 Plans page itself, mirroring Dashboard mode's own Overview/Dependencies split — see "Dashboard mode" above) — both confirmed before this was actually built.

**`l1PlansTab` (`'plans' | 'dashboard'`, module-level, UI-only, not persisted) is `dashboardTab`'s own direct counterpart, `setL1PlansTab(t)` mirroring `setDashboardTab(t)`.** It's rendered differently from `dashboardTab`, though, not identically: Dashboard mode's own Overview/Dependencies pill lives in a separate static toolbar (`#dashboardModeToolbar`) sitting outside `#main`, coordinated against Dashboard's Exec-Summary-export toolbar showing only on one of the two sub-tabs (see "Dashboard mode" above). L1 Plans has no such second toolbar to coordinate with, and `#l1PlansBody` is already fully torn down and rebuilt by `renderL1Plans()` on every call — so the sub-tab pill here is built as plain markup *inside* `renderL1Plans()`'s own returned HTML instead, with no separate static shell element at all. `tests/harness.js`'s `resetState()` resets `l1PlansTab = 'plans'` between tests, the same way `dashboardTab` is reset.

**`renderL1Plans()` dispatches early**, the same shape `renderDashboard()`'s own `dashboardTab === 'dependencies'` branch uses: with `l1PlansTab === 'dashboard'`, it renders the sub-tab pill plus `renderL1PlansDashboardHtml()`'s output and returns, before ever touching the ordinary Plans-list markup — the Import/Export buttons and "+ Add L1 Plan"/expand-all controls are scoped to the Plans branch only, since none of them apply to a read-only dashboard view.

**Two cards, reusing conventions already established by Dashboard mode's own Item Status card and Delayed-milestone color coding, rather than inventing new ones:**
- **L1 Plan Status** — a `STATUSES`-driven stacked bar (`.dash-bar-seg`) plus a full legend, counting every L1 Plan by `computeL1PlanStatus(p.id) || 'not-started'` (the exact same rolled-up status `itemRowHtml()`'s own `displayStatus` already reads for an L1 Plan's row — see "L1 Plans" above) — never a plan's own stale, vestigial `plan.status` field. This mirrors Dashboard mode's Item Status card almost exactly (`--stat-*` colors, the same bar-segment/legend markup shape), just counting L1 Plans instead of scope items.
- **Delayed** — a plain count card paired with a list section below it (`l1DelayedEntries()`, see next paragraph), the same "small stat card, then a full list underneath" layout the Overdue/Upcoming feeds use one page over.

**A per-plan rollup table — a later, explicit user follow-up request** ("Per-plan rollup table — one row per L1 Plan: status, milestone count, how many are linked vs still standalone, similar to the main Dashboard's per-workstream summary rows"), the third of the originally-proposed ideas, built as a separate pass after "features 1+2" shipped. `l1PlanRollupRows()` maps every L1 Plan to `{plan, status, total, linked, standalone}`: `status` is `computeL1PlanStatus(p.id) || 'not-started'`, the identical rollup the status-breakdown card above and the plan's own row in the Plans list both already use; `total` is `plan.milestones.length`; `linked` counts milestones where `linkedWorkstreamMilestones(m.id).length > 0` — the exact same predicate `l1LinkIndicatorHtml()`'s own "is this linked at all" check and the connect-modal's candidate-exclusion already use, so "linked" means the identical thing here as everywhere else this feature already establishes it; `standalone` is simply `total - linked`. **Placed directly below the top two cards, before the Delayed list** — mirroring the main Dashboard's own "Per-workstream summary" table's exact position relative to its own top cards and Overdue/Upcoming feeds (see "Dashboard mode" above), not the position of anything else on this particular page.

**Rendered as its own real CSS grid (`.l1-dash-plan-header`/`.l1-dash-plan-row`), not `.dash-ws-row`'s simpler flex shape** — the main Dashboard's per-workstream table only ever shows two numbers per row (item count, milestone completion %) and can get away with `.dash-ws-row`'s plain flex layout; this one has five real columns (Plan/Status/Milestones/Linked/Standalone), which needs a fixed `grid-template-columns` for the same "header and data rows must agree on a pinned width, `auto` silently misaligns them once row content differs" reason `.dash-dep-header`/`.dash-dep-row` (Dashboard's own Dependencies tracker) already document for themselves — copied from that same precedent rather than rediscovered. Status renders as the same `.status-badge` pill (`cursor:default`, non-interactive) every other read-only status display in this app uses, not plain text, so it visually matches the Plans list's own row one tab over. Every row is `.attention-row-link`-clickable via `openL1PlanFromDashboard(planId)` — `openL1MilestoneFromDashboard()`'s own counterpart, minus the milestone-level expand a plan-level row has nothing to point at — landing on the Plans tab with that plan expanded, the identical click-through precedent the Delayed list's own rows and the main Dashboard's `attentionHtml()` rows already set. A programme with no L1 Plans yet shows "No L1 Plans yet." in place of the table, the same `.attention-empty` treatment "Nothing delayed." already uses one section down.

**`l1DelayedEntries()` is a new function, not a reuse of `l1DelayedEntries`-shaped logic already sitting somewhere else — it has to merge two genuinely different kinds of lateness that already existed as separate, independently-computed checks.** For every L1 Plan's every milestone, it collects up to two distinct entries:
1. **The L1 milestone's own rolled-up Actual finishing late against its own manual Due** — reusing `isL1MilestoneActualLate(m, computedActual)` outright (see "L1 Plans" above), not reimplementing the comparison.
2. **Any linked workstream milestone whose own Due or Actual is late against the same L1 milestone's Due** — reusing `isWorkstreamMilestoneLate(wm, m.dueDate)` outright, skipped entirely when the L1 milestone has no manual `dueDate` set at all (nothing to compare against — the same "`m.dueDate` is the single, non-vacuous source of truth" reasoning "L1 Plans" above already established for both checks).

**Both can legitimately fire for the same milestone at once — this was confirmed as correct behavior, not a bug, while writing this feature's own tests.** A linked milestone that finishes after both its own due date and the L1 milestone's manual due date makes the L1's own rolled-up Actual late *and* makes that specific linked milestone read as late in its own right — two real, independently-true facts about the same underlying situation, so `l1DelayedEntries()` correctly produces two rows, not one deduplicated row. Each entry carries a `label` (`"<plan> — <L1 milestone>"` for the first kind, `"<plan> — <L1 milestone>: <scope item> — <workstream milestone>"` for the second, extending the same `"<item> — <milestone>"` convention Dashboard's own attention feed and `reviewHistoryRowHtml()`'s change-log labels already use), a `date` (whichever of Actual/Due is the late one), and `planId`/`milestoneId` for the click-through below. The full list is sorted by `date` ascending, oldest lateness first — the same ordering convention `l1DelayedEntries`' own sibling, Dashboard's Overdue feed, already uses.

**Every row is clickable, opening the underlying L1 milestone directly rather than leaving the Dashboard sub-tab as a dead end — the same "close the loop back to the real editable surface" precedent Dashboard mode's own `attentionHtml()` rows already set for scope items** (see "Dashboard mode" above). `openL1MilestoneFromDashboard(planId, milestoneId)` switches `mode` to `'l1plans'`, forces `l1PlansTab = 'plans'` (so navigating away from the dashboard sub-tab and back always lands on a real, editable view of what was just clicked, never back on the dashboard itself), expands both the plan (`expandedItemIds`) and the specific L1 milestone's own linked-milestones list (`expandedL1MilestoneIds`) so the clicked-through context is immediately visible with no further expanding needed, then calls `render()`. `.attention-row-link`, the exact class Dashboard's own clickable rows use, is reused here rather than a bespoke one — same cursor/hover treatment, same visual language.

**Deliberately does not build the remaining two ideas raised during the earlier "what would a good dashboard look like" discussion** — an Upcoming (not-yet-late) L1 milestones list and a Reporting Level breakdown were both proposed and ranked alongside the per-plan rollup table above, but each shipped only once its own explicit follow-up request named it ("features 1+2" first, the per-plan rollup table later — see above). None of the code here precludes adding the remaining two later; they simply weren't part of what was asked for through either pass.

### L1 Plans search and Reporting Level filter chips

**A search box plus multi-select Reporting Level filter chips on the Plans sub-tab — an explicit user request** ("add search/filter options on L1. like on the screenshot. instead of depencies, provide the different report levels a filter. filter should be multi select, like in workstream planning"), mirroring Planning's own search-box-plus-quick-filter-chips toolbar closely: `l1PlansSearchQuery`/`matchesL1PlansSearch(plan)` is `matchesPlanningSearch()`'s exact counterpart — a plain, case-insensitive substring match against the L1 Plan's own name, nothing else. `l1PlansReportingLevelFilters` (a `Set`) — a genuine multi-select (any number of chips active at once). There's no Dependencies-style single boolean toggle here at all, unlike Planning's own chip row — Reporting Level is a value to pick *among*, not an on/off flag, so every chip in this row is one of the multi-select group, none of them a standalone boolean. Each Reporting Level chip's `onclick` is keyed by array index into `reportingLevels`, not the raw level string — the established convention every other onclick in this app already follows (a generated id or a plain index, never free/admin-entered text embedded directly in a JS string literal), so a level name containing an apostrophe (an Admin can type anything into `reportingLevelsModalBg`'s own free-text rows) can never break out of the handler's own quoting the way embedding the raw string would risk.

**A matching status filter row (6 status chips plus a divider, mirroring Planning's own status-chips-then-divider-then-Dependencies-chip row) briefly existed alongside this one too — a later, explicit user request ("also provide status filter and the with a seperator the reporting level. similar like this") — then was removed again by a further, separate explicit user request ("remove the status filter options on l1. only reporting level")**, so `l1PlansStatusFilters`/`setL1PlansStatusFilter()` no longer exist at all — L1 Plans filtering is Reporting Level only, and the chip row (`renderL1PlansReportingLevelChips()`, its original name restored) is back to a single group with no divider.

**A user-reported bug during the status-filter's brief existence ("looks like the filer no not owrk at all", clarified as "chip highlights but list doesn't change") revealed a real design gap, worth keeping in mind since it applies just as much to Reporting Level alone: the original `matchesL1PlansReportingLevelFilter(plan)` only ever decided whether the *whole plan* showed** (a plan passed once *any* of its milestones carried a selected level), **but `l1MilestoneRowsHtml()` then still rendered *every one* of that plan's milestones underneath, including ones that didn't match at all.** On a plan with several milestones carrying different Reporting Levels, clicking almost any chip still left the same plan — and every one of its milestones, matching or not — on screen, which reads exactly like "the filter does nothing." Confirmed directly against a mockup: the fix the user actually wanted ("the filter should work on the l1 milestones") moves filtering from the plan level down to the milestone-row level.

`matchesL1MilestoneReportingLevel(m)` is the one real predicate now, applied *per milestone*, and `l1MilestoneRowsHtml(plan)` filters `sortedMilestones` down to just the ones passing it whenever `l1PlansReportingLevelFilters.size` is non-zero (`visibleMilestones`), mapping only those to rows — an unfiltered view still shows every milestone exactly as before, untouched. `matchesL1PlansReportingLevelFilter(plan)` is the plan-level wrapper `renderL1Plans()`/`l1PlansExpandAllIds()` actually use: a plan shows once it has **at least one** milestone satisfying the filter (`plan.milestones.some(matchesL1MilestoneReportingLevel)`), or unconditionally when nothing is active — the "which plans are listed at all" half of the feature is otherwise unchanged, only the milestone-row rendering underneath a passing plan is corrected. `renderL1Plans()`'s own filter chain is `p => matchesL1PlansSearch(p) && matchesL1PlansReportingLevelFilter(p)`; `l1PlansExpandAllIds()` matches the same way — "Expand all" still only ever acts on plans currently visible under both the search box and the level filter.

**The search+filter toolbar (`#l1PlansSearchToolbar`) is static, sitting outside `#l1PlansBody` — the identical reason `#planningSearchToolbar` sits outside `#main`: so a keystroke's own re-render of `renderL1Plans()` never steals focus from the input mid-type.** Building it revealed a real ordering bug worth documenting: the Plans/Dashboard sub-tab pills used to be built *inline*, as the first thing in `renderL1Plans()`'s own returned HTML (a `subTabsHtml` string prepended to every branch's output) — but a static toolbar placed ahead of `#l1PlansBody` in source order always visually renders *before* whatever that function puts at the very top of its own innerHTML, so the newly-added static search box ended up rendering *above* the sub-tabs instead of below them, reading backwards (search-then-tabs, when the tabs are the navigation and should read first). Fixed by moving the sub-tabs out to their own static `#l1PlansSubTabsToolbar` element too — mirroring `#dashboardModeToolbar`'s own Overview/Dependencies pills exactly — sitting *before* `#l1PlansSearchToolbar` in source order, with `render()`'s own `mode === 'l1plans'` handling toggling each tab button's `.active` class directly (`document.getElementById('tabL1PlansPlans').classList.toggle(...)`, etc.) the identical toggle-not-regenerate pattern the Dashboard sub-tabs already use, rather than `renderL1Plans()` rebuilding the pill markup via string concatenation on every call. `renderL1Plans()` itself no longer touches the tab buttons at all — each of its two branches (`dashboard`/`plans`) writes only its own tab's content into `#l1PlansBody`.

**A third L1 Plans sub-tab, Unlinked, existed for a time — a traceability-gap report listing every ordinary milestone with no L1 link, plus its own reverse-direction picker modal (`l1PickModalBg`, letting a user pick which L1 milestone(s) an already-known workstream milestone should link to) — before being removed entirely on a later, explicit user request ("in l1 plans, remove the unlinked functionality" / "i meant the unliked tab").** `l1PlansTab` is back down to just `'plans'`/`'dashboard'`; `unlinkedMilestones()`, `unlinkedMilestonesSorted()`, `l1UnlinkedHeaderHtml()`, `l1UnlinkedRowHtml()`, `renderL1UnlinkedItemsHtml()`, and the whole picker (`l1PickModalBg`, `openL1PickModal()`, `closeL1PickModal()`, `l1PickHeaderHtml()`, `l1PickRowHtml()`, `renderL1PickList()`, `toggleL1MilestoneLink()`) are all gone — the picker was reachable only from the Unlinked tab's own row action, so removing the tab made it dead code too. Linking a workstream milestone to an L1 milestone still works exactly as before, via the forward direction (`l1ConnectModalBg`, opened from an L1 milestone's own Connect button — see above) — only this one reverse-direction UI is gone. If a traceability-gap view comes back, it should be a deliberate, fresh re-add rather than resurrecting the removed code.

A review cycle is scoped to exactly one workstream (`activeReviewCycle(wsId)` finds the one cycle for that workstream with both `completedAt` and `cancelledAt` still `null` — `startReviewCycle()` is a no-op if one's already active, so there's never more than one open cycle per workstream at a time). Review mode has no workstream picker of its own any more — it reads the shared `filterWorkstreamId` (see "The shared workstream selector" above), which is guaranteed non-null whenever Scope Item Review specifically is showing (`reviewTab === 'scope'`) — the Action Log tab is the one exception, deliberately usable with no workstream selected at all (see "The shared workstream selector" above and "Action Log" below). The shared sidebar (`renderSidebar()`, `#wsList` — same one Planning and Dashboard use) shows each workstream a small badge regardless of which of the three modes is active: amber "In review" if it has an active cycle, red "Overdue" if `isReviewOverdue(w.id)` (no completed cycle in the last `REVIEW_STALE_DAYS` (30) days, or never reviewed at all), otherwise no badge — so review staleness is visible while planning, not just while reviewing. **`isReviewOverdue(wsId)` first checks whether the workstream has any scope items at all** (`items.some(it => it.workstreamId === wsId)`) **and returns `false` immediately if not** — an empty, just-created workstream has nothing to review yet, so it shouldn't read as "Overdue" from the moment it's added (every item is backfilled with a `startDate`/`dueDate` the instant it's created, by `normalizeData()`, so "no items" and "no items with any dates" are the same condition here — there's no in-between state of an item existing with no dates to check for separately). This guard is shared by both consumers of `isReviewOverdue()` — the sidebar badge and Dashboard's "Overdue for Review" card below.

**Confirmation is milestone-level, not just item-level.** An item with at least one milestone has *no independent stored confirmation at all* — `isItemConfirmedInCycle(cycle, it)` derives it as confirmed only once every one of `it.milestones` has its own entry in `cycle.milestoneConfirmations` (`toggleMilestoneConfirm(cycleId, milestoneId)`, a plain toggle). This mirrors the rest of the app's "computed vs manual" pattern (status, plan dates): there's no separate item-level flag that could drift out of sync with its milestones' actual confirmation state, so "a scope item can only be confirmed once all its milestones are confirmed" is an invariant by construction, not something enforced by a check that could be bypassed. `toggleConfirmAllMilestones(cycleId, itemId)` is a bulk convenience, not a separate source of truth — it confirms every not-yet-confirmed milestone on the item in one click, or un-confirms all of them if every one is already confirmed (same toggle shape). A zero-milestone item still confirms directly via `toggleReviewConfirm(cycleId, itemId)` into `cycle.confirmations`, exactly as it did before milestone-level confirmation existed — there's nothing to derive from. **A confirmation record is created once and only ever flipped, never spliced back out on un-confirm** — `setConfirmRecord()`/`toggleConfirmRecord()` (the shared mechanic behind `toggleReviewConfirm()`/`toggleMilestoneConfirm()`/`toggleConfirmAllMilestones()`) find-or-create the `{itemId|milestoneId, confirmed, updatedAt}` record for a given key and set/flip its `confirmed` flag — a later, explicit user follow-up to the "3 parallel reviewers" question below, since a plain presence/absence array can't carry an `updatedAt` on removal (the record's just gone), which is exactly what made the old shape un-mergeable across two concurrently-edited copies of the same cycle. `setConfirmRecord()` still never materializes a record at all when setting `confirmed:false` on a key with no existing one — a milestone auto-confirmed via its own Complete `status` (see `isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle()`'s own status shortcut, just above) must never pick up a redundant record purely from a bulk "confirm all" pass.

**A milestone (or zero-milestone item) whose own status is already `'complete'` auto-confirms — no manual click needed, with or without an actual entry in `cycle.milestoneConfirmations`/`cycle.confirmations`.** An explicit user request ("mark completed task as reviewed, no need to review them again"): once something is genuinely finished, a reviewer shouldn't have to re-click through it every single review cycle just to satisfy the "everything confirmed" gate — only milestones/items still actively open need attention. `isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle(cycle, m)` (its signature changed from taking a bare `milestoneId` to the whole milestone object, so it can read `m.status`) checks `m.status === 'complete'` *before* falling back to the stored-entry lookup — a milestone finished long before this review cycle even started, and never explicitly clicked in any cycle, still reads as confirmed today. `isItemConfirmedInCycle()`'s own zero-milestone branch gets the same treatment (`it.status === 'complete' || cycle.confirmations.some(...)`). This is deliberately the same shape as the existing `notApplicable` exclusion just below — "this doesn't need a reviewer's attention" — but for a different reason (genuinely done, vs. genuinely out of scope), so it's a separate check, not folded into the `notApplicable` one.

`toggleConfirmAllMilestones()` needed no logic change at all beyond passing the milestone object through — its existing `if (!isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle(cycle, m))` guard, before pushing a real confirmation entry, now naturally skips an already-Complete milestone the exact same way it already skipped an already-manually-confirmed one, so a bulk "confirm all" never writes a redundant entry for one. **Rendering-wise, a Complete milestone/zero-milestone-item gets its own inert, already-checked indicator** (`milestoneRowsHtml()`'s and `itemRowHtml()`'s review-cell branches both gained a `m.status === 'complete'` / `it.status === 'complete'` case, checked before the normal clickable-toggle branch) rather than the clickable circle a still-open, manually-confirmable milestone/item shows — the same "nothing here needs interaction" treatment `notApplicable` milestones already get in this exact column, just with the filled `fa-circle-check` icon instead of `fa-ban` (it *is* confirmed, not excluded). Only the zero-milestone case gets this treatment at the item level — an item *with* milestones keeps its real, clickable "confirm all" bulk button regardless of whether every underlying milestone happens to already be auto-confirmed, since clicking it in that case is just a harmless no-op, not worth a second inert-rendering branch.

**Completion gate, and why it needs no extra bookkeeping for mid-cycle adds.** `canCompleteReviewCycle(cycle)` checks that *every item currently in `cycle.workstreamId`* is confirmed per `isItemConfirmedInCycle()` — it re-derives the live item (and, for each, live milestone) list each call rather than snapshotting anything when the cycle started. This is what makes both "an item added mid-cycle automatically becomes an unconfirmed blocker" *and* "a milestone added mid-cycle to an already-confirmed item un-confirms it" fall out for free (per the product requirement this was built against): there's no separate "what does this cycle cover" list to keep in sync, so a newly-added item or milestone simply has no confirmation entry yet and the `.every()` checks naturally fail until someone confirms it. The flip side: an item (or milestone) *deleted* mid-cycle just stops being checked (its stray confirmation entry, if any, is harmless orphaned data — never pruned, never causes a crash since nothing indexes into `confirmations`/`milestoneConfirmations` by array position).

**"Check All" bulk-confirms the whole cycle in one action — an explicit user request ("in review mode, provide a check all option").** `confirmAllInReviewCycle(cycleId)` is `toggleConfirmAllMilestones()`'s cycle-wide counterpart: instead of one item's milestones, it sweeps every item in `cycle.workstreamId`, confirming every not-yet-confirmed applicable milestone (or, for a zero-milestone item, the item itself) via the same `setConfirmRecord()` primitive — `notApplicable` milestones excluded and an already-Complete milestone/item skipped exactly as `toggleConfirmAllMilestones()` already does, so a redundant confirmation record never gets created and an already-true value's `updatedAt` is never re-stamped (both matter for merge — see "Merge and last-write-wins" above).

**A genuine toggle, matching `toggleConfirmAllMilestones()`'s own shape one level up — a later, explicit user follow-up ("also allow to uncheck all") reversing this function's original strictly-one-directional design.** If `canCompleteReviewCycle(cycle)` is already true (everything confirmed), calling it un-confirms every applicable milestone/item across the whole workstream instead — same `setConfirmRecord()` primitive, just always writing `false`. **The two directions are gated asymmetrically, by design**: un-confirming is the safe, freely-reversible direction (it can't lose data, and just returns items to the honest "needs review" default) — no modal, applied immediately, the same "no gate on a non-destructive toggle" stance `toggleConfirmAllMilestones()`'s own un-confirm branch already takes. Confirming everything stays gated behind `openConfirmModal()` — an explicit part of the original request ("show a modal to double check with the user that really no updates are needed") — since skipping the normal item-by-item review is exactly the kind of action that should require a deliberate, described confirmation rather than a single accidental click. The button itself relabels between the two states ("Check All" / `fa-check-double`, vs. "Uncheck All" / `fa-rotate-left`) rather than ever rendering `disabled` — it always does something useful now, regardless of the cycle's current state.

**Repositioned to its own small, right-aligned toolbar directly above `.review-checklist` — a later, explicit user request ("move the check all above the check column")** — rather than sitting in the trailing button row alongside Complete/Cancel review. This puts it visually close to the review-confirm-toggle column it actually acts on (the rightmost column of every row, also right-aligned), instead of reading as one of the cycle's own terminal actions. Reviewer+ gated (the identical tier every other confirm control on this checklist already sits at) and omitted entirely — not just hidden via role, but the whole toolbar row — whenever the workstream has no items to confirm at all, the same "nothing to show, don't render an empty control" instinct this app already applies elsewhere.

`completeReviewCycle(cycleId)` re-checks `canCompleteReviewCycle()` itself (defense in depth — the UI already disables the button via the same check, but the guard lives in the mutating function, not just the render), snapshots the confirmation count (below), and sets `completedAt` — permanent once set, a cycle is never un-completed. The "Complete review" button itself reflects `canCompleteReviewCycle()` beyond just the `disabled` attribute — `.btn:disabled` is explicitly dimmed (`opacity:.4`) rather than relying on default/absent browser styling for a custom-colored button, and its `title` explains why it's inert when it is, so a reviewer isn't left wondering whether the button does nothing because of a bug or because something's still unconfirmed.

**A cancelled cycle isn't tracked at all.** `cancelReviewCycle(cycleId)` goes through the confirm modal (as before), but on confirm it splices the cycle out of `reviewCycles` entirely rather than marking a `cancelledAt` and keeping it — a review that got abandoned isn't a record worth keeping, unlike a completed one. This still frees the workstream for `startReviewCycle()` to open a new one, since `activeReviewCycle()` simply no longer finds a cycle there at all. `cancelledAt` still exists as a field (`normalizeData()` still backfills it, `activeReviewCycle()` still checks `!c.cancelledAt`, `reviewHistoryRowHtml()`'s callers still filter on it) purely so a cycle cancelled *before* this behavior existed — sitting in an older `pulse-data.json` with `cancelledAt` already set — stays correctly excluded from history and can't reactivate; nothing new ever sets that field again.

**Cancelling a review cycle is now tombstoned (`deletedReviewCycleIds`) — a user-reported gap** ("how will the app react if someone started a review and is actively working on it, and a second user cancelled the review?"), traced and confirmed live: since a cancelled cycle was never tombstoned at all, `mergeData()`'s reviewCycles merge — purely additive, only ever acting on what's *present* in incoming data (see its own comment) — never noticed a locally-known cycle going missing from another device's file. A device still actively working on the exact same cycle kept its own local copy completely untouched by the cancellation, with no indication anything happened — and, worse, that device's own very next save (any ordinary edit, like confirming a milestone) recomputed its workstream's file from its own still-intact local copy and wrote the "cancelled" cycle right back into the shared file, which the cancelling device then picked back up on its own next poll, silently undoing the cancellation. `cancelReviewCycle()` now calls `tombstone(deletedReviewCycleIds, cycleId)` alongside the splice, and `mergeData()` gained the same proactive sweep (plus the matching guard against a stale incoming copy re-adding a just-tombstoned cycle) items/milestones/`actionLog`/`decisionLog` already have.

**The sweep's escape hatch needed its own timestamp, since a review cycle — unlike an item — has no single top-level `updatedAt` to compare a tombstone's `deletedAt` against.** `reviewCycleLastTouchedAt(cycle)` reduces over every place activity on a cycle actually gets timestamped (`startedAt`, every `confirmations[].updatedAt`/`milestoneConfirmations[].updatedAt`, every `changeLog[].changedAt`, `minutes.updatedAt`, `completedAt`) to answer the same "how recently was this genuinely touched" question an item's own `updatedAt` answers for free. The sweep in `mergeData()` only removes a locally-known cycle when the tombstone's `deletedAt` is *at least as new* as `reviewCycleLastTouchedAt(cycle)` — a cycle nobody has touched since the cancellation is swept everywhere; one someone is still genuinely working on (a milestone confirmed, minutes edited, *after* the cancellation) survives, the identical "a genuine edit made elsewhere after the deletion wins" escape hatch items'/milestones' own sweeps already have. In practice this means: if the cancelling device cancelled *after* seeing the other device's activity (the common, informed case), the cancellation's own timestamp is the newer one and correctly wins; only genuinely concurrent, not-yet-synced activity can outrun a cancellation. Verified live with two real Chromium pages (Playwright) sharing one fake sync folder, reproducing the exact reported scenario end to end: before the fix, Device 2's cancellation never reached Device 1 (still mid-review), and Device 1's own next save (confirming a milestone) resurrected the cycle right back into the shared file, which Device 2 then picked back up — confirmed both symptoms on the pre-fix code, then confirmed the fix closes both; a second run confirmed the escape hatch itself, with Device 1's confirmation landing before Device 2's (informed) cancellation and the cancellation correctly still winning once it was the later of the two.

**History rows freeze a confirmation count at the moment a cycle closes, rather than recomputing it live.** `completeReviewCycle()` calls `snapshotReviewCycleCounts(cycle)` immediately before setting `completedAt`, storing `itemCountAtClose` and `confirmedCountAtClose` on the cycle itself. `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` then shows "X of Y confirmed" from these frozen numbers (omitted entirely for a cycle closed before this field existed — there's no way to know in hindsight what it would have shown). This can't be computed live from current data the way the *active* cycle's progress bar is: `isItemConfirmedInCycle()` derives confirmation from an item's *current* milestones, so a milestone added to an item long after some old cycle closed would otherwise make that closed cycle's history retroactively — and wrongly — show the item as unconfirmed for a review that happened before the milestone even existed. Snapshotting both the numerator and the denominator at close time is what keeps a completed cycle's history entry accurate regardless of anything that happens to the data afterward — including the item or its milestones being edited, or deleted entirely.

**Only the 5 most recent history entries show by default.** `renderReview()` sorts `reviewCyclesForWs(wsId).filter(c => c.completedAt)` newest-first and slices to `HISTORY_PAGE_SIZE` (5) unless `historyShowAllWs` (a `Set` of workstream ids, UI-only, mirroring `expandedItemIds`/`expandedHistoryIds`) has that workstream — `toggleHistoryShowAll(wsId)` flips it. Since the list is already sorted newest-first, `slice(0, 5)` naturally keeps the most recent ones and hides the oldest, so "Show N more" (N = however many are hidden) needs no separate query to know what it's revealing; once fully expanded the same button becomes "Show less" to collapse back, symmetric with every other expand/collapse control in the app.

**History also shows the actual changes made during the review, not just a count.** `logReviewChange(workstreamId, itemName, milestoneName, changeText, extra)` appends an entry to whichever cycle is currently `activeReviewCycle()` for that workstream — a no-op if none is active, which covers the overwhelming majority of edits made outside a review. `changeText` is always a plain-language fallback (used as the row's `title` tooltip); `extra` carries one of three structured shapes that `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` renders as something more visual than text:
- `tagChange: {field, oldValue, newValue}` — set by `cycleItemAttr()`. Rendered as the tag's own icon (`TAG_FIELD_ICONS[field]`) shown *twice*, once colored by `oldValue` and once by `newValue`, with an arrow between — the icon's shape identifies which of IT/Business/Budget changed, and seeing both colors side by side (not just the new one) makes the shift itself legible at a glance.
- `statusChange: {oldValue, newValue}` — set by `cycleMilestoneStatus()`. Rendered the same way but with two `.status-badge` pills (label + color, `cursor:default` overriding the base class's pointer cursor since these aren't clickable) instead of an icon — the same badge shown in the row itself, old value then new.
- `dateChange: {label, oldValue, newValue}` — set by `updateMilestoneDateField()`/`updateItemDateField()`. Rendered as two real `<input type="date" disabled>` fields with an arrow between, not formatted text — "no editable" is enforced by the browser itself refusing input, and it's the same date-field look used everywhere else in the app rather than a bespoke text format. For an actual-date change, the "former" value is whatever actual date was already there, or — the first time one's set — the milestone's own plan (due) date, so there's always a real reference point rather than "set to X" in isolation; clearing an actual date instead logs the plain `changeText` `"Cleared actual date"` with no `dateChange` at all (there's no meaningful before→after to show as fields).

Each entry's label is `"<item> — <milestone>"` for a milestone-level change (`e.milestoneName` set) or just the item name for an item-level one (tag/item-date changes have no milestone) — the same `"<item name> — <milestone name>"` convention Dashboard's attention feed already uses, so a milestone-level change is never ambiguous about which scope item it belongs to.

**Displayed order follows the scope item's own milestone order, not the order changes were logged in.** Every entry also carries `itemId`/`milestoneId` (stable ids, alongside the display-only `itemName`/`milestoneName`) purely so `orderedChangeLog(changeLog)` can re-sort for display: it groups entries by `itemId` (preserving each item's first-appearance order, so different items' changes are never interleaved) and, within one item's group, sorts by that milestone's *current* index in `it.milestones` — the same order the row itself displays them in. A reviewer jumping around a checklist rather than working straight down it would otherwise produce a change log that reads out of sequence relative to the item it's describing. An item-level entry (no `milestoneId`) or one whose milestone can no longer be found (deleted since) sorts to the front of its group (`indexOf` returns `-1` for both), with ties broken by original logged order since `Array.prototype.sort` is stable and there's nothing more meaningful to order them by.

**Only the net change per field is ever shown, not every intermediate edit — a user-reported observation ("history seems to track every change... shouldn't it only track the final change?"), fixed as a display-only collapse.** Before this, cycling a milestone's status through several values in a row, or correcting a mistyped date, logged (and showed) one row per click/edit — a reviewer bouncing Green→Amber→Red left both transitions visible in history, even though only the final Red actually mattered. `collapseChangeLogForCycle(changeLog)` groups one review cycle's own `changeLog` by `(itemId, milestoneId, field)` and reduces each group to a single synthesized entry: the *first* edit's `oldValue` paired with the *last* edit's `newValue` — so a reviewer reading history later sees "Not Started → At Risk" as one row, not "Not Started → On Track" followed by "On Track → At Risk". If a field was edited back to exactly the value it had at the start of the cycle (oldValue === newValue after collapsing), the whole group is dropped outright — a net no-op isn't a "change" worth showing at all. This only ever applies to the three structured change types (`tagChange`/`statusChange`/`dateChange`), which always carry a real `{oldValue, newValue}` pair to net together; a plain-text entry with no such pair (the Not Applicable toggle, a category switch) passes through unchanged, one row per edit, since there's nothing to synthesize a net value from. **This is deliberately display-only** — `logReviewChange()` and every one of its callers are completely untouched, so `cycle.changeLog` itself still genuinely stores every individual edit, exactly as before; `mergeReviewCycle()`'s own changeLog merge (a plain append-only union by id — see "Merge and last-write-wins" below) explicitly depends on entries never changing after creation, so collapsing at the storage layer instead would have meant reworking that merge too, for comparatively little benefit over just collapsing the view. **Must be scoped to one cycle's own `changeLog` at a time** — a status change logged in an old, already-completed cycle must never collapse together with one logged in today's brand-new cycle just because they happen to touch the same milestone; `workstreamChangeLogEntries()` (the cross-workstream Change Log tab's own flattening function, below) calls it once per cycle, before ever merging cycles together, for exactly this reason.

It deliberately fires regardless of whether the edit came from Planning's own rows or Review's — the record is of what changed *while the workstream was under review*, not only edits made through the Review screen specifically. Because it looks up the *active* cycle each time (not one passed in), nothing is ever logged against a cycle that's already completed or cancelled — an edit made afterward just isn't attributed to anything. `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` renders each entry's (collapsed) `changeLog` behind a chevron (reusing `.item-chevron` and the same collapsed-by-default pattern as an item's milestone list, `expandedHistoryIds`/`toggleHistoryExpanded()` mirroring `expandedItemIds`/`toggleItemExpanded()`) since a long-running cycle can accumulate many entries — the chevron itself only renders when `collapseChangeLogForCycle(c.changeLog).length > 0`, so a cycle whose every edit happened to net out to nothing shows the same plain, inert chevron placeholder as one that was never edited at all.

**Meeting minutes attach to a specific (completed) cycle, not the workstream or the programme.** `reviewCycle.minutes` is `null` until someone adds any (backfilled by `normalizeData()`'s reviewCycles pass, and set on the object literal at `startReviewCycle()` time too — both, since a freshly-created-this-session cycle is read before ever round-tripping through `normalizeData()`), otherwise `{summary, actionItems, decisions, nextSteps, importedAt}`. `summary`/`decisions`/`nextSteps` are plain strings; `actionItems` is an array of `{id, text, owner, dueDate}` — see below for why that one's structured and the other three aren't. `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` renders one icon-only `.row-icon-btn` per history row: an outline `fa-regular fa-file-lines` titled "Add meeting minutes" when `c.minutes` is null, or a filled, accent-colored `fa-solid fa-file-lines` titled "View meeting minutes" once it isn't — both call the same `openMinutesModal(cycleId)`, pre-filled from whatever's already there (or blank), so "add" and "view/edit" are the same modal and the same flow, not two separate UIs.

**The modal is one grid, not a form with a paste box bolted on top of it** (`.minutes-grid`, `grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr`, plain source order so the DOM order *is* the visual order): row 1 is the paste-to-auto-fill box (with its own "Auto-fill" button inside the same cell) beside Meeting Summary; row 2 is Action Items, spanning the *full* width via `.minutes-action-items-field { grid-column: 1 / -1 }` — its three-column table needs the room a half-width cell can't give it; row 3 is Decisions beside Next Steps. Five cells total, laid out to a hand-drawn wireframe the user provided (an earlier iteration also had a same-width "Open Points" field paired with Next Steps in row 3 — dropped in a later revision, see the same wireframe-driven request; **there is no Open Points field anywhere in this app, and it must not come back as one** — confirmed explicitly more than once). The modal itself is a fixed `900px` (not `.modal-wide`, widened from an original `700px` after the paste box grew taller — see below — so the other fields kept comfortable width rather than being squeezed narrower).

**`MINUTES_SECTIONS` recognizes `openPoints`/`attendees`/`discussionPoints` headers, despite there being no field anywhere in this app to put their content in.** Without recognizing one of these at all, that heading in a real source document wouldn't read as a section boundary, so `current` would stay whatever it was before, and everything under it would keep getting appended to *that* section instead of being excluded. `openPoints` was the original case (a real bug report: many minutes templates have an Open Points section between Action Items/Decisions and Next Steps, and it was leaking into whichever of those came first). `attendees`/`discussionPoints` is the same shape of bug, reported later: a narrative-style minutes doc with an "Attendees" list and a "Discussion Points" section, but no literal "Summary" header anywhere, matched *neither* recognized word at all — so `anyHeaderFound` stayed `false` and the "no headers recognized anywhere" fallback (below) dumped the intro paragraph *and* the attendee list *and* every discussion bullet into Summary wholesale ("meeting summary currently takes too much... other sections bleed into summary"). The fix generalizes the same mechanism to all three: `MINUTES_EXCLUDED_SECTIONS` (a `Set` of `'openPoints'`/`'attendees'`/`'discussionPoints'`) are recognized keys in `MINUTES_SECTIONS` (so the header still ends whatever section came before it, and still counts toward `anyHeaderFound`) but have no entry in `parseMeetingMinutes()`'s `buckets` object — the header-match branch skips pushing its own inline "rest" text (`!MINUTES_EXCLUDED_SECTIONS.has(current)` guard) and the per-line push is skipped outright (`if (MINUTES_EXCLUDED_SECTIONS.has(current)) return;`) for every line until the next *real* header. Net effect: content under any of these three is read past and discarded, never stored, never leaked into a neighboring section — and, for a doc like the Attendees/Discussion-Points one above that has no actual Summary/Decisions/Action-Items/Next-Steps header at all, every field correctly ends up empty rather than stuffed with the wrong content, same as any other structured-but-Summary-less document (see "unlabeled lead-in text before the first recognized header is dropped" below) — the unlabeled intro paragraph is still discarded rather than guessed at, since recognizing Attendees/Discussion Points as real headers means this document no longer counts as "no headers recognized anywhere" either.
