**This introduces no new data of its own.** A change already lives on the review cycle it was logged against (`cycle.changeLog`, written by `logReviewChange()` — see above), and until now was only ever visible nested behind that one cycle's own History row (`reviewHistoryRowHtml()`, expand-on-click). `workstreamChangeLogEntries(wsId)` runs each cycle's own `changeLog` through `collapseChangeLogForCycle()` first (see "Only the net change per field is ever shown" above — one cycle at a time, never across cycles), then flattens `reviewCyclesForWs(wsId)`'s (now-collapsed) entries across every cycle (active or completed — a cancelled cycle is deleted outright, see `cancelReviewCycle()`, so there's nothing to filter out) into one list, sorted by `changedAt` descending. This is deliberately *not* `orderedChangeLog()`'s own item-grouped order (used for a single cycle's own expanded history row) — that ordering only makes sense relative to one item's milestone list; a feed spanning every cycle of every workstream has no single item list to order against, so plain chronological is the only ordering that stays meaningful at this scope. `allWorkstreamsChangeLogHtml()` is `allWorkstreamsActionLogHtml()`'s counterpart: flattens `workstreamChangeLogEntries()` across every workstream into `{w, e}` pairs, re-sorts the merged list by `changedAt` (a per-workstream sort doesn't imply a correct merged-sort order), and renders through the same row renderer with `showWs: true`.

**The rendering itself was extracted, not reimplemented — `changeLogValueHtml(e)`/`changeLogEntryLabel(e)` are the exact tag-icon-pair/status-badge-pair/date-input-pair/plain-text logic `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` always had, pulled into shared functions so both views render a change identically.** `reviewHistoryRowHtml()` was refactored to call them instead of inlining the same `if (e.tagChange) … else if (e.statusChange) … else if (e.dateChange) …` chain a second time — one definition of what a change "looks like", regardless of which of the two views (one cycle's own expanded history, or the cross-workstream feed) is showing it.

**`changeLogRowHtml(w, e, showWs)` reuses `.review-history-row`'s own flex layout rather than a fixed-column grid** — unlike Action Log/Decision Log's plain-text cells, a change's own value is inherently variable-width (an icon pair, a badge pair, a pair of date inputs, or a plain string), which doesn't sit well in a fixed grid column the way a text cell does; flexbox with the label at `flex:1` (pushing the value and timestamp to the right, the same spacer trick `reviewHistoryRowHtml()`'s own top-level row already uses) sidesteps that entirely. `showWs` prepends a workstream dot+name using the exact same `.action-log-ws` markup/class Action Log's own `showWs` branch uses, just as an inline-styled flex row rather than a grid cell (`style="width:130px;flex:0 0 auto"`) — same look, different layout mechanism underneath. `changeLogCountHtml(list)` mirrors `actionLogCountHtml()`/`decisionLogCountHtml()`'s own `.review-progress` count badge ("N changes"), shown in `.review-header` next to the workstream name (or "All Workstreams").

## Dashboard mode

`renderDashboard()` is read-only — nothing on it mutates state, it only computes and displays — and, like Review, is scoped by the shared `filterWorkstreamId`: `visibleWorkstreams()` (the same helper Planning's status board already used) narrows everything on the page to just the selected workstream, or the whole programme if "All Workstreams" is selected. `allMilestonesFlat(itemList)` takes an optional pre-filtered item list for this reason (defaults to the global `items` if omitted) rather than always flattening every milestone in the programme. Three cards up top: **Item Status** (item count *within the current scope* + a `.dash-bar` stacked bar of RAG counts, colored via the same `--stat-*` variables as everywhere else, plus a text legend), **Milestone Completion** (`% complete` across the scoped items' milestones), and **Overdue for Review** (count + names of *scoped* workstreams where `isReviewOverdue()` is true — the same helper the shared sidebar's badge uses, shared rather than duplicated; scoping to one workstream makes this card either 0 or 1). Below that: a per-workstream summary table (item count + milestone completion % — only rows for workstreams in scope), then two side-by-side attention-needed feeds — **Overdue** (scoped items/milestones with `status !== 'complete'`, that haven't already happened, and `dueDate` before today) and **Upcoming** (same, but `dueDate` within the next 30 days) — each entry sorted by date and, for a milestone, labeled `"<item name> — <milestone name>"` so it's clear which item it belongs to without a nested list. **The "hasn't already happened" half of that check is a later, explicit user request** ("also consider the actual date to determine if a milestone is overdue and upcoming") — `actualDate` is deliberately manual and independent of `status` (see "Data model" above: it's never auto-set when `status` becomes `'complete'`, and setting one never auto-completes `status` either), so something can genuinely already be finished — an `actualDate` recorded — while its `status` field lags behind and still reads e.g. `'red'`/`'not-started'`. Without this exclusion, such an item/milestone would keep nagging as Overdue/Upcoming despite already being done in practice — the same "`actualDate` is the ground-truth signal of completion timing, `status` alone can't be fully trusted" reasoning `isCompletedLate()` already applies elsewhere in this app.

**`hasHappened(actualDate)` (`!!actualDate && actualDate <= today`) is deliberately not a bare truthiness check on `actualDate` — a user-reported gap, fixed on both feeds.** An `actualDate` is sometimes logged *ahead of time* as a plan — the identical "sometimes logged ahead of time" case `isCompletedLate()`'s own `actualLate` flag already accounts for via its own `<= todayStr()` gate (see "Delayed-milestone color coding" above) — and a plan that hasn't happened yet isn't the same as something genuinely finished. The original check was a bare `!it.actualDate`/`!m.actualDate`, which treated *any* `actualDate` — past or future — as "already done," so a milestone due soon with a forward-looking `actualDate` logged against it (a planned early or on-time finish, not yet arrived) silently vanished from Upcoming. `hasHappened()` only treats the date as a completed fact once it's today or earlier — `<=`, matching `actualLate`'s own "today already counts as having happened" convention — so a still-future `actualDate` no longer suppresses Upcoming. Both feeds are computed inside `computeDashboardData()` (see below), so the Exec Summary export — which reuses that same function's output rather than recomputing independently — picks up this fix for free.

**Overdue specifically goes one step further, weighing a *revised* (later) `actualDate` too — a further, explicit user request ("if actual date is newer and not yet overdue, do not show as overdue").** `isOverdue(dueDate, actualDate)` is Overdue's own predicate (Upcoming keeps the plain `hasHappened()`-gated check above, unchanged — this refinement was scoped to Overdue only): it first rules out anything `hasHappened()` already excludes (genuinely finished, or a same/earlier-than-due `actualDate` that's now in the past), then checks whether `actualDate` is later than `dueDate` — a revision that pushes the target out. Once such a revision is logged, the *original* `dueDate` is superseded; being past that stale date no longer means anything is actually late, so `isOverdue()` returns `false` in that case regardless of `dueDate`. Only once the *revised* target itself has passed does the item read as overdue again — and by then `hasHappened()` (checked first) already excludes it, on the reasoning that an `actualDate` in the past means it genuinely got finished then, however late. `isOverdue()`'s final fallback, `dueDate < today`, is only ever reached when there's no `actualDate` at all, or the one that's set isn't a later revision (an on-time/early actual finish, which `hasHappened()` would already have excluded if it's in the past) — so the plain `dueDate` check is still exactly right there.

**The item-level half of both feeds is restricted to zero-milestone items — a user-reported duplicate.** An item with at least one milestone has its own `dueDate`/`status` *computed* from those milestones (see "Item plan date range roll-up" above) — so before this fix, an overdue/upcoming milestone-bearing item produced two rows on the same feed for the exact same underlying lateness: one from the item-level check (`scopedItems.filter(...)`, labeled with just the item's own name) and one from the milestone-level check (`milestones.filter(...)`, labeled `"<item> — <milestone>"`), since the two checks were never de-duplicated against each other. Both `overdue`'s and `upcoming`'s item-level filters now start with `!it.milestones.length &&` — a milestone-bearing item is represented only by whichever of its own milestones is actually late/upcoming, never by a redundant row for itself on top of that. A genuinely zero-milestone item is untouched by this — it has no milestone row to stand in for it, so it still gets its own item-level entry exactly as before; this only suppresses the *item*-level row for an item that already has a *milestone*-level row covering the same thing.

There's no persisted dashboard state and no separate "refresh" action — every value is recomputed from `items`/`workstreams`/`reviewCycles` (filtered through `visibleWorkstreams()`) at render time, so it's automatically current whenever `render()` calls it (i.e. every time `setMode('dashboard')` runs, `setFilterWorkstream()` changes the scope, or any other mutation happens while already in Dashboard mode).

An L1 Plan (see "L1 Plans" above) never shows up in any of this, with no special-casing needed: `scopedItems` is filtered to `wsIds.has(it.workstreamId)`, and an L1 Plan's `workstreamId` is always `null` — the same reason Review mode's own per-workstream item list never picks one up either.

**Every Overdue/Upcoming row is clickable, opening that entry's own item modal — a user-reported gap** ("jumping from '\<item\> — \<milestone\>, overdue' to actually editing it means going back to Planning and hunting for it"). `allMilestonesFlat()` now also attaches `itemId: it.id` to each flattened milestone (alongside the `itemName`/`workstreamId` it already carried) specifically so a milestone-level feed entry can still resolve back to its *parent item's* id, not just display its parent's name; `computeDashboardData()`'s own `toEntry(x, isMilestone)` reads `isMilestone ? x.itemId : x.id` into each entry's own `itemId`. `renderDashboard()`'s `attentionHtml()` wires each row's `onclick` straight to `openItemModal(itemId)` — never role-gated (see "UI gating by role" above: viewing an item's full detail is always allowed regardless of role, only *editing* it is gated), so this works identically at every role, and closing the modal leaves Dashboard exactly as it was, without ever having to navigate to Planning first. `.attention-row-link` (`cursor: pointer` + a `:hover` background) is the new class carrying the click affordance, layered onto the existing `.attention-row` — mirroring `.review-date-row`'s own "reuse `.attention-row`'s layout, just clickable" treatment for `reviewDatesOverviewHtml()`'s rows (see "Review mode" below) rather than inventing a second pattern for the same idea.

**A row's own displayed date prefers `actualDate` over `dueDate` once one's set — a user-reported gap.** `toEntry()` used to hardcode `date: x.dueDate` regardless of `actualDate` — so a milestone/item that stayed on Upcoming specifically *because* it had a still-future `actualDate` (see `hasHappened()`'s own paragraph just above — a plan logged ahead of time, not yet arrived) kept showing its plain, now-superseded Due date, never the more relevant Actual one that was the whole reason it was still on the list. `toEntry()` now sets `date: x.actualDate || x.dueDate` and records `isActual: !!x.actualDate` alongside it; `attentionHtml()` renders a small muted `(actual)` qualifier (`.attention-date-actual`) next to the date whenever `isActual` is true, and swaps the row's `title` tooltip between "Actual completion date"/"Due date" to match — so the row is never ambiguous about which kind of date it's showing. **This is a no-op for Overdue's own rows in practice, not just in theory** — `isOverdue()`'s own gating (see its comment just above) already guarantees a surviving Overdue entry never carries a real `actualDate` to prefer: any `actualDate` that's already happened excludes the row entirely via `hasHappened()`, and any `actualDate` that's a still-future revision past `dueDate` excludes it via the revised-target check, so by the time an Overdue row is actually rendered, `x.actualDate` is always falsy and `date` still resolves to `dueDate` exactly as before. Only Upcoming rows can ever actually show the `(actual)` qualifier. Both the Exec Summary PDF's `execSummaryFeedRows()` and the PNG's `drawExecSummaryCanvas()` read `e.date` the same generic way `attentionHtml()` does, so both exports pick up the more-relevant date for free — neither renders the `(actual)` qualifier itself, though, since that's Dashboard-page-only markup, not part of either export's own inline-styled/hand-drawn output.

**`renderDashboard()`'s own computation was extracted into `computeDashboardData()`** (returning `{wss, scopedItems, milestones, itemCounts, totalItems, msDone, msPct, overdueReviewWs, overdue, upcoming, wsStats, dependencies}`), which `renderDashboard()` now just destructures and renders — done specifically so the Exec Summary export below (`buildExecSummaryHtml()`/`drawExecSummaryCanvas()`) can share the exact same numbers rather than recomputing them a second, independently-drifting way. `wsStats` is the per-workstream-row data (`{w, itemCount, msTotal, msDone, msPct}` per workstream in scope) that used to be computed inline inside the old `wsRows.map()`.

**Dashboard has its own two sub-tabs — Overview and Dependencies — a later, explicit user request** ("Move Dependency Tracker into a separate Dashboard tab"), reached via `dashboardTab` (`'overview' | 'dependencies'`, module-level, UI-only, not persisted — the exact same shape `planningTab`/`reviewTab` already established for their own sub-tabs) and `setDashboardTab(t)`. `#dashboardModeToolbar` (a `.planning-toolbar`/`.view-tabs` block, `#tabDashboardOverview`/`#tabDashboardDependencies`) sits above `#main` the same static-toolbar-outside-`#main` way Planning's/Review's own sub-tab rows do, shown whenever `mode === 'dashboard'` regardless of which sub-tab is active. `#dashboardToolbar` (the Export Exec Summary dropdown) is additionally gated to `dashboardTab === 'overview'` only — the export doesn't cover the Dependencies tracker's own content (see below), so showing the button while looking at that tab would imply otherwise. `render()`'s own `mode === 'dashboard'` branch toggles each tab button's `active` class before calling `renderDashboard()`, the identical pattern `planningTab`/`reviewTab` already use. `renderDashboard()` itself dispatches early: the empty-programme guard (`workstreams.length === 0`) still applies to both tabs, but past that, `dashboardTab === 'dependencies'` renders *only* the Dependencies table and returns, before any of the Overview cards/per-workstream summary/Overdue-Upcoming markup is even built.

**A dedicated cross-workstream tracker for every scoped item flagged `dependency: true` — a later, explicit user request, since folded into its own tab per the paragraph above.** `dependencies` (built in `computeDashboardData()`, shared by both tabs' render paths even though only the Dependencies tab currently uses it) is a flat list of `{itemId, label, workstreamName, spoc, date, status, overdue}`, one entry per dependency item, **excluding anything already `status: 'complete'`** — the same exclusion the Overdue/Upcoming feeds apply, for the same reason: this is meant to stay a short, actionable "what's still outstanding" list, not a growing historical log (Excel export and Planning's own per-workstream Dependencies sub-section — see "Dependencies" above — already cover the complete picture, completed items included). Sorted soonest-due-first with undated ones last (`'9999-99-99'` fallback, the same sentinel `sortedActionLog()` already uses for its own open-item sort), **not** the actual-date-aware exclusion the Overdue/Upcoming feeds use — a dependency's own `overdue` flag is computed independently per entry (`!it.actualDate && it.dueDate < today`) rather than filtering the list itself, since every dependency (overdue or not) belongs in this tracker, just flagged differently.

`renderDashboard()`'s `dependencyHtml` (built and rendered only on the Dependencies tab) renders these as their own small table — `Item / Workstream / SPOC / Due / Status` — reusing `.attention-row-link` for the identical clickable-row treatment the Overdue/Upcoming feeds already have (`openItemModal()`, never role-gated), but needs its own bespoke column set rather than `.attention-row`'s simple 3-part shape. `.dash-dep-header`/`.dash-dep-row` share one `grid-template-columns` with pinned widths (`minmax(160px,1fr) 150px 150px 90px 110px`) — the same "header and data rows must agree on a fixed column width, auto-sizing silently misaligns them" lesson Action Log's own header row already had to learn (see "Action Log" below) applied here from the outset rather than discovered later. An overdue entry's Due cell gets the same red/bold treatment the Overdue feed's own rows imply, inline (`color:var(--stat-red)`) rather than a shared class, since this is the only place in this table that needs it. A missing SPOC renders as a plain "—", matching every other "nothing recorded yet" placeholder in this app.

**A companion per-category rollup table (`catStats`, "Per-category summary") was built alongside the Dependencies tracker in the same original commit, then removed outright on a later, explicit user request ("remove the Per-category summary") — not hidden, not folded into a tab, gone.** `computeDashboardData()` no longer computes it, `allMilestonesFlat()`'s milestones no longer carry a `categoryId` field (it existed solely for that computation), and `renderDashboard()` has no `catRows`/"Per-category summary" markup left at all. If a category-cut rollup is ever wanted again, it would need to be rebuilt from scratch rather than resurrected — nothing here still references it.

**Deliberately not extended to the Exec Summary PDF/PNG export** — `buildExecSummaryHtml()`/`drawExecSummaryCanvas()` keep destructuring `computeDashboardData()`'s original field set only (`wss, itemCounts, totalItems, msDone, msPct, milestones, overdueReviewWs, overdue, upcoming, wsStats`), untouched by the Dependencies addition. Both exports are meant to stay a single printable/postable page; adding a whole extra section would mean redesigning the PDF's page layout and the canvas renderer's own height/column math (`execSummaryCanvasHeight()`, `execSummaryFeedRowCount()`) for a feature that was only asked for on the live Dashboard. A genuine, separate follow-up if ever wanted, not an oversight here.

### Export Dashboard as Exec Summary (PDF/PNG)

**A one-page, glance-able export of the live Dashboard — an explicit user request, distinct from both the plain-data Export and the full Excel report (see "Export to Excel" above).** Those two are complete, lossless data dumps meant to be re-imported or pored over row by row; this is the opposite: a single formatted page meant to be printed, emailed, or dropped straight into a status deck, built from the exact numbers `computeDashboardData()` already computes for the live Dashboard (see above) — never a separately-maintained recomputation that could silently drift from what's on screen.

Reachable only from Dashboard mode's own toolbar, `#dashboardToolbar` (a `.planning-toolbar` sibling to Planning's search box and Review's sub-tabs, shown/hidden by `render()` the same way — see "Rendering pattern" above) — a right-aligned `.data-dropdown` (`#execExportDropdown`, reusing that CSS class/markup pattern from the topbar's own Data menu rather than inventing a second copy) offering "Download as PDF" (`exportExecSummaryPdf()`) and "Download as PNG" (`exportExecSummaryPng()`). `toggleExecExportMenu()`/`closeExecExportMenu()` mirror `toggleDataMenu()`/`closeDataMenu()` exactly, including their own line in the shared document-level click-outside-closes listener.

**Always rendered with a fixed, literal light palette — never the live page's current `--stat-*`/`--ws-*`/dark-mode CSS custom properties** — the same "looks the same regardless of the active color scheme" convention Excel export already established (see "Export to Excel" above). `argbToHex(argb)` strips the leading alpha byte off one of `EXCEL_RAG_COLORS`' own ARGB hex strings to reuse those exact values here rather than hardcoding the same RGB numbers a third time; `WS_COLOR_HEX` is this file's first literal-hex table for workstream colors (Excel export never needed one, since it doesn't color workstreams by workstream) and mirrors the Standard scheme's own `:root` values exactly. `EXEC_SUMMARY_FEED_CAP` (8) caps the Overdue/Upcoming lists — unlike the live Dashboard's own feed, which always shows every entry, this is meant to fit on one printed page regardless of programme size, so each list shows at most 8 entries plus a "+N more" line once truncated. `programmeFileSlug()` (factored out of what used to be Excel export's own inline `safeName` computation, now shared by both) is the "slugify the programme name, fall back to `'Pulse'` if nothing alphanumeric survives" logic both exports' filenames use.

**PDF and PNG are two completely independent renderers, not one renderer feeding two output paths — a late, deliberate correction after the more obvious "one shared renderer" approach turned out to be broken in this app's own target browsers.**

- **PDF** (`exportExecSummaryPdf()`) uses the browser's own native "print to PDF" via `window.print()` — no library, matching this app's zero-dependency stance and the same "use what the platform already gives you" precedent Excel export's own ExcelJS exception sits alongside (there the platform didn't have an answer, so one deliberate dependency was added; here it already does, so none was needed). `buildExecSummaryHtml()` returns one self-contained, inline-styled HTML fragment (built from `computeDashboardData()`, no CSS classes at all — every color/spacing value is inline) which gets injected into `#execSummarySheet`, a hidden element sitting in the static HTML body (`display:none` at rest — see "Modals" above for the sibling pattern of a handful of always-present-but-hidden containers). `.printing-exec-summary` (added to `document.body` for the moment `window.print()` is open, removed by `cleanup()` right after — via the `afterprint` event, with a 5s `setTimeout` backstop in case that event doesn't fire in some browser/context) is what a `@media print` rule keys off to hide the *rest* of the page and show only `#execSummarySheet` — see the `<style>` block's own comment for the exact selectors. Setting `document.title` for the duration (restored by the same `cleanup()`) is what gives the OS "Save as PDF" dialog a sensible suggested filename, since `window.print()` has no direct equivalent of a Blob's own `<a download>` filename.
- **PNG** (`exportExecSummaryPng()`) is a hand-drawn Canvas 2D renderer (`drawExecSummaryCanvas(ctx, d)`) — **deliberately not** "rasterize `buildExecSummaryHtml()`'s own markup via an SVG `<foreignObject>`, then read the canvas back out," which was the first approach actually built and shipped, then reverted after direct testing in real Chrome (headless, via CDP) surfaced a hard blocker: Chrome unconditionally taints any canvas that ever drew an SVG image containing a `<foreignObject>` — `canvas.toBlob()`/`toDataURL()` then throws `"Tainted canvases may not be exported"` **every single time**, regardless of fonts, CORS, or same-origin `blob:` URLs. This is a deliberate Chrome security policy, not a bug with a workaround — Firefox allows the foreignObject-to-canvas technique, but Chrome/Edge (the only browsers this app targets at all, per the File System Access sync feature elsewhere in this file) do not, so a browser-detection fallback wasn't an option either. Drawing directly with `fillText`/`fillRect`/`arc`/`stroke` never loads anything as an image in the first place, so there's nothing for Chrome to taint the canvas over.
  - Since nothing here is measured from real rendered layout the way the HTML path is (no DOM, no reflow), every row/section height is a fixed constant tuned to this exact font/size combination. `execSummaryCanvasHeight(d)` computes the total canvas height *before* the canvas is ever created (a canvas's `width`/`height` can't be `"auto"` the way a plain HTML element's can) — deliberately kept pure (no `ctx`, no DOM at all), which is what makes it (along with `execSummaryFeedRowCount()`, the "how many visual rows does this feed take — capped entries, the '+N more' line, or the empty-state line" helper it's built on) directly unit-testable despite the rest of this export path needing a real canvas/`window.print()` to exercise at all.
  - `fitCanvasText(ctx, text, maxWidth)` binary-searches for the longest prefix (plus an ellipsis) that still fits `maxWidth` — canvas text has no built-in `text-overflow: ellipsis` the way the HTML path gets for free, so a long item/workstream name needs this instead of silently overflowing its column and running into whatever's drawn next to it.
  - `exportExecSummaryPng()` itself feature-detects via `document.createElement('canvas').getContext` (rather than, say, checking for `Image`, which the abandoned foreignObject approach depended on but the current one no longer touches at all) before doing anything else, showing a toast and returning early if it's missing.

**Testing caveat, matching the file-sync functions' own documented gap**: `exportExecSummaryPdf()`/`exportExecSummaryPng()`/`drawExecSummaryCanvas()` themselves touch `window.print()`/`canvas.getContext('2d')`/real font-metric `measureText()` — none of which this project's JXA test harness mocks — so only their own early feature-detection guards (which fire safely under the harness's fake `window`/`document.createElement`) are exercised by `tests/cases/dashboard.test.js`. Everything with no such dependency *is* tested directly: `buildExecSummaryHtml()` (content, the "+N more" cap, the empty-feed fallback, and a balanced-tag well-formedness check), `execSummaryScopeLabel()`, `programmeFileSlug()`, `argbToHex()`, `execSummaryCanvasHeight()`, and `execSummaryFeedRowCount()`. Verified beyond that with a real headless Chromium instance (the same CDP-driven verification pattern used earlier in this project's history for the `.btn-primary:hover` bug) — which is what caught the foreignObject-tainting issue in the first place, and confirmed the final Canvas 2D renderer produces a clean, correctly laid out image with no such error.

### Dashboard: Gantt chart

**A timeline view — an explicit user request ("build a gant chart view per workstream, all workstream and l1 level") — Dashboard's third sub-tab, `dashboardTab === 'gantt'`, alongside Overview and Dependencies.** Workstream/All Workstreams scoping reuses the *existing* shared sidebar filter (`filterWorkstreamId`/`visibleWorkstreams()`, the same mechanism Overview/Dependencies already scope themselves by) — no new UI needed for that half.

**An L1 Plans version of this same feature went through several iterations — an internal scope toggle inside this same Dashboard tab, then its own separate sub-tab under L1 Plans, then a milestone-row redesign of that tab — before being removed again entirely on a further, later, explicit user request ("remove gantt for l1").** Gantt is Dashboard/Workstreams-only now; L1 Plans has no Gantt tab or view of its own. If this comes back, it should be a deliberate, fresh re-add rather than resurrecting the removed code — none of the L1-specific functions (`renderL1GanttHtml()`, `ganttL1MilestoneSections()`, the old `ganttL1Rows()`) exist anymore.

**What a row's bar represents was confirmed directly with the user before building this**: a scope item's own bar spans its Start→Due range, with each of its milestones layered on top as its own small diamond marker — not a bar-only view with no milestone detail, and not a milestone-only timeline with no continuous bars at all.

`renderGanttHtml()` — `renderGanttChartHtml(ganttWorkstreamSections(), 'No dated items to show yet.')`. `renderDashboard()` dispatches to it before `computeDashboardData()` even runs, since none of that computation is needed here (unlike the Dependencies tab, which dispatches *after*, since it reads `computeDashboardData()`'s own `dependencies` field). `#dashboardToolbar` (the Export Exec Summary dropdown) stays hidden on this tab, the same `dashboardTab === 'overview'`-only gate Dependencies already has. `renderGanttChartHtml(sections, emptyMessage)` is kept as its own separate function (a plain function of `sections`, each `{name, color, rows}`, and an empty-state message) purely for a clean separation between "gather/lay out the rows" and "decide what data feeds the chart" — even though it has only the one real caller today, following the L1 removal.

**The date math is all pure, UTC-based day-number arithmetic, deliberately not the local-time `new Date(iso + 'T00:00:00')` parse every *display-facing* date elsewhere in this app uses** (see "Every inline date field renders as a 'pill'" above for why display stays local). `ganttDayNumber(iso)` (`Math.floor(Date.UTC(y, m-1, d) / DAY_MS)`) is the one primitive everything else is built on — since these values are only ever compared and subtracted to compute a bar/marker's own left%/width%, never shown to a user, a fixed timezone-independent reference point is what actually matters: two devices in different timezones computing the same bar's position from the same ISO string must land on the identical number, which a local-time parse doesn't guarantee across a DST boundary the way a UTC one does.

- `ganttDateRange(isoDates)` — the padded `{start, end}` day-number range the whole chart shares (returns `null` for an empty scope). Padding is `Math.max(3, Math.round(span * 0.04))` on each side, so a bar/marker sitting exactly at the earliest/latest date never renders flush against the chart's own edge, and a range never collapses to zero width even for a single repeated date.
- `ganttPercent(iso, range)` — a date's left% position, clamped to `[0,100]`.
- `ganttPercentOrNull(iso, range)` — the "today" line's own counterpart, which deliberately does **not** clamp: unlike a real data point (always inside the padded range by construction), "today" can genuinely fall outside a chart built from historical or far-future dates, and clamping it to an edge would misleadingly draw the line as if today sat right at the start/end of the plan rather than nowhere near it. `null` means "don't draw it at all" — verified live (a chart built entirely from 400-500-day-old dates correctly renders no today-line at all; a current-dated chart does).
- `ganttBarRect(startIso, endIso, range)` — a bar's `{left, width}`%, with `width` floored at `0.6` so a single-day (`start === end`) bar still renders as a visible sliver rather than collapsing to nothing.
- `ganttTicks(range)` — one tick per calendar boundary of an *auto-picked granularity* the range actually touches (not per boundary the range merely overlaps — a range starting mid-period correctly has no tick for the one it's already partway through), each carrying its own left% (computed against the exact same `range` every bar/marker uses) and a granularity-appropriate label. Originally always one tick per calendar month (`ganttMonthTicks()`), regardless of how long the overall range was — **a real, user-reported bug**: a genuine multi-year "All Workstreams" chart (several workstreams' worth of real items) produced one label per month packed into the same fixed axis width, every one of them overlapping its neighbors into unreadable, garbled text ("timeline does not look to nice. maybe scale to quarters?"). `ganttTickGranularity(range)` now picks month/quarter/year from the range's own total span (`months = (range.end - range.start) / (365.25/12)`; `> 48` → year, `> 14` → quarter, else month) — the same "zoom out the label granularity as the visible span grows" idiom any real timeline UI already uses, just as fixed tiers rather than continuous zoom, since this chart has no interactive zoom control of its own. Quarter/year ticks align to real calendar boundaries (Jan/Apr/Jul/Oct for quarter, always Jan for year), reading as genuine "Q1 2026"/"2026" marks rather than an arbitrary offset grid; month ticks keep the original `toLocaleDateString(undefined, {month:'short', year:'numeric'})` "Mon YYYY" label. Capped at 240 iterations regardless of granularity (20 years of month ticks, 60 of quarter, 240 of year) as a cheap safety net, not a real constraint any genuine Gantt scope would approach.

**Fixing the granularity alone didn't fully solve the report — a second, distinct bug, found once the fix above was already verified live: even at the coarsest sensible granularity, a genuinely long, many-tick range can still produce more ticks than comfortably fit in whatever width the chart's own container happens to have, and since no tick label was otherwise width- or spacing-constrained, they still ran together with zero gap between them.** `ganttChartMinWidth(tickCount)` (`GANTT_LABEL_COL_WIDTH + Math.max(400, tickCount * GANTT_MIN_PX_PER_TICK)`, `GANTT_MIN_PX_PER_TICK = 80`) computes a real, hand-tuned minimum pixel width from the *actual* tick count for the current chart — the same "pin a real, computed `min-width` and let `overflow-x:auto`/`.main`'s own scroll reach the rest" convention this app already applies to every other fixed-column grid it renders (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above), just computed dynamically here since tick count is data-dependent rather than a fixed column count. `renderGanttChartHtml()` sets it as an inline `style="min-width:...px"` directly on `.gantt-chart` — `#main` already has `overflow: auto`, so pinning this width is all that's needed for a long chart to become horizontally scrollable rather than visually overlapping. The `400px` floor keeps a short, few-tick chart from claiming an oddly wide minimum it doesn't actually need.
- `ganttRowDates(rows)` — flattens every row's own bar start/end plus every milestone marker date into one array, so `ganttDateRange()` can compute a *single* combined range across every section/row — this is what makes the axis and every bar/marker line up on one consistent timeline, rather than each workstream's own section picking an incompatible scale of its own.

`ganttWorkstreamSections()` — one row per scope item currently in view (workstream-scoped by the shared sidebar filter), grouped into a section per workstream. A row needs a real Start+Due (a zero-milestone item's own manual range, or the range already rolled up from its milestones — see "Item plan date range roll-up" above) to get a bar; every applicable (`!notApplicable`), dated milestone becomes its own marker regardless of whether the item itself has a bar. An item with *neither* is dropped outright — there's no meaningful position to place it at on a timeline. Unassigned items are excluded (`workstreamId` never matches a real workstream's own id in this scan), the identical "not real workstream-scoped data" treatment `computeDashboardData()`'s own `scopedItems` already gives them. A workstream that ends up with zero qualifying rows is dropped from the result entirely, rather than rendering an empty section header with nothing underneath it.

**`ganttRowHtml(row, range, todayPct)` builds one row's markup** — a fixed-width, clickable label column (`.gantt-row-label`, wired to `openItemModal(row.id)` — never role-gated, viewing an item's detail is always allowed) plus a flexible track column holding the bar (if any), every milestone marker, and the today-line, all positioned by the one shared `range`/`todayPct` threaded in from the caller rather than recomputed per row.

**`.gantt-axis-row` and `.gantt-row` deliberately share one `grid-template-columns` (a fixed 220px label column, then one flexible track column)** — the same "header and every data row must agree on a pinned column width, or their own bar/marker/tick positions silently misalign" lesson this app has already learned more than once for its other grids (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above) — so the axis header's own month ticks and every row's own bar/marker positions, all computed as a plain left% against the identical date range, line up vertically no matter how many rows sit between them. The today-line is rendered as its own small absolutely-positioned element inside *every* row's own track (plus the axis track) rather than one continuous overlay spanning the whole chart — repeating a tiny div per row is simpler and more robust than trying to match a single global overlay's position against a variable-height, multi-section layout, and costs nothing meaningful. `.gantt-marker` sits at a higher `z-index` than `.gantt-bar` so a milestone marker sitting on top of its own item's bar (the common case) still reads as its own distinct diamond shape rather than being swallowed by the bar's flat color underneath it.

Verified live via CDP at each stage, including the final removal: a two-workstream, three-item chart correctly renders grouped sections, colored bars, and status-colored milestone diamonds aligned to a shared month axis with a visible today-line, with each row's label opening that item's own modal; after the granularity + min-width fixes, a real ~3.5-year, four-workstream-item chart correctly rendered spaced-out, non-overlapping quarterly ticks ("Q3 2025", "Q4 2025", ...) extending past the visible viewport, reachable via horizontal scroll rather than cramming into the window width; and, after removing the L1 Plans version, L1 Plans' own tab bar correctly shows only Plans/Dashboard again with no Gantt tab, while Dashboard's own Gantt tab is unaffected.

**`.gantt-axis-row` stays fixed at the top of the chart while scrolling vertically — an explicit user request ("in gant view, can you keep the timeline row fixed in case of vertical scrolling").** A tall chart (many workstreams/items) previously scrolled the month/quarter/year axis away with everything else, so a row far down the list had no visible reference for what its bar/marker positions actually meant. `.gantt-axis-row` is now `position: sticky; top: 0;` — since Dashboard's Gantt tab renders nothing but `.gantt-chart` into `#main`, and `#main` is the one real scrolling container every mode's content already scrolls within (see "Rendering pattern" above), sticky-to-`#main` is exactly "stays fixed while scrolling this view, scrolls away again once you've scrolled past the whole chart," the correct behavior with no extra JS.

**This needed `.gantt-chart`'s own `overflow: hidden` removed — the same "an ancestor's overflow:hidden breaks position:sticky" gotcha, not a bug in the sticky rule itself.** Sticky positioning is computed relative to the *nearest ancestor with a real scrolling mechanism* — and `overflow: hidden` counts as one for this purpose even when nothing inside it actually needs to scroll (which is always true for `.gantt-chart`, whose height is plain `auto`/content-sized). With it in place, `.gantt-axis-row` would stick to the top of `.gantt-chart` itself rather than the top of `#main`'s own viewport — since `.gantt-chart` never scrolls on its own, that's indistinguishable from not being sticky at all, and was confirmed live as the first, broken attempt (the axis row scrolled away with everything else, unchanged from before). Removing `overflow: hidden` fixed the sticky positioning but reopened the exact corner-clipping problem this app has already hit (and fixed) more than once for `.ws-section-header`/`.action-log-header` (see "Grid rows losing their background/border" and "Action Log" above): without a clipping parent, `.gantt-axis-row`'s own `--panel-2` background — square-cornered by default — would visibly poke out past `.gantt-chart`'s rounded top corners. Fixed the same way those were: `.gantt-axis-row` (always the first child) now carries its own `border-radius: var(--radius) var(--radius) 0 0` directly, and the last row of the last section carries the matching bottom pair — `.gantt-row` itself has no background of its own (it's transparent, showing `.gantt-chart`'s own background through), so the bottom corner was never actually at risk in practice, but the rule was added anyway for the same "don't rely on today's row shape staying background-less forever" defensiveness this codebase already applies elsewhere.

`z-index: 3` on `.gantt-axis-row` keeps it painting above the rows scrolling underneath it once stuck (comfortably above `.gantt-marker`'s `z-index: 1`/`.gantt-today-line`'s `z-index: 2`, both scoped to their own row's track and never actually contending for the same stacking position, but a clean margin regardless). Verified live via CDP: seeded 6 workstreams × 5 dated items (tall enough that `.main`'s `scrollHeight` genuinely exceeds its `clientHeight`), then read `.gantt-axis-row`'s own `getBoundingClientRect()` at four different `.main` `scrollTop` values (`0`/`300`/`600`/max) — its top position holds essentially constant (119px → 118px, a single sub-pixel rounding difference at the unscrolled starting position) rather than scrolling away with the rows, confirming the row is genuinely pinned, not just visually similar in a screenshot. This is a pure CSS/UI fix with no data-model or merge-behavior change, so `APP_VERSION` was not bumped, per this file's own established convention for UI-only changes.

**A follow-up user report — "with the fixed timeline, scope items now appear between fixed and upper navigation bar if horizontal scroll" — turned out to be a real, if narrow, gap left by the sticky fix above: `top: 0` isn't actually flush with `#main`'s own top once a row genuinely scrolls behind the sticky axis row.** `#main` sits inside `.planning-main` for every mode that shares it (Planning/Review/Dashboard — see "Modes" above), and `.planning-main .main` overrides `#main`'s own `padding-top` from the base rule's `20px` down to `16px`. Sticky's own inset (`top: 0`) resolves against the scrollport's *padding* edge, not its *border* edge — so with `top: 0`, the "stuck" axis row sits flush 16px below `#main`'s true top (its own border-box, which is also the boundary `overflow: auto` actually clips against), leaving a genuine 16px strip of `#main`'s own content area that's inside its clip boundary (nothing there gets clipped) but above where the sticky row's own opaque background starts (nothing there is covered either). A row scrolled far enough to normally be fully hidden behind the sticky header can have its own marker/bar land in exactly that uncovered strip and render visibly there.

**Reproducing this reliably took real diagonal-scroll wheel events, not a scripted `.scrollTop`/`.scrollLeft` assignment** — setting both properties directly (even from two separate calls) rarely showed the bug visually, even though `getBoundingClientRect()` sometimes still reported a row's marker geometrically inside the uncovered strip; only genuine `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` `mouseWheel` events (CDP's stand-in for a real trackpad/mouse scroll, producing the same non-round, momentum-like scroll deltas a real diagonal gesture would) reliably reproduced a visible leak — confirmed via a live screenshot showing a milestone diamond genuinely poking out above the "stuck" tick row, right in the gap between the toolbar and the axis row. This is also why the fix's own verification below is keyed off `getBoundingClientRect()` geometry (a reliable, deterministic signal) cross-checked against real screenshots at several of those geometrically-flagged scroll positions, rather than trusting either signal alone.

**Fixed with `top: -16px` in place of `top: 0`** — cancelling exactly the `16px` padding-top offset described above, so the stuck axis row sits flush with `#main`'s own true top instead of 16px below it. This has no effect on the *resting* (unscrolled) layout — a sticky element's own `top` only ever affects where it engages "stuck," never its normal-flow position — verified live: at `scrollTop: 0` the axis row still renders at its original ~119px offset (unaffected), while at every non-zero scroll position tested (a spread of `scrollTop`/`scrollLeft` combinations), `getBoundingClientRect()` now puts the axis row's own top pixel-exact with `#main`'s own top (previously off by the full 16px), and a live screenshot at each of the previously-flagged positions shows a clean axis row with nothing poking out above it. **This `-16px` must stay in sync with `.planning-main .main`'s own `padding-top: 16px` by hand if that value ever changes** — the CSS comment on `.gantt-axis-row` itself says so, the same "hand-tuned pixel math, tracked via a comment, not a shared variable" convention this app's other cross-referenced measurements already follow (see the several `min-width` values throughout this file). Pure CSS fix, no `APP_VERSION` bump, same reasoning as the sticky fix above.

**A follow-up message — "in gant view do not overlap timleine over the edge" — was first misread as still being about the vertical gap just above, and briefly got its own (wrong) fix: a second, invisible sticky element covering the same 16px strip so the visible axis row could revert to a comfortably-inset `top: 0` instead of sitting flush against `#main`'s edge.** That fix was fully built, verified live, and shipped — then immediately reverted (`git revert`, clean) once the user clarified what they'd actually meant: **"I meant on the right edge Q1 2028 is going out of bounds"** — a completely different, genuinely unaddressed bug on the opposite side of the chart. The vertical-gap fix directly above (`top: -16px`) was never in question and stayed exactly as shipped; only the reverted commit's own two files (`pulse.html`, this doc) needed undoing, cleanly, since nothing else had been built on top of it yet.

**The real bug: the last axis tick's own label has no neighbor to its right to grow into, unlike every other tick.** `.gantt-axis-tick` is left-anchored (`left: t.pct%`) with its label text flowing rightward via `padding-left`/normal flow — for every tick except the last, `GANTT_MIN_PX_PER_TICK`'s own 80px-per-tick spacing (see its own comment) guarantees the *next* tick's slot has room to absorb that overflow before anything visibly collides. The *last* tick has no next slot — its label can render straight past `.gantt-chart`'s own right border, with nothing left to clip it now that the border-radius fix earlier in this section removed `.gantt-chart`'s own `overflow: hidden` (needed for the vertical sticky fix above). Confirmed live: a real chart ending in "Q2 2028" rendered that label's own right edge ~40px past `.gantt-chart`'s own right border — visibly spilling out of the card, exactly matching "Q1 2028 is going out of bounds."

**Widening `ganttChartMinWidth()` to buy more trailing room was tried first and confirmed, live, not to work** — the gap between the last tick's own anchor and the chart's right edge comes from `ganttDateRange()`'s own percentage-based padding (`Math.max(3, round(span*.04))`, a few percent of the whole date range), which stays a small *percentage* of the total width regardless of how many pixels that width actually is — widening the chart by a real 80px (an entire extra tick's worth) moved the measured overflow by under 1px (39.4px vs ~40px unpadded), since only ~3-4% of that extra width ever lands inside the trailing gap. Reaching a meaningful pixel buffer this way would need widening the *entire* chart by roughly (desired-buffer ÷ padding-percentage) — thousands of pixels — to buy back a few dozen.

**Fixed by anchoring the last tick from the right instead of the left**, so its label grows *leftward* — into space every earlier tick's own slot already reserves — rather than rightward past the edge. `.gantt-axis-tick-last` (added only to the last tick in `ticks`, in `renderGanttChartHtml()`'s own `axisTicksHtml` builder) mirrors every left-side property the base rule sets to its right-side equivalent (`border-right` in place of `border-left`, `padding-right` in place of `padding-left`, `justify-content: flex-end`) and drops `left` in favor of `right: ${100 - t.pct}%` — the complementary percentage that puts the div's own right edge (now hosting the border, i.e. the actual tick-mark line) at the *exact same pixel position* `left: ${t.pct}%` would have put its left edge — the tick mark itself doesn't move, only which side of the box it anchors from, and which direction the label text is free to grow. Verified live, directly against the committed source: the last tick's own label ("Q2 2028") now renders fully inside `.gantt-chart`'s own right border with real margin to spare, and its own tick-mark line sits the expected ~80px (one tick's worth of spacing) from its neighbor's own mark, confirming the anchor swap didn't shift what date it actually represents. A chart with only a single tick is handled the same way — the loop's own `i === ticks.length - 1` check makes it both the first and last, so it still anchors from the right, tested directly (`tests/cases/dashboard.test.js`). Pure CSS + a small `renderGanttChartHtml()` change, no `APP_VERSION` bump.

## Text Size

**Ported from this author's mytasks app — an explicit user request ("build the font size feature, like in mytask app").** A plain CSS `zoom` on `<html>`, picked from a 4-way segmented control (Small/Medium/Large/Extra Large) in a topbar popover — mytasks itself has no CLAUDE.md documentation for this feature (it predates that file's last update there), so this was ported directly from its own source/comments rather than its docs.

`UI_SCALE_STEPS` (`[{pct:90,label:'Small',short:'S'}, {pct:100,...'M'}, {pct:115,...'L'}, {pct:130,...'XL'}]`) is the single source of truth for the 4 stops; `UI_SCALE_DEFAULT` is `100`. `uiScaleStepIndex(pct)` finds whichever stop is closest to a given value; `clampUiScale(v)` (non-finite input falls back to `UI_SCALE_DEFAULT`) snaps any value to the nearest real stop via that index — so `uiScale` is always exactly one of the 4 `pct` values, never something in between, regardless of whether it came from `localStorage`, a hand-edited import, or the control itself. `applyUiScale()` sets `document.documentElement.style.zoom = uiScale / 100` — called once at init (right after `applySidebarWidth()`, the identical "apply once at startup, no flash of the default" role) and again by `setUiScale(v)`. `zoom` is a Chromium/Safari-only CSS property (not Firefox) — the same "Chrome/Edge is the primary target" stance the File System Access sync feature already takes elsewhere in this app; on an unsupported browser this is simply a no-op, never a crash.

**Reuses `.view-tabs`/`.view-tab` outright for the 4 segments — the same pill Planning/Dashboard/Review's own mode switcher already uses — rather than inventing a second segmented-control component**, the identical "port the mechanism, adapt to this app's own existing components" treatment the Color scheme system and Help/About modals already got (see their own sections below/above). `.text-size-panel`/`.text-size-panel-header` wrap the control with a small header label (mirroring mytasks' own `.ui-scale-panel`); a scoped `.text-size-panel .view-tab` override narrows `.view-tab`'s own text-label padding down to a compact, centered square better suited to a single letter/pair. The popover itself (`#uiScaleMenu`) reuses `.dropdown-menu` outright (the same positioning/border/shadow the Data dropdown and Exec Summary export dropdown already share) rather than `.dropdown-item`'s own list-of-actions markup, since this is one segmented control, not several separate clickable rows. `renderUiScaleMenu()`/`toggleUiScaleMenu()`/`closeUiScaleMenu()` mirror `toggleDataMenu()`/`closeDataMenu()`'s own open/close/click-outside pattern exactly, including their own line in the shared document-level click-outside-closes listener. The topbar button itself (`fa-solid fa-text-height`, title "Text size") sits in the icon cluster right after the role button and before the palette/theme-toggle pair — a later, explicit user request repositioning it from its original spot (right after the theme toggle, before Help/About).

**A plain per-device UI preference, the identical treatment `sidebarWidth` already gets** — `uiScale` is folded into `load()`/`save()`'s `pulse-v1` blob (clamped on the way in, so a stale or hand-edited out-of-range value self-heals to its nearest real stop rather than being trusted as-is) but is *not* added to `buildIndexPayload()`/`recombineSyncData()`, which already explicitly enumerate their own fields — simply never adding it there is what keeps a personal zoom-level preference out of the shared synced folder, exactly like `sidebarWidth`/`mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme`/`filterWorkstreamId` before it. **This is a deliberate divergence from mytasks' own choice** — that app *does* sync its own `uiScale` through its linked-file payload; Pulse's own already-established stance (see `buildIndexPayload()`'s own comment above) is that a personal zoom-level preference has no more business syncing to a shared folder than `sidebarWidth` does, so the port adapted to Pulse's own convention here rather than carrying over mytasks' choice verbatim. No role gating anywhere in this, matching the theme toggle's and sidebar-resize's own unrestricted-at-every-role treatment. `seedDefaults()` doesn't need to reset `uiScale` any more than it resets `sidebarWidth`/`mode`/`theme`/`colorScheme` — the module-level `let uiScale = 100` initial value is already correct for a genuinely fresh install.

**Shipping this feature surfaced a real, user-reported topbar bug: at 115%/130%, the topbar's own row of buttons no longer fit, and — since `.topbar` had no overflow handling of any kind — the trailing icons (Help/About included) became genuinely unreachable, not just visually clipped.** `zoom` scales every topbar button's own rendered width without growing the viewport to match, so a row that fit comfortably at 100% can outright overflow at higher steps. This is the exact "row wider than its own container" class of bug this app has already fixed several times over for the status board's own rows (see `.ws-section never clips its own content` and the row-`min-width` fixes above) — `overflow-x: auto` on `.topbar` is the identical fix, just scoped to the topbar this time. `.topbar > * { flex-shrink: 0; }` is what makes that scroll actually engage rather than every button/dropdown silently squishing its own icon+label first — a plain flex row's children default to `flex-shrink: 1`, which would otherwise absorb the overflow by cramming each control down toward its own min-content width instead of leaving every control at full size and letting the row scroll past it. `.topbar-spacer`'s own separate, more specific `flex: 1` rule (which is what pushes the icon cluster to the right in the first place) still wins over this wildcard on specificity — its own `flex-shrink: 1` (part of the `flex: 1` shorthand) is exactly what should happen to an empty spacer once there's no room left to grow it. Verified live: at 130% zoom on a 1440px-wide window, `.topbar`'s `scrollWidth` genuinely exceeds its `clientWidth`; scrolling the topbar to its own max `scrollLeft` brings every icon (About included) into view at full, un-squished size, and it's fully clickable there.

**That fix immediately broke the two dropdown menus hanging directly off the topbar (Data, Text Size) — a user-reported follow-up: the Text Size popover became invisible at higher zoom, "hidden behind the horizontal scrolling bar."** Root cause: setting `overflow-x: auto` on an element forces `overflow-y` to compute as `auto` too — a plain CSS rule (this app has hit the identical mechanic before, for the status board's own rows), not specific to `.topbar` — and unlike those earlier cases, `.topbar` has a genuinely *fixed* 54px height with children (these two dropdown menus, `position: absolute`) that deliberately extend below it; the forced `overflow-y: auto` now clips any descendant that visually extends outside `.topbar`'s own box, regardless of which element it's actually anchored to. Dashboard's own Export Exec Summary dropdown was never affected — it lives outside `.topbar` entirely, in a plain, non-clipping toolbar.

Fixed by switching just these two dropdowns to `position: fixed`, with coordinates computed by a new shared helper, `positionFixedDropdown(wrapperId, menuId)`, from the trigger wrapper's own real `getBoundingClientRect()` at the moment it's opened (`toggleDataMenu()`/`toggleUiScaleMenu()` both call it right before toggling `.open`) — `position: fixed` is anchored to the viewport, not any scrolling ancestor, so `.topbar`'s own clipping no longer applies to it at all. Sets `right` (`window.innerWidth - rect.right`), not `left` — matching `.dropdown-menu`'s own existing right-aligned CSS — and explicitly `left: auto`, since a `position: fixed` box with both `left` and an inherited `right: 0` from the CSS rule would otherwise have its width computed by the browser as the distance between the two, stretching/shrinking the popover unpredictably instead of sizing to its own content. This is a one-time snapshot, not a live-tracked position — scrolling `.topbar` after opening a popover doesn't drag the popover along with its trigger (an accepted, minor tradeoff; the existing click-outside-closes listener still closes it normally). `getBoundingClientRect()` isn't defined on the JXA test harness's fake DOM elements (the same gap `endSidebarResize()`'s own `getComputedStyle()` call already has) — `positionFixedDropdown()` guards on `typeof wrapper.getBoundingClientRect !== 'function'` and no-ops there, verified live in a real browser instead: reproduced the exact reported bug (the popover computing an off-screen position while its trigger sat scrolled out of view), then confirmed that scrolling the trigger into view first and reopening the popover renders it fully visible and correctly anchored, at both 130% zoom and the plain 100% default.

**A third, separate zoom side effect, also user-reported: the app didn't scroll vertically at all at 115%/130%, even when content genuinely overflowed.** Root cause: `body { height: 100vh; overflow: hidden; }` relies entirely on `.main`'s own `overflow: auto` for scrolling — `body` itself was never meant to scroll, since it's always assumed to fit exactly one viewport-tall box. `zoom` (applied to `<html>`) breaks that assumption: it inflates `body`'s own *rendered* height beyond the true viewport by exactly the zoom factor, even though `100vh` still resolves against the real, un-zoomed viewport height as its own used value first — verified live: at 130% zoom in a 900px-tall window, `body`'s rendered height read `1170px` (`900 × 1.3`), a genuine 270px past the physical window. `.main`'s own `overflow: auto` can only ever scroll *within* `body`'s own box — it has no way to reveal the part of the layout spilling past `body`'s own inflated height. This was genuinely unreachable, not just inconvenient: `body`'s `overflow: hidden` — via the CSS spec's long-standing html/body-to-viewport overflow propagation quirk — is what disabled page-level (root/viewport) scrolling entirely, the one mechanism that could otherwise have reached it. Fixed by splitting `body`'s overflow: `overflow-x: hidden` (unchanged in spirit — the topbar already handles its own horizontal overflow internally, see above, so the page itself never needs to scroll sideways) and `overflow-y: auto` (the fix) — the same "always leave a working escape-hatch scrollbar, never let overflow become truly unreachable" stance this app already takes everywhere else, just one more axis/layer up the same stack as the topbar fix just above. `.main`'s own internal scroll stays the everyday mechanism for ordinary content overflow (unaffected, still primary) — this new page-level fallback exists specifically for the zoom-inflation case, and rarely engages otherwise. Verified live: after the fix, scrolling the page itself (not `.main`) reaches exactly the 270px of previously-unreachable content from the same 130%-zoom repro above.

## Color scheme system

Ported wholesale from this author's mytasks app (see its own CLAUDE.md's "Color scheme system") — same two-axis design, same five schemes, same picker mechanics, adapted to Pulse's own CSS variable names and category-color model rather than mytasks'.

Two independent axes control appearance: `data-theme` (`light`/`dark`, the original sun/moon button, `toggleTheme()`, unchanged since before schemes existed) and `data-scheme` (picked from the palette-icon button's modal, `setColorScheme()`). Each scheme defines its own light *and* dark CSS variable block, so the two axes combine freely — e.g. `[data-scheme="dracula"][data-theme="dark"]`.

- **`THEME_SCHEMES`** (top of the `<script>` block) is the single source of truth for which schemes exist — each entry has `id`, `name`, `desc`, and `swatches` (three hex values shown as dots in the picker). `id` must exactly match a `[data-scheme="..."]` selector; nothing enforces this automatically except `tests/cases/theme.test.js`'s "every THEME_SCHEMES entry has a distinct, expected id" test, which will fail loudly on a typo but won't catch a selector that exists with no matching `THEME_SCHEMES` entry (a harmless orphan, not a crash).
- **`:root`** and the bare **`html[data-theme="dark"]`** block are the original, untouched palette — this is what renders when `data-scheme` is absent entirely (or explicitly `"standard"`, since `:root` already covers that case with no separate `html[data-scheme="standard"]` block needed), so a load with no scheme saved yet looks exactly as this app always has.
- Every other scheme is `html[data-scheme="x"] { ... }` (light) and `html[data-scheme="x"][data-theme="dark"] { ... }` (dark) — the dark block's extra attribute selector always wins on specificity over the bare `html[data-theme="dark"]`, so block order in the file doesn't matter for correctness. A scheme only redeclares the tokens that actually vary by hue (`--bg`/`--panel`/`--panel-2`/`--border`/`--text`/`--text-dim`/`--text-faint`/`--accent`/`--accent-bg`/`--danger`, every `--ws-*`/`--ws-*-bg` pair, every `--stat-*`/`--stat-*-bg` pair) — structural tokens (`--radius`, `--shadow`, `--item-grid-cols`) are deliberately left undeclared per scheme and keep cascading from the blocks above, since they aren't part of "color scheme".
- **Token mapping from mytasks' 6-slot system to Pulse's own**: mytasks only needed `blue`/`teal`/`amber`/`red`/`green`/`purple` (its category-chip + semantic colors); Pulse's `WS_COLORS` needs 8 (adds `pink`/`grey`) and its `STATUSES` needs a `not-started`/`complete` pair on top of green/amber/red. `--accent`/`--stat-complete` both reuse the scheme's "blue" hue (mirroring Standard's own existing convention, where `--accent` and `--stat-complete` are already two near-identical blues), `--ws-grey`/`--stat-not-started` derive from the scheme's own secondary/tertiary text tiers (`text-dim`/`text-faint`) rather than a separate invented neutral, and `--ws-pink` is either a real extra token where the source product actually has one (GitHub's own Primer data-visualization pink, fetched from primer.style — see below) or an honestly-labeled derivation (an even RGB blend of that scheme's own red+purple) where it doesn't.
- **Accuracy convention** (same as mytasks): when a scheme claims to replicate a real product's theme, its core anchor colors are the same values mytasks already verified via fetch (VS Code Light/Dark Modern, Dracula/Alucard's official spec, GitHub Primer's semantic palette, GitHub Dark Dimmed's real compiled values) — not reconstructed from memory a second time. The one value fetched fresh for this port specifically was GitHub Primer's real data-visualization pink token (`--data-pink-color-emphasis: #ce2c85`, `--data-pink-color-muted: #ffe5f1`, from primer.style/primitives/colors/) for `--ws-pink`, since mytasks never needed a separate pink slot; that token's dark-mode pair isn't published on the same page, so GitHub/GitHub Dimmed's dark `--ws-pink` is an extrapolation following the same light→dark lightening Primer's other real tokens show (e.g. red `#cf222e` → `#f85149`), flagged in the CSS comment as an extrapolation rather than a second verified value.
- Each scheme may add its own one-off "signature" rule, kept in its own block separate from the variable definitions (same convention as mytasks): Modern/Dracula/GitHub/GitHub Dimmed all set numerics (`.milestone-count-badge`, `.rag-pill`, `.review-progress`) to a monospace stack; Dracula adds a soft accent-colored glow to the brand icon; GitHub and GitHub Dimmed turn `.btn-primary` into GitHub's solid green.
- Persistence mirrors how `theme` already works: `colorScheme` (state, defaults `'standard'`) folded into `load()`/`save()`'s `localStorage` payload, same as `theme` — but, like `mode`/`theme`/`filterWorkstreamId`, it's deliberately **not** part of what gets written to a linked sync file at all (see `buildIndexPayload()`'s own comment under "File System Access API sync" above) — these are per-device display preferences, and since nothing on the read side ever consumed them from an incoming file anyway (`mergeData()` never did, and `replaceFromFileData()`'s own fresh-device path never did either), writing them bought no actual round-tripping — just a real file rewrite on every mode/workstream/theme change for a value nothing downstream ever applied.

To add a scheme: add its light+dark CSS variable blocks, add any signature rules, add a `THEME_SCHEMES` entry, and add its `id` to the sorted list in `tests/cases/theme.test.js`'s last test. To remove one: reverse all of the above and grep the whole file for its id/name to catch stray comments.

**A real bug this port introduced and fixed**: the "Color scheme system" comment block above originally described `THEME_SCHEMES` as living "in the `<script>` block" — a literal `<script>` substring sitting inside a CSS comment. `tests/harness.js` (and any other tool) that extracts the inline script via a naive `indexOf('<script>')`/`indexOf('</script>')` scan latches onto the *first* occurrence of that literal text anywhere in the file, including inside a comment — so this accidentally moved the extraction's start point up into the middle of the `<style>` block, turning a few thousand characters of CSS into "JavaScript" and breaking the entire test suite with an opaque parse error. Fixed by rewording the comment to avoid the literal substring ("the inline script block below"). **Never write the literal text `<script>` or `</script>` anywhere in this file outside the real tags themselves** — not in a CSS comment, not in a JS comment, not in a string — since `tests/harness.js`'s extraction (and this exact failure mode) has no other safeguard against it.

## Help & About modals

**Ported wholesale from this author's mytasks app — an explicit user request ("add help and info like in mytask app, same design")** — the same "port the mechanism, adapt the variable names and content" treatment the color scheme system above already got. Two plain, non-role-gated, non-mutating modals (`helpModalBg`/`aboutModalBg`), reachable from two new topbar `.icon-btn`s (`fa-regular fa-circle-question` / `fa-solid fa-circle-info`) at the very end of the topbar, after the theme toggle — mirroring mytasks' own trailing Help/About pair exactly, just using Pulse's own `.icon-btn` class rather than mytasks' `.btn.btn-icon` (Pulse's topbar already has its own established icon-button convention for this exact row — role/palette/theme-toggle — so the ported buttons match their neighbors, not their mytasks source). `openHelpModal()`/`closeHelpModal()`/`openAboutModal()`/`closeAboutModal()` are the same plain open/close pair every other simple modal here has (see "Modals" above) — wired into the shared Escape-key handler and the per-modal backdrop-click listener list the same way every other modal is, unlike mytasks' own single `closeAllModals()` (Pulse has no such generic helper; each modal owns its own close function).

**Content is written for Pulse specifically, not translated line-by-line from mytasks' own task-manager copy** — mytasks' Help modal describes tasks/subtasks/board-view/keyboard shortcuts, none of which exist in Pulse. `helpModalBg`'s `.help-body` covers, in order: **Overview** (the four modes), **Planning** (workstreams/scope items/categories seeding the checklist, drag-reorder, the Unassigned/quick-add flow, search+filter pills), **Review** (starting a cycle, milestone-level confirmation, meeting minutes auto-fill, the Action/Decision/Change Logs), **Dashboard** (the rollup + Overdue/Upcoming feeds + Exec Summary export), **Roles** (the soft ladder, not real security), and **Data & sync** (localStorage, file linking, Export/Import/Backups), closing with the same "nothing ever leaves your machine" `.help-tip` mytasks' own Data section ends on. **Deliberately no "Keyboard shortcuts" section** — mytasks has one because it has real, documented keyboard navigation; Pulse doesn't, and fabricating one here would be actively misleading rather than just an omission. `aboutModalBg` mirrors mytasks' own About modal shape exactly (app icon + name + one-line tagline, a divider, "Created by Peer Rexroth", a feedback `mailto:` link) with Pulse's own brand icon (`fa-wave-square`, matching the topbar brand mark) and name in place of mytasks', and the author's current contact address rather than mytasks' own (a different, work-scoped email — the two apps' About modals were never meant to share one contact address).

**CSS**: `.help-modal`/`.help-modal-header`/`.help-body`/`.help-section`/`.help-section-title`/`.help-row`/`.help-divider`/`.help-tip` are ported 1:1 from mytasks, with mytasks' `--bg`/`--bg-2`/`--text-2`/`--text-3` variable names swapped for Pulse's own `--panel`/`--panel-2`/`--text-dim`/`--text-faint` equivalents (`--text`/`--border`/`--radius` are named identically in both apps, so those carried over unchanged). mytasks' `.help-badge`/`.help-badge-blue`/`-teal`/`-amber` variants weren't ported — nothing in Pulse's own Help content actually uses a colored inline badge the way mytasks' priority terminology does, so there was nothing to adapt them for; add them later if a future Help row genuinely needs one; and mytasks' `var(--mono)` (used once, for the linked-file `.json` extension) has no equivalent variable in Pulse at all, so that one spot inlines the same monospace font stack directly instead (`ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, Consolas, monospace` — the exact stack already used elsewhere in this file for numeric columns in the Modern/Dracula/GitHub color schemes, see "Color scheme system" above).

**Modals fade in/out now, across the whole app — a related, explicit user follow-up ("do modals fade in like in the mytask app?") surfaced while porting the above, and answered by porting mytasks' own `.modal-bg` transition mechanism too, not just its Help/About content.** Every modal in Pulse shares the one `.modal-bg` class (see "Modals" above), so this is a single, global CSS change rather than something scoped to just the two new modals: `.modal-bg` used to toggle via a plain `display: none` ↔ `flex` swap (instant, no transition possible on a `display` change) — now it's always `display: flex`, gated instead by `opacity`/`visibility`/`pointer-events`, with `transition: opacity .15s ease, visibility .15s ease` doing the actual fade, exactly mirroring mytasks' own mechanism. `visibility: hidden` (not just `opacity: 0`) is what keeps a closed modal genuinely non-interactive and out of the tab order for the whole transition, rather than leaning on `pointer-events: none` alone. A `backdrop-filter: blur(3px)` (`-webkit-backdrop-filter` alongside it for Safari, though Safari can't install this app as a PWA anyway per "Running it" above — the blur itself still renders fine in a plain Safari tab) was added to the same rule too, matching mytasks' own backdrop treatment, layered on top of Pulse's existing `rgba(10,12,16,.4)` overlay color rather than adopting mytasks' own lighter one — the overlay color itself is untouched, already correct for both of Pulse's themes with no per-theme override needed. `#wsModalBg`'s own `z-index: 60` override (the one deliberately-stacked-modal-on-top-of-a-modal case, see "Modals" above) is unaffected — it's a separate, additive selector, not something this change touches. No JS changes were needed anywhere: every modal already opens/closes via `classList.add/remove('open')`, exactly the mechanism `.modal-bg.open`'s new opacity/visibility values key off; nothing in this app (or its test suite, which asserts on `classList.contains('open')`, never `.style.display`) ever read the old `display` value directly.

## Conventions from this project's history

- Font Awesome 6.5 (`fa-solid`) via CDN, matching PromptLab/mytasks.
- `button:focus:not(:focus-visible) { outline: none; }` is global — focus rings only show for real keyboard navigation, not post-click.
- IDs are generated by `genId()` (timestamp + random suffix); `isSafeId()` plus `normalizeData()`'s regeneration passes are the guard against a crafted id from an external `.json` file (import, linked file) breaking out of an `onclick` attribute — see "Data integrity" above.
- **`.btn-primary:hover` must always re-declare `background`, not just `filter`** — a user-reported bug, confirmed on video (hovering a modal's "Done" button made its white text unreadable, background gone pale, only the blue border still visible). Root cause: `.btn:hover { background: var(--panel-2) }` and `.btn-primary:hover` share identical specificity (one class + `:hover` each), but `.btn-primary:hover` originally only set `filter: brightness(1.06)` — leaving `background` uncontested, so `.btn:hover`'s pale background won by default over `.btn-primary`'s own solid accent fill, with `.btn-primary`'s `color: #fff` now sitting on top of it unreadable. This is a plain CSS specificity collision, unrelated to the separate first-frame paint-glitch fix below (that one's a compositor timing issue that self-corrects on any repaint, including a hover — this one is deterministic and hover *causes* it). Fixed by adding `background: var(--accent)` to `.btn-primary:hover` itself, same specificity as `.btn:hover` but later in source order, so it wins the tie-break back. The GitHub/GitHub Dimmed scheme's own `.btn-primary` override (`html[data-scheme="github"] .btn-primary`, one type selector higher in specificity) was never affected by this bug in the first place — verify any future `.btn-primary`-adjacent CSS change against this exact specificity arithmetic, since it's easy to reintroduce by adding a hover rule that "looks like" it should just tweak `filter`.
